## Minister’s Foreword

### Minister’s Foreword

*Pages 6–6*

Minister’s Foreword

An efficient transport system plays a critical role in economic growth, environmental sustainability, and overall quality of life. Our Programme for Government (PfG) commits to implementing actions that optimise mobility, reduce congestion, and ensure that transportation works seamlessly for everyone. This Strategy sets out a clear vision and outlines the key steps that we will need to take together to deliver on those commitments.

The Strategy represents a critical component of the decarbonising pathway for transport, however its benefits are much wider than climate.

Moving Together is about supporting the development of better and more liveable towns and cities, cleaner air, efficient public transport, safer spaces for walking and cycling and an end to the inefficiency and pollution of car-clogged streets.

It aligns closely with the extensive range of Government investment and supports already in place or planned for public transport, walking, cycling, and electric vehicles, and it focuses on gradual, and meaningful shifts in travel behaviour as people’s travel options are expanding.

Like many countries over the past four decades, Ireland embraced motorised transport in line with a growing economy. At the end of 2025, there were 3.2 million licensed vehicles in Ireland – an increase of over 2 million when compared with 1985. Of that increase, 1.8 million were private cars. Alongside this was a misalignment between population growth, economic activity, land-use patterns, and infrastructure capacity, which has had consequential impacts across all modes. While the growing presence of vehicles in our shared environment is placing immense pressure on our natural spaces, the effect on well-being is also widely accepted. Long hours spent in traffic leads to a loss of valuable time with families and friends, but it also leads to reduced productivity and cost impacts for business. Congestion also disadvantages those relying on public transport and creates further obstacles for walking and cycling.

The current Programme for Government is ambitious for transport, and for delivering transformative projects. The recent NDP Review has allocated €24bn to the transport sector over the next 5 years. However, the success of planned initiatives cannot be fully realised while current levels of congestion remain. Heavy traffic makes public transport less reliable, often discouraging people from using it, and makes the environment for vulnerable road users, such as pedestrians and cyclists, less safe, again, too often, discouraging people from using active travel, particularly for shorter journeys.

To address these issues, this new Strategy seeks to prioritise the needs of people and align those needs with best practice in urban and rural transport planning. It also looks to support the freight and haulage industry, given the importance of efficiency to this sector. The Strategy is a framework for working collaboratively - and ‘moving’ together – across government, the economy and communities – to co-create the solutions needed for a more balanced, and equitable transport system.

The Strategy is high level in nature and touches on a vast array of Government policies and programmes with a suite of 32 recommendations. It also provides for the preparation of guidance to local authorities and local council representatives to develop plans for their own areas that suit their communities’ needs best.

The Strategy has the potential to unlock transformative change in how we move around our country, our towns and our cities. Its success will be measured against its objectives and will be monitored and evaluated from a ‘wellbeing’ and Just Transition perspective.

We recognise that for many people, the car may be the only viable transport choice - particularly for those who are living in rural or isolated communities, or for those with mobility needs. Equally, all sectors of the economy and society rely heavily on the delivery of goods and services by road. The Strategy’s aim is to make the system better for all road users, private and commercial, and to explore innovative solutions for making travel by any means more efficient, sustainable, and pleasant for everyone.

Darragh O’Brien Minister for Transport

## Executive Summary

### Executive Summary

*Pages 13–15*

Executive Summary

Moving Together – ‘A Collaborative Approach to Systems Change in Transport’ has been developed to make our transport system more efficient for everyone and to help alleviate the impacts of congestion on the economy, the environment and the health of our society.

In line with commitments in the Programme for Government 2025, this Strategy is a call for collective action across Government and society not only to reduce carbon emissions from transport over the medium to long term but to help alleviate the more immediate issues of congestion, road collisions and air pollution.

but the personal costs of congestion are just as concerning. As well as the air quality implications of congestion – for vehicle occupants and those in close proximity - and the negative health impacts due to stress and inactivity, time spent in traffic can also mean a displacement of time away from family and community life.

In the revised NDP, the Government has committed to investing in a diverse range of transport options— public transport, active travel, roads, maritime, and aviation—to enhance connectivity across urban and rural areas, giving people more choice and supporting sustainable growth.

Compounded by a historical misalignment between population growth, economic activity, land-use patterns, and infrastructure capacity, Ireland has become ever more reliant on private transport in recent decades. While our carbased system has served us reasonably well up to now, it is largely accepted that change is needed especially by those who routinely spend large parts of their life negotiating heavy traffic. In previous decades, space across the country was systematically reallocated to cars - through urban planning and as part of large-scale road building programmes. This was done to keep pace with a swell in vehicle numbers on our roads. For example, the number of licensed vehicles in Ireland increased by 2.3 million from 1985 to 2025. While goods vehicles increased by 465% over this period, private cars accounted for most of the growth, with an increase of 1.8 million vehicles2.

The Strategy will unlock the full potential of this investment in sustainable mobility options by prioritising these modes. Essentially, it is about putting people, and their needs, at the centre of Ireland’s future transport system.

While the impetus for this document arises from a commitment in the Climate Action Plan, and will support the Government’s pledge to take sustained action to tackle the climate crisis and to decarbonise the economy, the Strategy is also seeking to reinforce a shift to more sustainable modes of transport for better health, safety and wellbeing outcomes through network optimisation and associated reductions in congestion, air and noise pollution as above.

Good infrastructure is vital, and high-quality roads will continue to be the life-blood of the transport network, but there are physical, environmental and societal trade-offs in supporting an everincreasing car-dependent system – with limits on the level of additional capacity that can be built. For this reason, a more efficient use of the existing system is needed – one that reduces the need for travel in the first instance by delivering significant system change, and by releasing the full potential of the Government’s investment – current and future - in active, shared and public transport infrastructure and services.

The economic cost of congestion in the Greater Dublin Area was estimated to be €336m in 2022, rising to circa €1.5bn by 20401. An additional analysis of the regional cities - Cork, Galway, and Waterford - shows that congestion costs are projected to rise substantially in these cities over the same period. Without further intervention, the per capita economic impact could double or even triple in certain locations. Clearly, time lost from congestion has significant economic costs,

1 https://assets.gov.ie/static/documents/the-economic-cost-of-congestion-in-the-greater-dublin-area-2022-2040.pdf

2 https://data.cso.ie/table/EIIEEA25

In relation to climate targets, the 2022 Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) report ‘Redesigning Ireland’s Transport System for Net Zero’3 advised that Ireland is unlikely to achieve its ambitious climate goals without a systemic change in its transport system and substantially different patterns of travel behaviours than those observed today. By way of demonstration, the recent National Household Travel Survey 2024 (NHTS) reported that over 7 in 10 (71%) journeys in Ireland are made by car.

particular, the Strategy notes the potential that new rail services and extra capacity within our existing services can provide and notes the need for ongoing investment in rail in order to deliver a fully efficient public transport system. The Strategy further acknowledges the need to continue supporting electrification and improving the charging infrastructure for electric vehicles across Ireland, in particular noting that people in rural areas are more dependent on cars and, on average, use cars more than in urban areas. This will continue to be the case for many, and the Strategy reflects this reality.

The OECD called on the need for policies with a high transformative potential such as road space reallocation, the mainstreaming of on-demand shared services and greater communication efforts to address car-centric mindsets.

It is intended that this Strategy will provide the guidance needed for public bodies, including local authorities and local council representatives, to develop bespoke plans for their own areas – plans that are co-created with local communities.

The development of ‘Moving Together’ is a policy response to the recommendations of the OECD, and will provide an over-arching direction for achieving fair and equitable efficiencies in our transport system, guided by four key principles Just Transition; Accessibility and Inclusion; Rural Recognition; and Economic and Financial Sustainability.

Reflecting the system-wide changes required to optimise the use of the transport network across all modes, this Strategy has been guided by substantial research, notably the work of the Five Cities Demand Management Study, insights from the 2022 OECD Report and the International Transport Forum4. Engagement has been central to its development also, involving stakeholders from government departments and agencies, local authorities, industry, academia, and NGOs. Key areas that were considered as part of the Strategy were Integrated Land Use and Transport Planning; Optimal Use of Space; Fiscal Measures; Generators of Demand (Movement of People); Generators of Demand (Movement of Goods) and Captive Car Users. The outcome from this engagement is reflected in Chapter 9 of the Strategy and the collective suite of recommendations, are represented in Chapter 10.

This Strategy is not about eliminating the car as a mode of transport or curtailing travel and personal freedoms. It is about finding new approaches to making travel, by whatever means, more efficient and pleasant for everyone.

In this way, the Strategy seeks to:

ᴏ identify and leverage the potential of existing Government policies and programmes to impact travel demand;

ᴏ identify and address any impediments to policy measures that reduce travel demand and/or increase transport system efficiencies; and

The Strategy does not, however, contain a prescriptive set of top-down actions. This type of approach will not work. It is a strategic framework intended to provide the high-level direction required at a national level to achieve the objectives identified, referencing a wide range of options that can be selected at national, regional and local level, and supporting, guiding and enabling the appropriate authorities and entities, both in the public and private domain, to take ownership and shape their own actions in a coherent, evidence-based manner.

ᴏ enable action at national, local and community level that will help deliver the objectives of the Strategy in a coherent and collaborative manner.

It also recognises the importance of an efficient, comfortable and affordable public transport system, and emphasises the need to significantly improve the availability and reliable frequency of public transport in rural and urban areas. In

3 https://www.oecd.org/en/publications/redesigning-ireland-s-transport-for-net-zero_b798a4c1-en.html

4 https://www.itf-oecd.org/tcad

The 32 recommendations in Chapter 10 are the product of a constructive, collaborative and concerted effort across a broad range of Government departments, members of the National Steering Group, the expert subgroups members of the public and a wide range of representative and civil society bodies to identify the type of interventions that can cumulatively make a lasting and positive impact on behaviour.

This Strategy provides a framework for delivering the systemic change recommended by the OECD by signaling the need for that change, identifying all the policies and programmes that can contribute to that change and enabling those actors across the system, whether national, local, civil society or business, to collaborate and move together towards the vision of a future transport system that sustainably connects people and places, in a way that is efficient, accessible, safe, and healthy for all. It is vital to the credibility of the Strategy that its implementation is demonstrably fair and equitable. This has been the approach taken to developing the Strategy and it will be the approach taken to its implementation.

## Introduction

### Introduction

*Pages 16–16*

## 1. Introduction

### Ambition

*Pages 17–17*

Ambition

For the transport sector, this translates to a cut of 50% in transport emissions (relative to 2018 levels) or 6 Mt CO2 equivalent by 2030. To achieve this target, Government is prioritising a large-scale transition to Electric Vehicles (EVs) through generous and targeted incentives, higher penetration levels of biofuels in the fuel mix through regulation and increased modal shift towards more sustainable journeys through unprecedented investment and improvements in active and public transport infrastructure and services. Effectively, these represent ‘supply’ side measures – supplying more sustainable alternatives such as walking, cycling and public transport as well as the use of EVs.

The Programme for Government (PfG) 2025 commits to investing in a diverse range of transport options, with the aim of enhancing connectivity across urban and rural areas, giving people more choice and supporting sustainable growth which will also address congestion, improve air quality, reduce noise, and enhance the attractiveness and accessibility of cities, neighbourhoods and town centres. The new PfG also commits to identifying, researching and coordinating delivery of behavioural change initiatives to support modal shift and reduce congestion and improve travel options, focusing on wider societal benefits of the change. Aligned to this ambition are the Government’s climate goals, which were most recently restated through the Climate Action Plan 2025.

However, supply side measures alone will not deliver the emissions abatement required for the transport sector as they do not address the projected increases in travel demand over the intervening years, linked to population and economic growth. This means that there is a gapto-target and more needs to be done. To fully address the environmental, economic and social costs of transport inefficiency and congestion, a balance must be struck between supply and demand-led measures. While the latter will be vital to bridging the ’gap-to-target’ and to addressing those wider social and economic issues, it is acknowledged that the effectiveness of such measures will remain largely dependent on the availability of supply-side alternatives.

However, it is the Climate Action and Low-Carbon Development (Amendment) Act 2021 which sets out Ireland’s legally binding emissions reduction targets, along with a carbon budget programme and sectoral ceilings that are consistent with achieving a 51% reduction in emissions by 2030 and a net-zero position by 2050. The new Government has reaffirmed its commitment to achieving the targets set in the Climate Action Plan.

![Figure 1: Gap-to-Target](assets/p017-f01.webp)

*Figure 1: Gap-to-Target*

### Positive Trends

*Pages 18–18*

In view of the sectoral emissions ceilings in place, a significant programme of work was undertaken to refresh, revalidate and recalibrate the decarbonisation pathway for transport. This was set out in CAP 2023 which identified that an additional basket of measures would be needed to bridge the above ‘gap’, estimated to be 2. MtCO2 equivalent (see Figure 1 Gap-To-Target).

The reduction in transport emissions from 2023 to 2024 also suggests that emissions from the transport sector have peaked and demonstrates the positive impact of demand management and decarbonisation measures introduced in recent years with increases in the use of biofuels and electric vehicles being major factors behind the emissions decrease.

A similar programme of work is underway again, given the latest Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) projections for transport. The Corrective Action Modelling Project, signalled in the Climate Action Plan 25 (CAP25), will consider all potential measures and identify what is feasible over the remaining period, taking account of just transition principles, in terms of bridging the gap to target.

That said, in relation to Carbon Budget Period 1, the transport sector has emitted 46.28 MtCO2eq. (86%) in the first four years (2021-2024). It is probable that 2025 will be broadly on par with 2024 (11.65 MtCO2eq), and therefore any overshoot beyond ~7.7 MtCO2eq. 2025 would be deducted from the allowable ceiling for 2026-2030 of 37MtCO2eq. It is therefore not unreasonable to expect that the 2026-2030 budget may be reduced to 33MtCO2eq., or 6.6 MtCO2eq. per annum.

This Strategy is intended to provide an overarching framework for the delivery of a range of potential behavioural change measures that can be deployed to manage transport demand and to bridge the above ‘gap’ in a fair and equitable manner. It will play a key role in contributing to specific CAP targets such as achieving a reduction of 20% in total vehicle kilometres by 2030.

In line with the pledge in the Programme for Government to take sustained action to tackle the climate crisis and to decarbonise the economy, there remains an urgent need, therefore, to continue to tackle emissions and to avoid jeopardising the sector’s capacity to achieve its targets in future years. Further and sustained decoupling of the direct correlation between transport emissions and wider social and economic activity thus forms the fundamental challenge for the sector.

Emissions Trajectory

The achievement of the 50% reduction in transport emissions must be consistent with the Government’s carbon reduction programme, which includes a sectoral emissions ceiling of 54 MtCO2eq. for the first carbon budget period (2021-2025), and a further reduced sectoral emissions ceiling of 37 MtCO2eq. over the second carbon budget period (2026-2030).

Positive Trends

Positive changes are already happening in terms of travel behaviour. Passenger numbers across Public Service Obligation (PSO) services reached an all-time high in 2025 with almost 53 million additional journeys taken when compared to 2023. This is the third consecutive year of record-breaking figures, with patronage rates now exceeding pre-2019 levels.

Based on the latest EPA inventory (1990 – 2024), in total, the domestic transport sector emitted 11.65MtCO2eq. in 2024, compared to 11.79MtCO2eq. in 2023 – a reduction of 1.2%. Transport emissions are now 5.4% below pre- COVID levels and 2024 marked the first post- COVID emissions decrease in the transport sector.

As well as being unique internationally, this trend provides evidence that people respond to policies that are well considered and responsive to their needs. For example, under the National Sustainable Mobility Policy, the Connecting Ireland Rural Mobility Programme delivered new rural bus services in towns and villages at a rate of approximately one new service every week in 2022, with over 140,000 weekly vehicle kilometres added to the public transport network

This was achieved against a backdrop of economic growth (as expressed by modified final domestic demand) of 2.7% during 2024 and population growth of 1.9% to April 2024, thus indicating an absolute decoupling of transport emissions from economic growth.

### Purpose of Strategy

*Pages 19–20*

Purpose of Strategy

through new services, frequency enhancements and route extensions.

Accordingly, the purpose of this Strategy is to encourage changes in travel behaviour with a view to reducing, where possible, unsustainable journeys and overall travel demand. However, it is not about eliminating the car as a mode of transport or curtailing people’s freedom to move around. Rather, it is about finding new approaches to making travel, by whatever means, more efficient and pleasant for everyone. The Strategy recognises that the car will remain the dominant mode of transport for many people for a variety of reasons including the lack of readily available sustainable alternatives for many necessary journeys.

To give a better sense of the rate of uptake, 5.5 million passenger journeys were carried on services launched or enhanced under Connecting Ireland in 2023, a 90% increase on 2019 (65% on 2022). In 2025, TFI Local Link services recorded almost 7 million passenger journeys, a 19% increase compared with 2024.

In 2022, to encourage and facilitate greater uptake of public transport services, a 20% average fare reduction on PSO services was introduced – the first such reduction in 75 years. Young adults aged 19 to 25 and students also benefitted from a further 50% reduction in fares with the introduction of the Young Adult and Student Card (YASC) Fares Scheme that same year on PSO and participating commercial services. Such fare reductions have remained in place, or have been extended in some cases e.g. free travel for children under 5 years has become available to those aged 8 and under from September 2025.

The approach set out in the Strategy is threefold - 1) to identify and leverage the potential of existing Government policies and programmes to impact travel demand (by optimising their positive impacts and mitigating their potential to generate travel demand); 2) to identify and address impediments to policy measures that reduce travel demand and/or increase transport system efficiencies; and 3) to enable and empower initiatives to be taken at national, local and community level that will help deliver the objectives of the Strategy in a coherent and collaborative manner.

While the recent CSO Census 2022 data5 has highlighted that levels of transport demand have increased, it shows encouraging changes in behavioural trends and an appetite to respond when the right type of investments and decisions are made at local and national level, most notably with a reversing of the trend in total share of children and young people being driven to school.

The Strategy has been developed following extensive engagement with a wide range of interests and expertise. This collaborative approach to the design of policies and measures, from community to national level, will be vital to delivery. Experimentation and innovation will be critical to designing measures that deliver in a targeted manner, addressing local needs and circumstances, without creating unintended consequences for the broader transport and planning system. For this reason, many of the recommendations allow for the development of tailored solutions, in some cases requiring demonstration or pilot projects to test solutions which can then be scaled up.

Irish drivers are also embracing the switch to electric, with EV sales exceeding profiled trajectory targets to 2025, with 204,000 EVs on our roads as of end 2025. EV uptake can also be expected to be bolstered by significant expansions in our public charging infrastructure in coming years, and through the ambition in the Programme for Government 2025 to accelerate the electrification of the fleet by examining ‘the current EV grant system and the introduction of additional incentives with a view to increasing take-up of EVs and replacing older, polluting vehicles’.

This national approach to managing travel behaviour is also relatively unique from an international perspective. While there are examples of strategies at city or even at regional Government level, there is not an equivalent demand management strategy in place at a national level.

Building on the momentum of the substantial investment that is being made in public transport services, and infrastructure for active travel and EVs, this Strategy seeks to support that investment through the integration of measures that will encourage greater system efficiency in the transport system to the benefit of all.

5 https://www.cso.ie/en/statistics/population/censusofpopulation2022/

As there is no precedent or model against which we can benchmark success, a vital aspect of the Strategy will be to ensure that the impacts of any interventions are appropriately measured and evaluated from the outset, ensuring that the range of expected benefits can be optimised and measures can be re-calibrated and adjusted as and when required.

so, the Strategy can support a reduction in emissions, with significant co-benefits for Irish society in terms of road safety, air quality, health, and community life as well as assisting in objectives to revitalise urban centres in line with the Government’s Town Centre First policy. The new Programme for Government 2025 speaks to this through its commitment to ‘Work with local authorities and State agencies to develop decarbonised zones in our urban areas, focusing on pedestrianisation, cycling facilities, and use of renewable energy across all transport modes’.

It is intended that the Strategy will provide an overarching framework for the development and delivery of measures that can effect a transformational change in travel behaviour. The choice of measures that implementing authorities and others can take to achieve the strategy’s objectives will depend on various factors including, for example, the timing and the extent to which sustainable alternatives are provided in particular locations. The Strategy will provide the evidence base to inform these choices and the guidance to facilitate their delivery in a coherent and equitable manner.

At a government level, the National Transport Authority (NTA), Transport Infrastructure Ireland (TII) and the local authorities (LAs) are the primary implementing authorities for transport-related interventions - they will be required to determine the type of measures that are most suitable for implementation in any given geographical area or any element of the transport network in line with relevant statutory remits.

The Strategy is based on a set of principles with a particular focus on a just transition. It will guide, support, and enable relevant authorities to implement demand-led measures at national, regional, and local level to achieve the desired emissions reduction target and well-being benefits.

Some measures, such as policy, regulatory and fiscal measures, will require implementation at a national level by government departments. However, the Strategy is also widely applicable to many agencies and organisations beyond the transport and national government sphere, and this is reflected in the recommendations. The recommendations call for leadership across business, education, voluntary and community organisations to consider and make appropriate adjustments to the travel demand impacts of their activities. Travel behaviour is influenced by a range of factors, including individual and collective choices. The Strategy will enable and empower both organisations and individuals to support the making of more sustainable choices.

The concept of ‘demand management’ can often have negative connotations in a transport context – it can be viewed as limiting or as an attempt to constrain mobility. This is not the case. In fact, demand management measures have enormous potential to support improvements in the transport system, as well as having wider societal and environmental benefits, unlocking congestion, re-prioritising road space towards active, shared and public transport modes and ultimately, making sustainable travel choices more attractive, reliable and cost effective to wider cohorts of the population. It is understood that by building greater efficiency into the transport system, greater value can be extracted from the significant investment being made by Government.

A system-wide, societal change is required to reduce demand from transport – at individual, local and national level. The associated outcomes of such change will realise greater efficiency for the transport system, benefiting those who still need to travel by car, as well as the freight and logistics sector which are critical enablers for the Irish economy.

With people’s needs to the fore, this Strategy seeks to bring equity and well-being into the use of public space generally. With a particular focus on road and street spaces, the Strategy will support conditions that are more amenable and attractive to daily life, active travel, shared mobility and public transport services. In doing

The Strategy seeks to support a radical, yet equitable transformation in how we travel over the coming years, helping in the transition towards a fully decarbonised transport sector by 2050. By ‘moving together’, whether through small steps or systemic change, the cumulative impacts will be

Given the importance of ensuring a just transition in the design of these measures, special attention has been given to captive car usage – those journeys where alternatives are not readily available or affordable.

Strategy Structure

The work of expert subgroups established across all of these areas as part of the development of the Strategy is summarised in Chapter 9. Their inputs, along with the wider research and evidence considered, form the basis for the Strategy’s recommendations – described in Chapter 10 - and further detailed in Appendix 3.

The following sections set out the vision, and objectives of the Strategy, as well as the guiding principles which have been and will continue to be applied to its development and ongoing implementation (Chapter 1). It is vital to understand the scale of the challenge from a behavioural and systems perspective, but also the areas of real opportunity to effect that change (Chapter 2).

A strong representative oversight mechanism will be critical to the delivery of the Strategy given the system-wide changes and multiplicity of stakeholders involved. Recognising the wide range of benefits that can also be achieved through this Strategy, a broad range of key performance indicators will be used to monitor success, leveraging in particular the work by the Department of An Taoiseach and the OECD on developing a well-being framework6 and the 2022 report by the OECD on Redesigning Irelands Transport for Net Zero. The final chapters set out how the Strategy will be monitored and evaluated on an on-going basis – a critical component to ensure it remains responsive to the challenges that will inevitably arise.

The Strategy has the potential to serve a broad range of Government policy objectives and international obligations in relation to climate, energy efficiency, health and well-being, air quality and road safety (Chapters 3 and 4). It will also seek to leverage and/or re-enforce aspects of many existing policies and programmes to serve the objectives of this strategy. These policies and programmes – and their relevance to this Strategy - are described in Chapter 5. Communications and public engagement will be critical to the effective delivery of the strategy (Chapter 6) along with the necessary legislative levers to enable the implementation of potential new measures (Chapter 7).

Vision

The development of the Strategy has been informed by extensive research and analysis of a wide range of case studies both nationally and internationally – a survey of the relevant research and case studies are highlighted in Chapters 8 and Appendix 1.

The National Sustainable Mobility Policy (SMP), which was published in 2022, seeks ‘to connect people and places with sustainable mobility that is safe, green, accessible and efficient’ as part of its vision for ‘sustainable mobility in Ireland in 2030’. Similarly, this Strategy emulates and aligns itself to that vision, by seeking to sustainably connect people and places, with a transport system that is efficient, accessible, safe, and healthy for all.

Informed by research and evidence from national and international experience, the Strategy focuses on key areas where policy interventions will be most impactful such as integrated land-use and transport planning; optimal use of space; and fiscal measures, with a particular focus on main generators of travel demand both in terms of people and freight.

### Objectives

*Pages 22–22*

Objectives

our towns and promoting sustainable transport and creating attractive places where people can live and work. This Strategy will look to reinforce the aims of these policies by examining how we can make better use of space in our town and cities streets to create liveable centres that are destinations rather than congested thoroughfares. By developing homes that are closer to workplaces, services and amenities, people have less need for travelling in an unsustainable way and more time for spending with family and friends.

The objectives for the Strategy have been developed to capture the broad potential benefits that can be achieved, extending well beyond carbon emission reductions. They are set out below:

To support the health, safety and wellbeing of people, and minimise the social, and environmental costs of transport and car dependency including those related to congestion, air and noise pollution.

To support economic and financial sustainability in the just transition to net zero.

The Strategy will also play a significant role in helping to achieving health, safety and wellbeing objectives, particularly in the context of improving urban air quality and the ambition set out in Clean Air Strategy. Irish cities are some of the most heavily congested in Europe. Recent studies by TomTom and INRIX ranked Dublin the third and 11th most congested city in the world respectively, while Cork was ranked 42nd on TomTom and Galway was ranked 71st according to the INRIX Global Traffic Scorecard. In addition to increased emissions, congestion means lost time, more stress, and increased costs for people and business as well as greater noise and air pollution with significant impacts on health and personal well-being.

The transition to net zero must be managed in a way that is fiscally and economically sustainable, both for the citizen and the State – having regard to the Just Transition Framework. This Strategy has been developed with fairness and equity as a core principle and, being mindful of this, the Strategy encourages the implementation of costeffective measures and the exploration of nontraditional and innovative financing options, that are less onerous on the taxpayer and offer more value for money to the State.

To contribute to the national target of halving transport emissions, through network optimisation and reduced kilometres travelled.

To improve the efficiency of the existing transport system, by reducing demand for less sustainable journeys, by people and by freight.

This Strategy, combined with the SMP, will be a key lever in Ireland’s effort to meet climate targets for transport. The Climate Action Plan established indicators of progress in this respect including a 20% reduction in total vehicle kilometres travelled by 2030 compared with 2018. Other indicators over the same period include increases in daily active travel journeys (50%) and daily public transport journeys (130%). Along with supports to assist in the transition to more sustainable journeys as provided for in the SMP, this Strategy provides the enabling framework for relevant authorities to implement any complementary demand management measures that may be required to contribute to the required reduction in vehicles kilometres travelled – an important target in the effort to halve transport emissions in the sector by 2030 in line with the Government’s Climate Action Plan and to reduce harmful emissions from transport as part of the Clean Air Strategy (2023). By reducing demand for less sustainable journeys, considerable societal benefits can be realised for society.

The Strategy will provide a framework for improving the efficiency of the existing transport system, by encouraging the implementation of measures across all sectors in an effort to reduce transport demand. With little scope to build additional capacity within the transport system, particularly the road network, a projected increase in population over the coming decade combined with a growing economy means further pressure on the transport system with more congestion, longer journey times and greater associated social, environmental, and economic costs.

To encourage compact, mixed use and transport led development, reinforcing the role of the city, town and village centres as attractive, walkable, accessible destinations.

Government policy set out in the Revised National Planning Framework, and further developed through Town Centre First, highlight the importance of compact growth in reinvigorating

Status of the Strategy

The Strategy provides recommendations and guidance for further consideration that allow for the integration of demand management into decision making across Government and society.

Process to Develop the Strategy

The key policies underpinning the development of this Strategy are the SMP (2022) and the Climate Action Plan 2023. The SMP committed to developing an approach to traffic demand management, taking account of the recommendations of the Five Cities Demand Management Study, while the Climate Action Plan 2023 committed to developing this Strategy.

The Strategy does not provide consent or establish a framework for granting consent and will not be binding on any decisions relating to the granting of consent. The Strategy is situated alongside statutory documents that form the framework for future development consent of projects and are subject to requirements relating to environmental assessment/screening for environmental assessment, as appropriate.

At the outset, reflecting the system-wide changes required to deliver an effective strategy, the Department of Transport undertook an initial engagement with stakeholders on demand management at the department’s National Sustainable Mobility Stakeholder Forum in September 2023 and the National Climate Stakeholder Forum led by the Department of Energy, Climate and Communications (DECC) in May 2023.

In order to be realised, transport related plans and projects (in a similar way to other plans and projects from any other sector) will have to comply, as relevant, with various legislation, policies, plans and programmes (including requirements for lower-tier Strategic Flood Risk Assessment, Strategic Environmental Assessment, Appropriate Assessment and Environmental Impact Assessment and other licensing requirements as appropriate) that form the statutory decision-making and consent-granting framework.

Recognising the need for a whole of Government approach, a National Demand Management Steering Group was established in May 2023 consisting of the members as shown below.

Six expert subgroups were established for each of the above, consisting of stakeholders from government departments and agencies, local authorities, industry, academia, and non-governmental organisations (NGOs). While the six areas were not mutually exclusive and there was a degree of overlap across many of the subgroup discussions and conclusions, this approach provided for a series of different lenses through which to consider and evaluate the challenges and opportunities in developing a comprehensive strategy. The sub-groups were tasked with identifying demand management and related measures, including any barriers to implementation, that should be prioritised across multiple policy areas in Ireland.

Following this process, the Department published a draft of the Strategy as well as a draft Implementation Plan for public consultation during a four-month period in 2024. To align with the public consultation, the Department also held a further round of engagement with a wide range of stakeholders including, among others, representatives from community organisations, social justice advocacy groups, sporting bodies, third level institutions, business and industry as well as Disability Persons Representative Organisations (DPROs). A summary of the submissions and views collated during 2024, and how this process helped to strengthen the Strategy, was published alongside this document. The implementation plan has since been fully incorporated into the Strategy and is attached at Appendix 3.

Over 100 stakeholders, from 57 organisations, were involved in the work of these subgroups. In addition to the organisations represented on the Steering Group (see Figure 2), the following made important contributions to the process: Department of Health, Health Service Executive (HSE), Sport Ireland, Office of Public Works (OPW), the Department of Rural and Community Development and the Gaeltacht, Department of Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science, Enterprise Ireland, Heritage Council, Irish Planning Institute, Office of Planning Regulator, Land Development Agency, National Economic and Social Council (NESC), Northern and Western Regional Assembly

Wider public sentiment and attitudes towards demand management measures were also considered while shaping this Strategy, through a series of focus groups which were held in October 2023.

*Pages 25–25*

In general terms, the research found a general openness to small reductions in car usage at a personal level but with a desire for change to be fairly applied - and there were concerns about a direct attack on all car drivers. The research acknowledged that realistic change will depend on integrated, reliable public transport as well as public knowledge of this and pointed to the need for translating the change in behaviour and reduction of car usage into something positive. endeavors, in so far as is practicable, to maximise employment opportunities, and support persons and communities that may be negatively affected by the transition. That said, the objective of this Strategy will largely support all cohorts of society in terms of accessibility, safety, health and wellbeing.

Accessibility and Inclusion

It is recognised that people with additional mobility requirements or considerations, including disabled people and people with caring responsibilities, are more reliant on private cars for accessing services such as employment, education, retail, medical and entertainment. This Strategy will not prevent people from using cars, rather it will ensure the provision of accessible parking is retained, and strengthened, where possible. By ensuring urban centres have a balanced approach to land-use and transport planning, towns and cities can become more accessible, and permeable to all irrespective of age, gender, physical ability or ethnicity when collaboration and disability-proofed design is deployed.

Finally, the Department also took steps to assess the gender impacts of this Strategy and participated in a pilot phase of the European Commission’s Technical Support Instrument (TSI) flagship project on ‘Gender Mainstreaming in Public Policy and Budgeting’, led by Department of Children, Equality, Disability, Integration and Youth (DCEDIY). The development of this Strategy was one of two areas within the Department of Transport that volunteered for inclusion in the Irish workstream of this European project.

Guiding Principles in Developing and Implementing the Strategy

Rural Recognition

Not surprisingly, kilometres travelled by car, and the associated level of emissions per capita from transport, is significantly higher in rural areas than for inner suburban and urban areas. It is acknowledged that those living in rural areas, particularly in one-off houses or on offshore islands, have less access to sustainable means of travel compared with those living in or close to large urban centres. In light of this and given that the greatest health impacts from traffic congestion and pollution is experienced by urban residents and workers, it is intended that demand management measures will be rolled out, in the first instance, to such centres. Having regard to rural communities, where the provision of

Taking account of evidence, research and experience nationally and internationally in developing and implementing similar strategies, the following guiding principles were identified as key to ensuring broad public acceptance of the Strategy and as a means of elevating the key issues for consideration, particularly during the implementation phase.

Just Transition

Just transition is a key principle in efforts to address climate change. It is recognised that the cost of transitioning to carbon neutrality can have different impacts on different cohorts of the population. Not everyone will have the means to switch to driving an electric vehicle nor easy and immediate access to public transport.

This Strategy will seek to ensure that the timing and sequencing of measures will align with the rollout of new and enhanced public transport services and public transport and active travel infrastructure projects. ### Oversight and Responsibility

*Pages 26–26*

Economic and Financial Sustainability

The Group will meet on a quarterly basis to oversee delivery of the recommendations and actions and to shape developments in demand management as issues arise and as policy evolves. It will be serviced by the Department of Transport.

This Strategy supports measures that are economically and financially sustainable. An efficient transport network is vital to the economy. As well as social, personal and environmental costs, a poorly performing transport system, where congestion is commonplace, is proven to have a significant cost to the economy whereas the efficient movement of traffic is beneficial for industry and society alike. Access to sustainable transport modes and the optimisation of roadspace across users in favour of such modes has generally been found to have a positive impact on footfall and spend in retail outlets, particularly in urban centres. This Strategy has the potential to create a positive impact on both national and local economies.

As the policy levers and scope of this strategy extend well beyond transport and many of the recommendations are directed at business, community and voluntary sectors, it will be important to bring leadership perspectives from these sectors to the Steering Group.

To ensure that the implementation process is disability and equality proofed, having regard to obligations under the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities (UNCCPD), a consultative group will also be established to challenge and test the approach to implementation in consultation with relevant organisations including nominees from the likes of the Just Transition Commission, Disability Persons Representative Organisations (DPROs), business representatives and Local Authorities to ensure the guiding principles of the strategy remain central to implementation.

Oversight and Responsibility

This Strategy sets out several action-orientated recommendations, including the governance structures that will be established to monitor, evaluate, and measure impacts and outcomes.

Recommendations from this Strategy will be incorporated, where relevant, into further iterations of the Climate Action Plan, which will provide an additional layer of oversight and accountability at a government level.

Each recommendation in this Strategy is assigned a lead owner who will be responsible for its delivery where appropriate (see Appendix 3). However, the success of this Strategy will require continued collaboration from all involved. As recommended by the Climate Change Advisory Council (CCAC) in its 2024 Annual Review of the Transport Sector, timely delivery of actions across all aspects of the Strategy will need to be supported by a well-resourced, system wide implementation mechanism. This will require a whole of Government approach, including local government and enterprise and community/citizen buy-in to ensure delivery.

This approach is captured in Recommendation 32: Establish an oversight mechanism to monitor and evaluate the Strategy (see Chapter 10).

In that context, the National Demand Management Steering Group will be reconfigured with its membership and terms of reference refreshed as an oversight mechanism to monitor and evaluate implementation of the strategy. The Group should be a strong, advocate for change across the system as well as ensuring effective implementation of the recommendations contained in the strategy itself.

## Understanding the Challenge

### Understanding the Challenge

*Pages 27–32*

![Figure from page 27](assets/p027-f01.webp)

*Figure from page 27*

## 2. Understanding the Challenge

Our transport system must operate efficiently to secure the full benefits of Government investment. This can only be achieved by reducing congestion and overall demand for unsustainable journeys. By tackling these issues, other societal benefits can be unlocked through reductions in emissions and harmful localised pollutants.

Given the above, it is not surprising that the car is the dominant form of inland transport in all Member States compared with journeys by train, coach or bus. In 2018, travel by car accounted for 82.9% of passenger-kilometres7 across the EU, compared with 9.3% for coaches, buses and trolley buses, and 7.9% for trains. As of 2023, private cars made up 74.3% of total kilometres travelled8.

It is widely acknowledged, at a global level, that transport will be one of the most difficult sectors to decarbonise despite the scale of associated benefits, including to the economy and national competitiveness. Transport emissions are stubbornly difficult to reduce, compounded by an embedded reliance on private car use – nationally and internationally. Reflecting levels of car dependency across the EU, the average number of passenger cars per 1,000 inhabitants increased by 13.8% (from 501 to 570 passenger cars per 1,000 inhabitants) during the decade 2013-2023. (See Figure 3)

As previously mentioned, targets (if achieved) for the electrification of the vehicle fleet, along with an increasing penetration of biofuels in the fuel mix, could potentially help to reduce emissions in transport by circa 37% by 2030. The remaining 13% will largely be dependent on reducing kilometres travelled and a large-scale shift to more sustainable journeys. This translates to reducing the mode share of daily car journeys from over 70% to circa 50%, in parallel with an increase in daily public transport journeys by 130%, and an increase in daily active travel journeys by 50%.

Ireland was not immune from this trend in recent decades, with the number of licensed vehicles having increased by 250% from 915,000 in 1985 to 3.2 million in 2025. While the number of goods vehicles increased by 363% over this period, from 93,400 in 1985 to 432,460 in 2025, private cars accounted for most of this growth with an increase of 1.8 million vehicles. However, Ireland’s per capita ownership rate remains relatively low when compared to other EU Member States – indicating scope for additional growth and increased car dependency.

The level of change envisaged – over a very short period - is unprecedented. To provide some context, during the Covid crisis in 2020 when the country experienced a number of ‘lock downs’ and where long-distance travel by citizens and many businesses was severely curtailed, transport emissions fell by only 16%. While emissions from road freight were reduced, given the impacts of restrictions on certain industries (e.g., construction), goods vehicle traffic remained largely unhindered.

However, the following shows that most trips that people make are over relatively short distances. According to the 2024 NHTS, approximately 69% of all trips taken nationally are for distances of less than 10km with 36% of all trips below 3 kilometres. Just 11% of journeys are for 30km or more.

Figure 9: Breakdown of National Journeys by Distance

So, while the challenge to address car dependency – both practically and culturally - is significant, the above distances would suggest that there is significant scope for reducing car journeys in favour of other modes where sustainable alternatives are available and viable.

A key driver of the behavioural change needed is the relative cost and benefits to the individual and the availability of alternative options. Various techniques, including the National Transport Authority’s Regional Modelling System, are used to model likely responses to the relative costs of travelling by car versus other modes. However, it is evident from these models that travel demand is highly inelastic. This means that people tend to absorb very high-cost inflation before behavioural change occurs. Therefore, achieving the level of required modal shift from the car to more sustainable modes requires a significant change in awareness around the relative cost and benefits between modes - particularly as the car continues to be perceived as more convenient, more reliable and more cost effective for most journeys.

It is noteworthy that, using the NTA model, fossil fuel price increases are effective in bridging the cost competitiveness gap between the internal combustion engine (ICE) car and sustainable transport alternatives. Implementation of such measures, however, is more challenging given the potential impacts on cohorts of the population for whom sustainable alternatives are not available and/or where affordability is an issue, particularly in relation to buying Electric Vehicles (EVs). This is the challenge in designing the range of policy interventions that will cumulatively deliver the required behavioural change in a fair and equitable manner.

The objectives of this Strategy align closely and will support a range of United Nations (UN) and European Union (EU) policies and obligations on climate, sustainable development, transport and energy in particular. Key policies and their relevance are set out below:

although the Trans European Network – Transport (TEN-T) focuses very much on aligning investment towards a defined set of core and comprehensive transport corridors.

WHO – Air Quality and Health Impacts

The Paris Agreement to the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) has a legally binding overarching goal of holding “the increase in the global average temperature to well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels” and in line with recommendations of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) take steps “to limit the temperature increase to 1.5°C above preindustrial levels”.

There are important and related international ambitions and objectives associated with countering external costs from transport, through policies on health and sustainable development in its broadest terms. The UN spearheads such ambition through its agencies such as the World Health Organisation (WHO) and through its Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Of particular relevance are the WHO’s updated guidelines on outdoor (ambient) air pollution levels. They include very ambitious levels for nitrogen oxides (NOx), which is the primary pollutant associated with transport, followed by particulate matter (especially PM2.5). These guidelines, which were updated in 2021, reflect how air pollution affects many aspects of health, even at low levels.

To realise this, the European Commission approved the European Green Deal in 2020, which sets out a blueprint for transformational change including the goal of becoming the first climate-neutral continent by 2050. In the same period, the EU commits to achieving a 90% reduction in transport-related greenhouse gas emissions (relative to 1990 levels). Aligned to this is the EU’s ‘Fit for 55’ package, which commits to reducing net greenhouse gas emissions by at least 55% by 2030, compared to 1990 levels. For transport, the package is primarily focused on reducing fossil fuel dependency and increasing the availability of, and infrastructure for, renewable alternatives.

Transport is a key source of harmful emissions in urban environments, and the associated impacts, particularly in cities, is the single biggest environmental threat to human health according to the WHO. Accordingly, Ireland has committed to working towards the WHO values in its Clean Air Strategy.

The EU’s Effort Sharing Regulation (ESR) transforms the targets of the Clean Energy Package into binding annual climate targets for each Member State for the period 2021–2030. An amendment to the ESR came into force in May 2023. The new legislation increases the emissions reduction target for the targeted sectors including transport, from 29% to 40% by 2030, compared to 2005 levels.

There are other important international and EU level ambitions to reduce the health impacts of air pollution including:

ᴏ the EU Zero Pollution Action Plan (2021) which aims to reduce the health impacts of air pollution by 55% by 2030, compared to 2005; and

At a sectoral level, the EU’s Sustainable and Smart Mobility Strategy calls for decisive action to shift towards more public passenger transport like buses and trains, given the role that such modes can play in reducing greenhouse gas emissions and other environmental pressures such as air pollution and noise. The EU’s Strategy also acknowledges that the share of buses and trains in total passenger transport has changed very little across Europe since 2005, albeit with fluctuation and rebound in 2020 and 2021 due to the COVID-19 pandemic, and that a significant shift will not occur without decisive action. There is no equivalent EU strategy to address travel demand

ᴏ the new Ambient Air Quality Directive (due to be fully transposed in 2026), which sets more stringent air quality limits for all pollutants including NOx to be achieved by 2030.

Air pollutant emissions from the road transport sector are also regulated by EU emissions standards (such as the Euro emission standards for road vehicles) and fuel quality requirements. For example, the Euro 7 standard, which was adopted by the European Council in 2024, aims to further lower air pollutant emissions from exhaust fumes and brakes and will impose more stringent limits for various pollutants on heavy-duty vehicles in particular.

### Energy Taxation Directive (ETD)

*Pages 35–35*

UN Sustainable Development Goals

Renewable Energy – Amended Renewable Energy Directive

The 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development provides a shared blueprint for peace and prosperity for people and the planet, now and into the future. At its heart are 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), which reflect the need for all countries to urgently act as a global partnership.

As part of its Fit for 55 package, the Commission also adopted a legislative proposal for an amendment to the Renewable Energy Directive (RED) which came into force in 2023. The amended RED contains revised targets for renewable energy consumption in transport, of 29% energy share (known as the ‘RES-T’) or a 14.5% GHG reduction by 2030.

Sustainable transport is mainstreamed across several SDGs and targets, especially those related to food security, health, energy, economic growth, infrastructure, and cities and human settlements.

The EU target for renewable energy across all economic sectors has also been increased in the RED to 42.5% by 2030 energy share. Member States were required to set out interim targets in their submissions to the European Commission on National Energy and Climate Plans (NECP) by June 2024, against which compliance with the 2030 target is to be assessed. On 28 May 2025, the Commission published its EU Wide Assessment of the final updated National Energy and Climate plans (NECPs) accompanied by a Staff Working Document with the individual assessment of 23 final updated NECPs submitted and evaluated to that date.

Of specific importance to transport, and integral to the principles of this Strategy, are SDG targets 3.6 and 11.2:

ᴏ SDG Target 3.6 – By 2030, halve the number of global deaths and injuries from road traffic accidents.

ᴏ SDG 11.2: By 2030, provide access to safe, affordable, accessible and sustainable transport systems for all, improving road safety, notably by expanding public transport, with special attention to the needs of those in vulnerable situations, women, children, persons with disabilities and older persons.

Energy Taxation Directive (ETD)

The EU’s ETD entered into force in 2003 and lays down structural rules and minimum excise duty rates for the taxation of energy products used as motor and heating fuel, and electricity. However, given its age, the Directive does not suitably reflect the EU’s current climate and energy policy frameworks or legally binding climate targets.

This Strategy has been developed to align Ireland with international ambition and commitments in the above areas.

European Policy on Energy

Three other energy related policies at EU level are also particularly relevant to this Strategy:

Accordingly, this Directive is currently being revised, with proposals aimed at ensuring that the most polluting fuels are taxed the highest and that minimum rates can be revised annually to reflect most up-to-date prices.

Energy Efficiency Directive

A new recast Energy Efficiency Directive (EU) 2023/1791 introduces a series of measures to help accelerate energy efficiency, including embracing the “energy efficiency first” principle in energy and non-energy policies. Changes from the previous directives 2018/2002 and 2012/27/EU include the establishment of a legally binding target to reduce the EU’s final energy consumption by 11.7% by 2030 (relative to the 2020 reference scenario). Transport is the most significant contributor to final energy consumption as defined by the EED and can contribute significantly to achieving this target.

In summary, this Strategy is aligned with and will support a wide range of UN and EU objectives and obligations across climate, transport, energy, and sustainable development sectors. Early and effective implementation of the Strategy will contribute to Ireland’s economy wide efforts to comply with EU targets and mitigate against noncompliance penalties and associated fines.

## 4. National Climate and Transport Policy Drivers

### National Sustainable Mobility Policy

*Pages 37–37*

At a national level, this Strategy will support the achievement of objectives on climate. It will also contribute positively to a variety of policies in the transport domain such as policy on sustainable mobility, freight and road safety. The key polices and their relevance is set out below:

To realise the abatement required, CAP23 identified the need to reduce vehicle kilometres travelled by 20% relative to what would otherwise be expected under a business-as-usual approach. It included a specific action to support this target through the development of a national demand management strategy.

Climate Action Plan

As the population continues to grow, reducing travel demand will become increasingly important. The development and implementation of this Strategy thus form crucial elements of our decarbonisation pathway and fulfills the commitment to develop a national demand management strategy as per CAP23.

The challenge that Ireland faces to comply with national climate targets cannot be overstated. Climate Action Plan 2023 (CAP23), which was the first statutory Climate Action Plan that incorporated both the economy-wide targets and legally binding sectoral emission ceilings under the provisions of the Climate Action and Low- Carbon Development (Amendment) Act 2021, brought a renewed focus to the need for systemic action, at all levels of Government. Transport is particularly challenging. Emissions from the sector had been steadily rising over 2021 and 2022 at a rate of approximately 6% per annum, reflecting the strength of the economy, full employment, and continued population growth in Ireland. This trend peaked in 2023 and 2024 will be the first year since COVID where transport emissions have reduced by 1.2% despite continued economic growth.

Corrective Action Modelling Project

The Department of Transport and the NTA are undertaking a transport modelling and appraisal exercise (the Corrective Actions Modelling Project) to revise and recalibrate the modelling that was completed for CAP23, and to re-assess and strengthen the decarbonisation policy pathway for transport to 2030. The objective of this project is to develop clear policy pathways for adhering to carbon budget ceilings in transport over the coming years (for more detail on this project see ‘Modelling for CAP’ in Chapter 8).

CAP23 recognises that achieving our climate ambition will require a multi-faceted, integrated, and interdependent combination of measures – measures that can change long-established patterns and preferences in how and where we travel, while also addressing many potential factors that influence transport decisions. To this end, CAP23 applied an Avoid-Shift-Improve lens to the range of measures being progressed under the Plan. CAP24 and CAP25 have continued to build on the policy shift set out in CAP23.

Without the effective and early implementation of the measures envisaged in this Strategy, it is highly unlikely that transport emissions will fall within the sectoral emissions ceilings over the period out to 2030. Further iterations of the Climate Action Plan will reflect the implementation path for this Strategy.

National Sustainable Mobility Policy

While Government will continue to support and prioritise ‘Improve’ measures related to electrification of the vehicle fleet and higher penetration levels of renewable transport fuels in the fuel mix, these measures alone will not deliver the emissions abatement required for the transport sector — more corrective action is required. Under the decarbonisation pathway set out by CAP23 and subsequent Plans, approximately 2 MtCO2eq./year (or roughly 25%) of emissions abatement will have to be achieved through ‘Avoid’ and ‘Shift’ measures by 2030. This level of abatement may even be higher when work is undertaken to update and recalibrate the emissions trajectory to 2030.

The National Sustainable Mobility Policy (SMP), which was published in April 2022, sets out a strategic framework to 2030 for active travel (walking and cycling) and public transport journeys to help Ireland meet its climate obligations.

Action Plans under the SMP support a wide range of interventions including, among other things, public transport infrastructure and services, active travel promotion and supports, road safety initiatives, legislative measures, research, and public engagement. The Policy also supports the implementation of large-scale transport projects including MetroLink and DART+ in Dublin,

### Metropolitan Area Transport Strategies

*Pages 38–38*

BusConnects in the five cities, the Connecting Ireland scheme in rural areas, and an Active Travel Infrastructure Programme providing high-quality cycling infrastructure across the country.

noise, and enhance attractiveness and accessibility of cities, neighbourhoods and town centres.’

Metropolitan Area Transport Strategies

Under the Active Travel Infrastructure Programme, 1000km of walking and cycling infrastructure has been delivered nationwide, and over 1,000 projects are currently being progressed and delivered under the Active Travel Programme across all 31 local authorities.

Ensuring coherence between transport and wider planning decisions will be critical to this Strategy. The integration of sustainable mobility and land use planning in our cities is supported through the Metropolitan Area Transport Strategies (MATS).

In effect, the SMP sets out the ambition for ‘supply’ side measures that will complement and re-enforce the ‘demand’ side recommendations provided for in this Strategy. Both will need to work together to ensure they are mutually supportive and effective. The timing of supply side schemes will be critical to the success of any future demand management measures.

Through MATS, programmes and a vision for sustainable transport investment and service enhancements are established for each city over a 20-year period. Encompassing active travel, bus, light rail, heavy rail and park and ride facilities, these MATS are renewed as part of a regular cycle of review (typically every 6 years).

In the recent years, new and revised MATS have been completed and published for the Waterford and Limerick-Shannon metropolitan areas, and the Greater Dublin Area (GDA). It is also anticipated that the NTA will commence the review process of the 2020 Cork Metropolitan Area Transport Strategy (CMATS) in 2026.

The SMP highlights the potential for the development of rail as a sustainable transport mode. The All-Island Strategic Rail Review (AISRR), published in July 20249, aims to inform policy and future strategy for the railways in both Ireland and Northern Ireland. It has examined how the island’s railways are currently used, what role rail could play in the future and how the island’s railway could better serve the people of both jurisdictions. The AISRR has focused on how the rail network across the island could contribute to the decarbonisation of the island’s transport system, promote sustainable connectivity into and between major cities, enhance regional accessibility and support balanced regional development. 32 strategic recommendations have been put forward to achieve these goals.

It is worth noting that the GDA Transport Strategy (below) addresses the wider Dublin and Eastern Regions, extending beyond the Dublin Metropolitan Area Spatial Plan (MASP). Collectively, these reflect the latest policy commitments to reduce transport emissions by 50%, in accordance with the provisions of the Climate Act. Following extensive public consultation, these updated MATS recognise the need to reduce high levels of car dependency in each of our cities and enhance the provision of sustainable alternatives.

The recommendations of the AISRR will be implemented through a Project Prioritisation Strategy, with all future projects being subject to appropriate feasibility options and environmental assessments at project level, where required.

Outside the Greater Dublin Area, the MATS are currently non-statutory plans developed by the NTA in co-operation with the relevant Local Authorities and other Agencies. As committed to in CAP23 and the SMP, the Department of Transport intends to amend legislation to extend the remit of the NTA to the five cities, which will bring a coherent approach to transport objectives consistent with the Climate Action Plan.

Actions to advance the recommendations in the AISRR, along with all other sustainable mobility programmes, projects and initiatives, are included in a new SMP Action Plan to cover the last five years of the Policy, 2026 to 2030. This is in line with the 2025 Programme for Government commitment to ‘Develop a new Sustainable Mobility Action Plan which promotes sustainable modes and transport- oriented development to address congestion, improve air quality and reduce

The MATS process, along with the associated guidance documents provided by the NTA and TII, are a vital planning framework on which to develop and specify demand management
