### Electrification and Renewable Energy Policy

*Pages 39–39*

measures which will support the achievement of this Strategy’s objectives and that of the Climate Action Plan. Their importance is reflected in relevant recommendations in Chapter 10.

The work undertaken by the TOD Working Group informed the National Planning Framework (NPF) 2025, which introduced TOD as a new element of the Framework, with potential for supporting the delivery of new sustainable communities at brownfield and greenfield locations. National Planning Objectives 10, 97 and 99, combined, seek to deliver TOD at suitable locations (as identified in Metropolitan Area Strategic Plans) supported by the necessary institutional and funding arrangements to support the development and accelerated delivery.

Transport Orientated Development

Transport Orientated Development is a form of urban development that seeks to maximise the provision of housing, employment, public services and leisure space in close proximity to frequent, high-quality transport services. It is a form of development that is friendlier to public transport users, cyclists and pedestrians, and seeks to convert car trips to public and active transport trips. This in turn improves mobility and environmental conditions and delivers more efficient and sustainable urban development. TOD can also be designed to provide a higher proportion of social and affordable housing. (National Economic and Social Council 2019 report)

GDA Strategy and Demand Management Scheme

As a key delivery agency for sustainable mobility, the NTA’s Greater Dublin Area Transport Strategy 2022-2042 sets out how the transport system will be developed over this period to meet the needs of a growing population while reducing emissions by 50% across the region, covering Dublin, Meath, Wicklow and Kildare, by 2030. As well as significant investment in active, shared and public transport infrastructure and services, a key lever to achieve such reduction will be a GDA Demand Management Scheme, which the NTA expect to publish by 2026. This Strategy provides the strategic framework and impetus for that Scheme and further schemes to be developed in the remaining five cities and other local authority areas in a coherent manner.

The PfG commits to focusing on linking new housing developments to public transport and schools through TOD. Further to this, the Revised NPF 2025 affirms a strong commitment to TOD with potential for supporting the delivery of new sustainable communities at brownfield and greenfield locations - by locating them along existing or planned high-capacity public transport corridors.

Electrification and Renewable Energy Policy

A Transport Orientated Development Working Group, which was established in 2021 under Housing for All, is looking at opportunities for TOD in the five cities of Dublin, Cork, Limerick, Galway and Waterford. The intention of this process was to identify strategic brownfield and greenfield lands served by existing and / or planned high-capacity public transport services that are suitable for TOD.

CAP23 targets the electrification of almost a third of the total private car fleet in Ireland by 2030, which will require 100% of new private registrations to be electric before the end of this decade.

A report for Dublin was published in June 2023. It identified opportunities for up to 130,000 residential units at fourteen locations with further significant opportunities identified in the other as yet unpublished reports. It is clear these reports identify the potential for significant numbers of residential units and the analysis undertaken underlines the role that targeted public investment in certain transport projects can play in enabling the delivery of large-scale housing delivery in the major metropolitan areas.

### Freight and Haulage Strategy

*Pages 40–40*

Ireland has over almost 204,000 EVs and plugin hybrids on the road at the end of 2025 and Zero Emission Vehicles Ireland (ZEVI) provides a range of grants and incentives to the public and businesses to support the uptake of EVs. Additionally, to support the uptake of EVs, ZEVI published the Electric Vehicle Charging Infrastructure Strategy 2022 – 2025 and an accompanying implementation plan10 which sets out a pathway for the delivery of EV charge point infrastructure. This Strategy, which will be revised in 2026, will also provide for the rollout of EV infrastructure as required under the EU’s Alternative Fuels Infrastructure Regulation (AFIR), where a 300% increase in the amount of public recharging infrastructure is targeted for delivery.

particularly efforts to reduce emissions, which have direct implications for energy consumption. This Strategy, along with the other policy measures being pursued as part of the transport decarbonisation pathway will support the achievement of the Government’s energy efficiency and security objectives.

Having regard to strategic energy security risks, this Strategy has a role in helping to restrain national energy use, given its remit in reducing vehicle kilometres travelled. In 2022, as part of its contribution to the development of the National Energy Security Framework, the Department of Transport identified a range of potential transport-related energy efficiency and demand restraint measures as well as likely mechanisms for implementation over three energy constrained scenarios – some of which have been taken into consideration in this Strategy.

The transition to EVs has the potential to make the greatest contribution to tackling emissions from medium to longer journeys, particularly those taken by non-urban captive car users who have no sustainable mobility alternative, and this is recognised in the Strategy. Similarly, electric technology and alternatives are expected to play a significant role in the freight, haulage and logistics sector. In relation to energy efficiency (see below), Ireland was required to achieve energy savings of 40% by 2030. EVs are at least three times more energy efficient than their ICE equivalents.

The ‘Energy Security in Ireland to 2030’ report, which was published in November 2023, provides a roadmap for ensuring a sustainable, affordable and secure energy landscape that balances energy risk and resilience against binding European and domestic energy and climate commitments. This Strategy will support, and align with, the goals of this roadmap.

Freight and Haulage Strategy

Ireland also has ambitious plans for the penetration of renewable transport fuels (including biofuels) into our transport fuel mix. The Renewable Transport Fuel Policy, 2025 – 2027 sets out a pathway to incentivise the supply of renewable transport fuel through annual increases in the renewable transport fuel obligation rate to 2030.

Ireland’s domestic and international road freight sector is an integral part of the economy, with the vast majority of freight movements in Ireland taking place by road. Ambition to increase rail freight in Ireland is considered through the All- Island Strategic Rail Review and also features in this Strategy.

While Ireland will seek to maximise the sizeable contributions to emissions abatement that electrification and renewables can make, this Strategy seeks to target the remaining abatement that is required to achieve the legally binding targets set for transport by 2030.

Heavy-Duty Vehicles (HDVs) (including buses and Heavy Goods Vehicles) and Light Duty Vehicles (LDVs), represents 51% of overall road transport emissions in Ireland and without significant intervention, this is likely to grow as economic activity increases over the coming decade. Currently more than 99% of HDV on Irish roads are diesel-fueled. Road freight transport is energy intensive and while electric vehicle technologies are now widely commercially available, they have a significant upfront cost differential when compared to a traditional ICE alternative.

Energy Efficiency and Security

Transport is the largest source of energy consumption in Ireland. For this reason, policies and related targets on energy efficiency, renewable energy and energy security are heavily reliant on effective transport measures,

### National Investment Framework for Transport in Ireland

*Pages 42–42*

In addition to this, the quantity of tonnage that is being moved by road continues to grow, responding to population growth, increased consumer demand and a post-Covid economic recovery. The decarbonisation pathway for transport set out in the Climate Action Plan assumes improvements in Ireland’s HGV fleet will reduce carbon emissions by 10% overall by 2030 - primarily through reduced fuel consumption and/ or increased fuel efficiency and use of renewable fuels, rather than a significant penetration of zero emissions trucks before 2030.

the delivery of Project Ireland 2040’s National Strategic Outcomes, particularly those related to enhanced regional accessibility, strengthened rural economies and communities and sustainable mobility. Priority in TII’s Strategy is given to decarbonisation of national road investment through the Department of Transport’s National Investment Framework for Transport in Ireland, described hereunder.

National Investment Framework for Transport in Ireland

Published in 2023, Ireland’s Road Haulage Strategy 2022 – 2031 sets out the measures needed to deliver on key objectives for the industry, including decarbonisation. Key measures include signing a non-binding commitment to reach a minimum of 30 percent of all new Medium and Heavy-Duty Vehicle sales (bus and truck) to be zero-emission by 2030, as well as increasing the use of renewable fuels as a transitional measure, made possible by the Renewable Transport Fuels Obligation Scheme. Of relevance to this Strategy are measures that explore the potential for operational and digital efficiencies, as well as freight consolidation, road usage charging, and scope for behavioural change through emissions-based taxation of HGVs.

The National Investment Framework for Transport in Ireland (NIFTI) is the Department of Transport’s high-level strategic framework for prioritising future investment in the land transport network.

Published in December 2021, NIFTI set out four strategic investment priorities to address transport challenges. These are: decarbonisation; protection and renewal; mobility of people and goods in urban areas; and enhanced regional and rural connectivity.

Accordingly, NIFTI establishes high-level investment priorities to efficiently and effectively address key transport challenges. More specifically, the high-level investment priorities are guided by a modal hierarchy and an intervention hierarchy. Under the Modal Hierarchy Framework, sustainable modes of transport will be prioritised, while acknowledging that the private car will remain an important mode of travel in much of Ireland.

This ‘Moving Together’ Strategy seeks to encompass, and align itself with, the objectives of the haulage strategy. However, the freight and haulage sector presents a variety of specific and complex challenges and opportunities from a demand management perspective. As above, emissions from the freight sector will continue to increase without significant intervention. For this reason, an expert sub-group was established to focus on this area and to develop a set of sector specific recommendations. These are set out in detail in the sub-group report, summarised in Chapter 9 and further reflected in the full suite of recommendations in Chapter 10.

Under the Intervention Hierarchy Framework, NIFTI sets out four high-level categories of investment. These four categories of investment are used to inform intervention decisions, both at the budgetary level and the project level. These four high-level categories are: maintain; optimise; improve; and new. Maintaining the existing transport network is given first priority, followed by maximising the value of the network through optimising its use. Infrastructural investments are only to be considered after these two categories have been assessed as inappropriate for the identified problem, with upgrades to existing infrastructure to be considered before new infrastructure.

National Roads 2040

Transport Infrastructure Ireland (TII) published its strategic vision for roads in 2023. ‘National Roads 2040’ seeks to support an evolving transport system which has safety, innovation and accessibility at its core, and includes over thirty commitments to address issues such as population growth, decarbonisation, climate adaptation, safety and congestion. National roads are key economic enablers and are essential to

The objectives of this Strategy, notably, to increase the efficiency of the existing transport system, are clearly aligned and reinforce the strategic framework provided by NIFTI.

### Road Safety Policy

*Pages 43–43*

Road Safety Policy

Analysis of the 2025 data shows that there were 174 fatal collisions resulting in 185 fatalities. In 2025, driver fatalities represent 41% of fatalities (76), while passengers represent 11% of fatalities (21). Compared to 2024 there has been an increase in fatalities amongst drivers (+5), pedestrians (+8), pedal cyclists (+2), and motorcyclists (+13). In contrast there has been a decrease in fatalities among passengers (-14), and no change in the number of fatalities among E-Scooter users. Of fatalities 74% (137) were male and 26% (48) were female.

Every citizen is entitled to use our roads without concern for their safety. For this reason, every fatality and serious injury that occurs on Irish roads is a tragedy for all involved and effected. The numbers of fatalities in 2025 (185) were 8% higher than in 2024 (172). Fatality figures in recent years have increased to levels last seen approximately a decade ago.

![Figure from page 43](assets/p043-f01.webp)

*Figure from page 43*

Apart from the obvious human costs, the financial costs of road traffic collisions in Ireland are also significant, conservatively estimated at €1.36 billion in 2023 across the economy. When appraising transport investment, safety is a factor in the Cost-Benefit Analysis. The Transport Appraisal Framework (TAF) measures the social and economic costs of collisions by monetising them and including them in the Cost-Benefit Analysis (c. €2.8m for fatal incidents, €0.03m for minor incidents). The TAF also requires a safety assessment in the Transport and Accessibility Appraisal (TAA) which indicates how options for a scheme perform on the safety front.

The framework for road safety is set out in the Government’s fifth Road Safety Strategy 2021 – 2030. This follows international best practice, aligns the timeframe with EU and UN targets, and is based on a Safe System approach. This approach adopts a holistic view of the total road transport system and the interaction between people, vehicles, and the road environment to create a safe mobility system forgiving of human error. The Strategy has the ambitious target of reducing road deaths and serious injuries by 50% by 2030, and to achieve Vision Zero (as close as possible to no deaths or serious injuries) on Irish roads by 2050.

In the context of Moving Together, demand management measures have considerable cobenefits for road safety. A 2025 study by TU Dublin on behalf of the Road Safety Authority, namely ‘RSA Action 44 - Towards Vision Zero for Cities & Towns: People, Process, Package of Interventions’ examines the mechanisms for reducing vehicular traffic in cities and towns, and how this can improve road safety, as well as increase quality of life for residents and regenerate towns centres. The study shows how the small Galician city of Pontevedra was revitalised and achieved Vision Zero for road deaths over many years by implementing pedestrian-focused policies, and reducing the dominance of congested roads. Other use cases in the study illustrate the importance of 30-40km/hr speed limits, reducing through-traffic and the importance of investing in good walking and cycling infrastructure, public transport and high-quality public spaces.

![Figure from page 43](assets/p043-f02.webp)

*Figure from page 43*

Notwithstanding the increases in fatalities in recent years, there is good reason to believe that the figures could have been worse had certain measures not been implemented and that further measures, including benefits from certain demand management style measures to reduce vehicle kilometres travelled, could lead to more improvements and more lives saved.

It should be remembered also that, while a demand management strategy can help to contribute to road safety, the converse is also true – improved road safety can contribute to demand management (by making active travel more attractive).

### Speed Limits Review

*Pages 44–44*

For example, TII’s appraisal methodology uses historical information to derive expected numbers of collisions per million vehicle kilometres (PEPAG-02030-01 (tiipublications.ie), p. 15). This depends on the type of road (with/without central divider) and the speed limit (above/below 60km/hr). All else being equal, a reduction in vehicle kilometres travelled is shown to lead to a modelled decrease in collisions. The scale of benefit would also vary considerably based on the scheme. While cyclist fatalities increased in 2025, they remain close to historical low levels. It is likely that increased investment in road space reallocation and segregation of vulnerable road users has played an important role in these outcomes.

appropriate for ensuring the safety of all road users in any given urban area, including limits of 30kmph.

Speed limits are an important tool in managing traffic, and in efforts to rebalance space in favour of more vulnerable road users such as pedestrians, and cyclists. The speed of vehicles also has an impact on emissions, and this was considered as part of the Review. For example, emission rates are highest at lower speeds for fossil fuel vehicles. However, emissions reduce as speed increases to between 60 km/h and 80 km/h. Beyond 80km/h, emissions begin to increase slowly again. Frequent acceleration and deceleration (e.g., in congested traffic) also increases emissions. An efficient, and free-moving transport system that complies with safe speeds can also have an optimum effect on emissions arising from fossil fuelled Internal Combustion Engines (ICEs).

Over half of all 2025 fatalities occurred on high speed roads, outside of urban areas - roads with a speed limit equal to or greater than 80km/h. If public transport uptake is further increased in rural areas in particular, this should help to reduce the high share of fatalities on these non-urban roads.

Finally, the Review also pointed to the wide ranging and adverse health, social and economic effects of noise pollution and the role that appropriate speed management can have in reducing excessive traffic noise, with international efforts to achieve noise abatement measures in urban areas increasingly focusing on the implementation of 30km/h speed limits.

Speed Limits Review

The current approach to speed limits in Ireland is guided by several key policy areas e.g., road safety, climate, and the environment, and is reflected in specific plans such as the Government’s Road Safety Strategy and associated action plans, the Climate Action Plan and the Sustainable Mobility Policy (SMP).

In summary, there are a wide variety of national climate, transport and energy policies that are strategies which will benefit from the effective implementation of this ’Moving Together’ Strategy and many of these are complementary and mutually reinforcing. The next iteration of the SMP Action Plan will need to ensure continued alignment and accelerated delivery of ‘supply side’ measures that complement the roll-out of demand management measures. Embedding demand management principles into the MATS process and the development of the GDA scheme will be critical to advancing the objectives of this Strategy. Policy on the electrification of the private transport fleet will also need to take greater account of just transition principles in the context of so called ‘captive car users’ in certain areas.

A review of speed limits was undertaken in recent years with the final report published in September 2023. This Speed Limit Review was a high impact action during Phase 1 of the government’s Road Safety Strategy 2021-2030, and proposed a reduction in the speed limits across a range of different road categorises. The first phase of these changes – the reduction in the default speed limit on rural local roads from 80km/h to 60km/h - took effect nationally on 7 February 2025. This affects approximately 82,000km of road — about 80% of all roads.

It is intended that the second phase of the speed limit changes - the introduction of a 30km/h speed limit in built-up and urban areas - will be implemented by way of ‘special speed limit byelaws’, rather than ‘default speed limit’ as above. As the making of ‘special speed limit bye-laws’ is a reserved function, this means that local councils can determine the speed limits that are most

The co-benefits of this Strategy particularly in terms of road safety will be captured as part of the well-being framework for its evaluation (Chapter 12).

## 5. Leveraging Other Policies and Programmes

### National Planning Framework

*Pages 46–46*

A range of non-transport focused policies and programmes are also vital to advancing the objectives of this Strategy and will also benefit significantly from its effective implementation. Many relate to spatial and land use planning and the support programmes that underpin their objectives – e.g., the National Planning Framework, regional and rural development policies and programmes, Local Authority Climate Action Plans, and parking measures. Fiscal policies are critical to achieving climate objectives and are particularly effective for transport. Wider polices in relation to education, health, accessibility and inclusion, along with support programmes for business, tourism, sports and leisure sectors will be both critical enablers for and beneficiaries of the objectives of this Strategy. The key policies and their relevance are set out below.

NPF implementation is hugely important for transport. Overall levels of transport demand and travel patterns are driven to a large extent by Government policy (national, regional and local) and settlement patterns. Low-density development and the dispersal of residential settlements, commercial zones, schools, and workplaces in peripheral areas, are major contributory factors to high levels of car dependency and the transport poverty that is experienced in some regions, posing a particular challenge for the decarbonisation of the sector.

Throughout the NPF, compact and sustainable growth is recognised as being a strategic economic objective that will aid in the reduction of private car travel and improve the ‘liveability’ and quality of life in urban places. Consolidation of development, instead of urban sprawl, is also important in creating value for money for the State in terms of investing in infrastructure.

National Planning Framework

Key to the success of sustainable and accessible mobility objectives is compact growth, better integrated land use, and planning that takes a transport-led approach to the development of housing and new settlements. As building communities predicated on car ownership is no longer sustainable, better integrated, transport-led planning will promote a modal shift towards public transport and empower people to move away from car dependency.

The revised NPF was approved by Government in April 2025. It emphasises the need for Transport- Oriented Development (ToD) by providing new sustainable communities at brownfield and greenfield locations along existing or planned high-capacity public transport corridors. The revised Framework will be critical in the context of meeting climate objectives and emphasising the intrinsic link between transport and spatial planning; particularly in the context of compact growth, demand management, transport orientated development and improved, people-centred urban centres. There has been close engagement between departments and relevant agencies on how this revision and its implementation can optimise outcomes from a transport and specifically a demand management perspective (see Recommendation 1 in Chapter 10).

The National Planning Framework (NPF) and the National Development Plan (NDP) combine to form Project Ireland 2040. The NPF sets the Government’s vision and strategy for the development of Ireland to 2040, while the NDP provides the supporting capital investment plan for the period. Project Ireland 2040 supports sustainable mobility objectives through certain strategic outcomes not least the following: Compact Growth; Enhanced Regional Accessibility; Strengthened Rural Economies; Sustainable Mobility; and Transition to a Low Carbon and Climate Resilient Society. Like this Strategy, wellbeing, equality, and opportunity represent the core, and interdependent themes of Project Ireland 2040 (PI 2040).

![Figure from page 46](assets/p046-f01.webp)

*Figure from page 46*

The NPF, which was revised in 2025, is at the apex of strategic planning in Ireland and establishes the overall national planning policy strategy, with which other planning policy at the national level is aligned.

### Local Authority Climate Action

*Pages 47–47*

The National Development Plan

Regional Spatial and Economic Strategies

As part of PI 2040, the revised National Development Plan, which was reviewed in 2025, sets out the Government’s over-arching investment strategy with planned capital expenditure of over €100 billion for the period 2026-2030. The NDP allocates €24.3 billion for transport over this period to help develop active travel, bus and rail infrastructure, which will facilitate the transition to sustainable mobility and a low carbon transport system. An additional €2.25 billion in capital investment from windfall corporation tax receipts was made available to be allocated in the 2024 to 2026 period. This funding will facilitate the progression of important projects and enable more rapid development of key Programme for Government commitments under the NDP such as further delivery of immediate Public Transport projects such as Dart.

The three Regional Assemblies form the regional tier of governance in Ireland and the Regional Assemblies implement the objectives of PI 2040 at a regional level through the Regional Spatial and Economic Strategies (RSESs). The RSESs are strategic plans and investment frameworks to shape future growth in the Regions, which support the implementation of the NPF and align with the investment priorities of the NDP. The Regional Assemblies also have oversight and coordination in relation to the compliance of statutory city and county development plans at a local policy planning level, ensuring these plans are aligned with the RSESs, and hence the NPF.

Under the provisions of the Planning and Development Act 2024, regional spatial and economic strategies are required to identify sustainable settlement patterns and transportation strategies in urban and rural areas (including public transportation). This includes a statement of the actions being taken (or proposed) for the purpose of ensuring the effective integration of transport and land-use planning and how the regional spatial and economic strategy proposes to address matters identified in any relevant transport strategy of the National Transport Authority.

Budget 2026 sets out €3.4 billion in capital funding for transport. This funding will support a number of initiatives including enhanced capacity on existing public transport routes and ongoing investment of almost one million euro per day in cycling and walking infrastructure.

The NDP emphasises the need for a transport-led approach to housing development, allowing for the emergence of sustainable and well-connected communities where active travel is feasible and attractive for many localised journeys and good quality public transport is available to facilitate longer journeys into major urban centres.

Local Authority Climate Action

The success of policy on climate and transport relies heavily on the support of Local Government and the implementation capacity of Local Authorities (LAs). It necessitates the formulation and implementation of measures aimed at better aligning transport with spatial and land use planning, as well as the implementation of demand management measures such as, but not limited to, road space reallocation, kerbside management, the provision of active and shared mobility infrastructure and services, and the creation of low traffic/car free environments.

The NDP also continues to support the protection and maintenance of the existing road network to ensure the safe and efficient travel of all road users across the country, as well as targeted new road investment to provide for regional connectivity and compact urban growth.

The ongoing current funding requirements such as pay, maintenance and other operational costs associated with NDP-funded projects are not captured under the investment envelope outlined above.

These measures are key to supporting mode choice where viable alternatives exist. The role of LAs in demand management cannot be overstated. Increased capacity will be needed to achieve the objectives of this Strategy and underpin the ambition expected from Local Authority Climate Action Plans.

The critical link between travel behaviour, settlement patterns and planning, and the extent to which the NPF can advance the objectives of this Strategy, are discussed in more detail in Chapter 9 and inform a number of important recommendations in Chapter 10.

### Urban Renewal and Rural Development

*Pages 48–49*

LAs play a key role in climate mitigation and adaptation, as set out in Section 16 of the Climate Action and Low Carbon Development (Amendment) Act 2021. This requires, inter alia, each local authority to prepare a local authority climate action plan (LA CAP) specifying the mitigation and the adaptation measures to be adopted within their administrative area. The plans will be updated once every five years. Each local authority can deploy a bespoke approach to the style and structure of plans, but all LA CAPs must align with the national Climate Action Plan to ensure the delivery of our climate goals at local and community levels.

signed for a period of six years (2023 – 2029) for the four CAROs to continue their work in supporting local government to coordinate and deliver climate actions, and to assist them in developing and implementing their LA CAPs.

DCEE also funds upskilling and capacity building in climate action in the local government sector, with training developed for local authority staff and elected members, through the ongoing Local Authority Climate Action Training Programme.

In 2021, the European Commission set up the EU Climate Neutral and Smart Cities Mission. Its aim is to support 100 cities to become climate neutral by 2030 and to pave the way for all cities in Europe to be climate neutral by 2050. In April 2022, Cork City and Dublin City were selected from over 300 applicants to be part of the initiative. Galway City was selected to be part of the Pilot Cities Programme in March 2023.

The LA CAPs should see each local authority actively translating national climate policy to local circumstances, with the prioritisation and acceleration of evidence-based measures. This will assist in the delivery of climate goals at local and community levels and a reduction of greenhouse gas emissions in each local authority area.

The cities’ success in the application process is a testament to the cities and demonstrates their determination to take effective climate action at a local level. Demand management measures are likely to play a significant role for these cities in their plans to deliver on this ambition.

To support the development of the LA CAPs, the Department of Transport wrote to all LAs setting out the range of transport related measures to consider as part of their plans and the importance of linking measures to the metrics set out in the transport chapter of CAP 23. The CCAC in its 2023 Annual Review called on LAs to include specific actions and indicators in respect of accessibility, modal shift and active travel in their CAPs.

It is clear that there are various levers and supports available to LAs which can be deployed to deliver demand management measures. The vital role of LAs as implementing authorities is rehearsed across all of the expert subgroup reports that input into this strategy and features in various key recommendations in Chapter 10, notably Recommendations 3 to 9 with a view both to enabling LAs to deliver and also providing guidance and tools to develop appropriate demand management strategies to support the achievement of the obligations placed on them under the Climate Act 2021.

As part of its LA CAPs, each local authority is responsible for establishing Decarbonisation Zones (DZs). These are intended to be the focus for a range of climate mitigation, adaptation and biodiversity measures and will help local authorities to identify projects which will contribute to the delivery of the national climate objective. Aligned to this is the 2025 Programme for Government commitment to work with local authorities and State agencies to develop decarbonised zones in our urban areas, focusing on pedestrianisation, cycling facilities, and use of renewable energy across all transport modes. The Department of Climate, Energy and the Environment (DCEE) authorised funding for the recruitment of specialist staff to form the nucleus of climate action teams.

Urban Renewal and Rural Development

The Town Centre First (TCF) policy, which was published in February 2022, seeks to address the decline in the health of our towns and take measures to regenerate and revitalise them, in line with overarching objectives of the NPF.

Under the policy, local communities are enabled to prepare a TCF Plan for their town. TCF recognises that increasing housing in towns will

In addition, the DCEE funds Climate Action Regional Offices (CAROs). A Service Level Agreement totaling €12 million in funding was

be a critical part of their regeneration - providing homes for people close to local services and amenities and unlocking the potential of vacant and underutilised buildings and sites. Twenty-six TCF Plans have been completed nationally as demonstrators of the TCF approach.

more remote rural areas where population levels may be low or declining, on the other.

The objective is aligned with the established planning approach in Ireland whereby considerations of social or economic need to live in a rural area, may be applied by planning authorities in rural areas that are under urban influence in order to prevent urban-generated sprawl. Social need arises where a person is an intrinsic part of a rural community, while economic need arises where a person works full or parttime in the rural area. In more remote rural areas, NPO19 allows housing to be facilitated subject to normal planning and environmental considerations and having regard to the viability of smaller towns and rural settlements.

Additional plans are being developed for other towns and villages in the future, based on emerging best practice from the pilot phase. To drive the delivery of TCF, several supports have been established including Town Centre First Offices, Town Regeneration Officers (TROs), the alignment of various funding streams related to town regeneration and investment and the jointly funded TCF National Office.

The TCF approach can play a significant role in supporting the objectives of both the RSES and overall National Planning Policy related to sustainable development, compact growth and strategic town and urban regeneration.

Aligned to this are Rural Housing Guidelines, which were last issued in 2005 as Ministerial Guidelines under Section 28 of the Planning and Development Act 2000. These Guidelines provide that people who are an intrinsic part of the rural community, on the basis of an economic or social need, should be facilitated in all rural areas. In areas under strong urban influence, the policy is to direct urban generated housing into cities, towns and villages. In a rural area suffering persistent and substantial population decline the policy is to accommodate anyone wishing to build a house, subject to normal planning and environmental considerations.

The Strategy can also support the objectives of Our Rural Future: Rural Development Policy 2021- 2025. The vision for this policy is a thriving rural Ireland which is integral to national economic, social, cultural and environmental wellbeing and development. An efficient and accessible transport system can support an Ireland which recognises the centrality of people (rather than cars), the importance of vibrant and lived-in rural places, and the potential to create quality jobs and sustain our shared environment.

The NPF objectives together with the 2005 Guidelines, enable planning authorities to prepare and adopt local development plan policies for one off housing in rural areas.

The NPF also supports the sustainable development of rural areas and recognises the need to ensure that they continue to be viable places in which to live, work and invest. Importantly, it also recognises that there are different types of rural area, noting in particular, that in areas close to cities and large towns, ‘overspill’ development pressure for housing can affect rural character and environmental quality. Equally, in more remote rural areas, the challenges faced by communities when population is aging or in decline are recognised, as is the impact this has on community and social facilities and local infrastructure.

The alignment and mutually reinforcing nature of the objectives of this Strategy and those in the TCF and Rural Development policies is clear. There is also scope for reviewing the potential for greater integration of the structures in place to deliver across all three policy areas to optimise their impact and bring greater efficiencies in terms of the resources allocated towards delivery. A number of the expert sub-groups established to inform this Strategy considered the potential for leveraging these policies to advance demand management objectives (see related sub-group report) and some key recommendations in this regard are set out in Chapter 10.

National Policy Objective (NPO) 19 of the NPF relates specifically to rural housing. This objective aims to ensure that a policy distinction is made between rural areas experiencing development pressure from urban areas, on the one hand, and

### Parking Measures

*Pages 50–50*

Parking Measures

The Climate Change Advisory Council (CCAC) has also advocated for interventions in relation to parking. In line with Five Cities Demand Management Study, the Council in its 2023 Annual Review recommends parking restraints alongside the implementation of Part 18B of the Finance Act (No. 2) of 2008 (Parking Levy in Urban Areas) in the major urban areas of Cork, Dublin, Galway, Limerick and Waterford.

The provision of car parking – location, availability, and cost - has a considerable role to play in influencing people’s travel decisions. Parking measures feature strongly in the NTA’s modelling for the Climate Action Plan, including the removal of free workplace parking spaces, a 400% increase from 2016 parking charges and a minimum charge of €5/hr introduced in all urban areas - by 2030. This demonstrates the significant potential that parking measures are modelled to have on travel behaviour.

The Commission on Taxation and Welfare went a step further and recommended the introduction of an additional duty on non-residential car parking, both public and private - not limited to employerprovided car parking. Aside from levies and duties, alternative mechanisms to support less car-based commuting could include income tax changes and addressing subsidies and incentives to workplace car parking.

As a result, CAPs 2023 to 2025 include a range of measures aimed at reducing parking provision in public places and in residential developments. They reference the Public Sector Climate Action Mandate, which commits to phasing out the use of parking at public sector buildings ‘that have access to a range of public transport services and active/shared mobility options for the majority of staff/visitors while providing that sufficient accessible parking is maintained for those with physical mobility issues’. In addition, through the lens of enhanced spatial and land use planning and the need to enable a systems change, CAP23 highlighted the importance of managing parking from the perspective of availability, form and price. It advocated for public authorities to adopt market pricing of car parking to avoid subsidising car use as well as a reduction of on-street car parking spaces where it complements measures to prioritise active travel and public transport and to improve the public realm. Powers related to public parking control rests with Local Authorities.

In its 2024 Review, the CCAC also urged rapid availability of the national parking data inventory, proposed in this Strategy, and a review of existing legislation to support a reduction in car parking use where sustainable travel alternatives are available.

Other policies and programmes have a direct impact on parking – notably the Department of Transport’s Active Travel Programme and the rollout of major public transport infrastructure such as BusConnects – which will involve the removal of a significant amount of on-street parking. Pilot studies are underway on kerbside management, including a Pathfinder project in Dublin City, which aims to develop more dynamic solutions to the management of street space including parking, loading bays etc. The CCAC, in its 2025 Review, state that the reallocation of road space is critical to support sustainable modes of transport and has also been demonstrated to contribute positively to local economies and wellbeing. However, it notes that this reallocation, which oftentimes involve a reduction in space for on-street parking or car lanes in favour of pedestrianisation, dedicated cycling infrastructure and public transport, can face initial opposition, requiring significant stakeholder engagement.

CAP23 included a specific action to review Planning Guidelines to ensure that a graduated and performance-based approach is applied to the quantum and form of car parking, having regard to the proximity of urban centres and public transport services. To this end, new Sustainable Residential Development and Compact Settlements Guidelines (SRDCSGs) for Planning Authorities were published in 2024.

These new guidelines include maximum parking rates (Specific Planning Policy Requirement 3) based on recommending a graduated approach to car parking for residential development – with a further requirement for car parking ratios to be minimised, reduced, or eliminated at locations that have good access to urban services and to public transport.

Finally, the CCAC also recommended that the local authority of each city should introduce park and ride schemes along major arterial roads (by 1 January 2030), as well as deliver (by 1 January 2030) a plan to reduce the number of public city centre parking spaces. All of the aforementioned

### Fiscal Policies

*Pages 51–51*

recommendations were considered as part of the development of this Strategy and were discussed by a number of the expert sub-groups to inform the recommendations in Chapter 10.

That said, in recent years, the tax system, across multiple tax heads (VRT, motor tax, Benefit in Kind, capital allowances, carbon tax) has been leveraged to dis-incentivise the use of high emission fuels and to influence the purchase of more fuel-efficient private cars including EVs.

From a national policy perspective, parking is not managed centrally or nationally and does not fall within any one Government Department’s remit. It is a locally managed service, delivered by a range of different agents in different ways. Parking can be provided by public authorities, by private companies on behalf of public authorities or by commercial entities exclusively. Within the urban realm, parking can be provided on the street and off the street (adjacent to shops or within multi-storeys), at residential and commercial developments, at workplaces and transport hubs e.g., train stations and airports - to name but a few. Significant parking is also available at large out-of-town retail parks, industrial and enterprise zones, and at large tourist sites.

In addition to the above, other fiscal measures that can improve network performance, provide investment for transport enhancements, or help to manage peak-period congestion, where needed, are tolls, road user charging, parking charges, low emissions or clean air zones, mileage allowances, congestion charging, workplace parking levies, and incentives for sustainable business travel. While this is a non-exhaustive list, such measures, along with excise and carbon taxes, are aligned to the user and polluter pays model and can assist in meeting various policy and operational objectives while supporting the ongoing economic and fiscal sustainability of the State. These measures have proven very effective in optimising network efficiency and are among some of the case studies set out in Appendix 1.

A critical issue in designing a credible policy in relation to parking is the lack of data. Secondly, there is no single mechanism that can be deployed to effect a large-scale change in the quantum of available parking nationally so developing a consistent and coherent approach in a fragmented space is challenging. For these reasons, a multifaceted approach is required which should be deployed at various levels, based on a number of key principles as outlined in CAP23. Informed by the sub-group discussions and the work done already in this space, a number of impactful recommendations are made in Chapter 10, notably Recommendations 3, 5, and 30, to begin to chart a significant change in parking policies at all levels.

As highlighted by the analysis done by the Parliamentary Budget Office11 and the Fiscal Advisory Council12, some aspects of climate policies (e.g., transition to EVs) will reduce Exchequer revenues. As the Tax Strategy Group (TSG 18/07) noted – ‘The structural shift to lower CO2 emitting vehicles, and in particular any significant take up in Electric Vehicles (EVs), will severely challenge the revenue base from vehicles across fuel excise duties, motor-tax and VRT’13.

As cited later, the Commission on Taxation and Welfare also examined these issues and made a number of recommendations in relation to transport in the context of mutually achieving climate goals and long-term financial sustainability. For the purposes of this Strategy, there is a need to examine fiscal measures in the round from the perspective of their proven capacity to drive behavioural change, taking account of the impacts on Exchequer revenues over the medium to longer term.

Fiscal Policies

Traditionally, fiscal measures relating to transport were used to raise revenue for the Exchequer.

While the revenue raised was not ring-fenced for transport per se, the taxes and charges applied to private car use largely reflects the under recognised costs to the State of providing and maintaining related infrastructure, particularly the improvement and maintenance of roads and streets, and related services such as lighting and signage.

While most revenue from transport-related taxation is not hypothecated, it is intended that revenue from carbon taxes will be ring-fenced for just transition and other climate initiatives.

Typically, vehicle taxes are levied on ownership while fuel excise and carbon tax are based on usage.

The potential for fiscal measures to be deployed in a demand management context is explored

### Educational Sector

*Pages 53–54*

Any proposals of a fiscal nature that may emerge from these recommendations in the future will be informed by social, economic and distributional analysis and will ultimately be the subject of Government decision.

of Education and Youth (DEY), is transporting over 181,000 children, including over 24,400 children with special educational needs, to and from primary and post-primary schools every day. This also includes for school transport scheme services which are being provided in the current school year to over 5,800 children who have arrived in Ireland from Ukraine. Under the Scheme, 100 million sustainable kilometres are travelled per year – the equivalent to 2,500 journeys around the world. The total cost of the scheme in 2025 was €537m.

Educational Sector

Except for 2020, due mainly to the impact of the pandemic, there has been an ever-increasing demand for the service. A Review of the School Transport Scheme ‘School Transport 2030’, which was recently completed, marks the largest review of the School Transport Scheme since it was established in 1967. The review encompassed the School Transport Scheme for Children with Special Educational Needs as well as the Primary and Post-Primary School Transport Schemes. It considered how each element of the schemes currently operate, having regard to eligibility criteria, trends, costs, availability of drivers, and overall effectiveness in meeting key objectives.

Education is a significant driver of transport demand in Ireland. It also presents a significant opportunity to effect behavioural change and establish sustainable travel habits among young people from Early Learning and Care to third level. The school journey also presents an ideal way for children to take part in regular physical activity, to interact with their peers, and to develop good road sense as pedestrians and cyclists. Active modes also help to improve children’s alertness.

However, as things stand, the provision of educational services generate a significant number of trips across the country every day – with journeys to education accounting for 19% of daily trips (NHTS: 2024). One of the key metrics in CAP23 for delivering abatement in the transport sector is to shift 30% of all Escort to Education car journeys to more sustainable modes (public transport or active travel, either accompanied or unaccompanied).

The review was conducted for the purpose of examining the broader effectiveness and sustainability of the Scheme, ensuring that it serves the students and their families adequately. The review built upon commitments on school transport within the Programme for Government, including examining options to reduce car journeys and assessing how the School Transport Scheme can work in liaison with the Safe Routes to Schools Programme. The review has also examined the potential for integrating different strands of the scheme and a more co-ordinated approach with other Government Departments that also use transport services.

The Green-Schools Travel Programme was rolled out nationally in September 2008 and has worked, or is working with schools

Safe Travel to School: A Child’s Perspective

Key enablers of sustainable mobility at school level are the School Transport Scheme, the Green Schools Programme (specifically the Travel theme), the Safe Routes to School Programme and the National Strategy on Education for Sustainable Development – ESD to 2030. In the current year, the School Transport Scheme, which is operated by Bus Éireann (BÉ) on behalf of the Department

around the country to promote sustainable travel to school. 336 schools participated in the Green Schools Travel programme in 2025. Cycle parking was provided at 44 schools in 2025, and scooter parking was provided at 16 schools. The Safe Routes to School Programme (SRTS), which was launched in March 2021, is supported by the NTA and the Departments of Education and Youth. The SRTS is designed to encourage pupils and students in primary and post-primary schools to walk and cycle. It has three aims:

An Mhodhscoil after the School Street was introduced.

The Guidance also clarifies the difference between School Streets and School Zones and includes consultation and design recommendations.

ᴏ To accelerate the delivery of walking/scooting and cycling infrastructure on key access routes to schools.

The SRTS Programme, combined with investment in active travel infrastructure, has the potential to embed healthy travel behaviours at an early age. Consequently, over the period 2020 – 2024, significant progress has been made on delivering active travel infrastructure, including 664 kilometres of walking infrastructure, 399 kilometres of cycling infrastructure and 291 kilometres of greenways. Approximately 200km of additional walking and cycling infrastructure were added to the network in 2025 through the NTA’s Active and Sustainable Transport Investment Programme and TII’s National Greenways Programme.

ᴏ To provide “front of school” treatments which will enhance access to school grounds.

ᴏ To expand the amount of bike parking available at schools.

The aforementioned Active Travel Programme also funds projects under the SRTS Programme. Schools which applied to the Programme in 2021 are being selected in tranches for inclusion in the Programme. Over 900 schools applied, and following the announcement of Round 4 in December 2025, over 500 of these schools are now active in the Programme including 156 which have had projects completed. School Streets is also part of the SRTS – an initiative that restricts general traffic outside schools during drop-off and pick-up times. In the right location, School Streets can help address a range of issues associated with vehicle congestion at the school gate.

As well as the beneficial impacts of sustainable mobility on school-going children and third level students, the education sector, in conjunction with the local planning authorities, has a considerable role to play in reducing unsustainable transport demand through the provision of effective mobility management plans for schools and third level institutions and building programmes that locate or develop existing schools and institutions in a way that restrains, not accelerates transport demand, such as the development of student specific accommodation – thus negating the need for travel.

In September 2025, the NTA published School Streets Guidance which outlines how to plan, trial, and implement School Streets, with case studies from several Irish local authorities. The following shows the before and after effects of a School Streets initiative at An Mhodhscoil, Limerick City.

### Business and Enterprise

*Pages 55–56*

The Generators of Demand (People) subgroup focused on a wide range of potential measures in the education sphere that could impact positively. These are summarised in Chapter 9 and amplified in Chapter 10’s set of recommendations.

(CSRD) are placing much greater focus on climate and environmental decisions by businesses in Ireland and internationally. Transport emissions are captured directly and indirectly and are becoming an increasing focus of attention by large enterprises, particularly in terms of Scope 3 emissions (i.e., emissions a firm is responsible for but which happen outside of its walls and are controlled by other parties up and down the value chain). In terms of Scope 1 emissions, many companies in Ireland are already making significant strides to reduce carbon footprint from their own transport fleets (e.g., An Post, DPD etc).

A second National Strategy for Education for Sustainable Development – ESD to 2030– was published in June 2022 and aims to ensure that all learners have the knowledge and skills needed to promote sustainable development. ESD to 2030 is co-sponsored by the Department of Education and Youth, Department of Further and Higher Education, Research, Innovation and Science and Department of Children, Disability and Equality and so covers education at all levels and beyond into the non-formal youth education sector.

It is clear that Ireland’s sustainability credentials are increasingly important in attracting foreign direct investment (FDI) and talent into the country. This represents a major competitive opportunity for Ireland in leading the way on decarbonising transport in particular across the enterprise sector. In the National Competitiveness and Productivity Council’s (NCPC) 2023 Scorecard14, it was identified that Ireland lags behind competitor countries in terms of environmental sustainability. It also noted that the slow delivery of infrastructure and the backlogs in the planning system are key challenges for Ireland across several sectors.

The Department of Education and Youth has published a Sustainability Toolkit to support schools adopting a Sustainability Policy Statement in line with the School Sector Climate Action Mandate. The toolkit comprises a self-assessment audit, a guidelines document and a template policy statement. The audit asks schools to rate itself in terms of 1) promoting sustainable transport including walking and cycling to school and b) engagement with the Safe Routes to School programme. The guidelines document includes information about active travel and the Safe Routes to School programme.

As a result, and as outlined in the Government’s White Paper on Enterprise, 2022 – 203015, the integration of decarbonisation and net zero commitments has been identified as a priority. The delivery of public transport infrastructure has been an ongoing issue with delays in the planning system resulting in significant delays in the rollout of many planned projects such as BusConnects. That said, many planning related delays will be alleviated in the future with the introduction of statutory timelines for decisions in the new 2024 Planning Act to go alongside increased resourcing for An Coimisiún Pleanála and LAs.

A quarterly ESD to 2030 Newsletter issues to all schools and regularly contains articles and information for schools about sustainable transport initiatives and projects. ESD funding for schools has included projects in the field of sustainable transport e.g. bike libraries.

Business and Enterprise

This Strategy has a two-fold focus in respect of business, and enterprise. It identifies the role that related activities have in generating transport demand, including the contribution they can make to reducing this demand, but also identifies the inherent economic opportunities in this regard - helping these sectors to contribute, as required, to the national vision of a net zero economy by 2050. The advent of regulatory levers notably the Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive

The planning system is not the only delivery issue, however. Delays can arise due to a range of factors e.g., the cyclical nature of investment, multiple large-scale projects entering the system at the same time, capacity to deliver once approved, time taken to comply with the Infrastructure Guidelines, and that a level of prioritisation will be required in the future to

enable project delivery under the NDP given wider systems capacity issues, as highlighted in the ESRI’s NDP Report16.

Generators of Demand (Freight) provide some useful examples of how adjustments to the logistics model can make a significant impact on congestion and carbon emissions, as well as positively impacting the bottom-line.

Such issues are often identified by the Department of Enterprise, Tourism and Employment who oversees the implementation of nine Regional Enterprise Plans (REPs). These Plans facilitate regional stakeholders to work collaboratively to identify growth opportunities, recognise vulnerabilities, and in response strengthen the regional enterprise ecosystem.These are bottom-up plans, developed by regional stakeholders including the Local Authorities, the enterprise agencies, local enterprise offices, regional skills forum and education and training institutes in each region. The Plans focus on undertaking collaborative initiatives that can help strengthen the regional enterprise ecosystem thereby realising enterprise growth and supporting job creation in each of the nine regions across Ireland. They are complementary to the ongoing work of the various bodies such as the IDA, Enterprise Ireland, Local Authorities, Local Enterprise Offices, Regional Assemblies, and could be leveraged to support the principles of this Strategy and the NPF.

Work-related journeys currently account for approximately 25% of all travel in Ireland. During the Covid Pandemic, working from home became the norm for large cohorts of workers and this had a significant impact on travel demand. While there has been a gradual return to the office, employers and employees have experienced the benefits of a blended approach. The NTA modelling for the Climate Action Plan assumes a continuation of this blended approach with white collar commute trip rates reduced by 25% for example. Government has put in place various supports for remote working, with the public sector itself leading by example.

Among the main actions under the Making Remote Work: National Remote Work Strategy, which was published by Department of Enterprise, Tourism and Employment (DETE) in 2021, is mandating that home and remote work should be the norm for 20 percent of public sector employment. Making Remote Work reflects how work can occur in lots of places - at home, at a hub, or on the move. A key action for the development agencies under the strategy is to promote remote work amongst businesses. This includes raising awareness of remote work training and advising on the skills interventions required for the successful adoption of remote work. It also commits to mapping and investing in a network of remote working hubs across Ireland.

Business and enterprise are a significant generator of vehicle use. In 2018, manufacturing alone was directly responsible for 227,052 jobs in Ireland while also indirectly supporting 182,000 additional jobs. Due to their nature, many manufacturing jobs are located in areas where public transport is not readily available which means people use their car to travel to work. Additionally, the manufacturing sector has a high export orientation which adds to freight kilometres travelled.

Through Our Rural Future, Ireland’s rural development policy, the Department of Rural and Community Development and the Gaeltacht (DRCDG) has led on the delivery of a national network of remote working hubs and launched a booking platform in 2021 - https://connectedhubs. ie/ - which provides information on the location of, and facilities available at remote working hubs throughout Ireland. As part of the strategic development of Ireland’s hub sector, DRCDG in conjunction with DETE are leading on the formulation of a National Hub Strategy. As part of this Strategy, DETE will lead on the enterprise pillar while DRCDG will be the lead department for coworking in hubs and digital services at Broadband Connection Points.

The retail sector is also a significant generator of travel demand, both in terms of the location of retail outlets – particularly large retail centres – and also in terms of product delivery to and from retail outlets. Planning guidelines play a key role in the decisions around retail locations. Stronger alignment of decisions with the NPF objectives for compact growth in particular is key. There are also significant opportunities for rationalising and decarbonising deliveries including home deliveries, with many companies already seizing the opportunity (e.g. An Post). A key driver of congestion in urban areas are goods delivery requirements set by traditional logistics models. Appendix 1 and sub-group reports (notably

Trends in remote working will need to be closely monitored to fully appreciate the level of potential it could have – or otherwise – in reducing transport demand as reflected in research insights from the ESRI’s NDP Report (2024).

vehicles are also ICE vehicles meaning these journeys are contributing to our carbon emissions. In addition to car rental, approximately 600,000 holidaymakers use private coach hire as part of a package or group holiday. Government is already working with businesses to assist them in working to achieve more sustainable business practices. For example, Fáilte Ireland provided tourism businesses with the Carbon Calculator for Businesses19 while ZEVI are working with the car rental industry to support the move to zero-emissions vehicles, as well as working on destination charging at major tourist attractions.

Tourism

Tourism is also a significant contributor to the Irish economy in terms of revenue raised by tourists visiting Ireland but also in terms of the number of jobs it creates in Ireland. In 2019, the tourism sector generated approximately €9.5 billion in revenue17 and supported an estimated 284,800 full-time equivalent jobs18. That said, tourism is a significant generator of trips. In economic terms, tourism remains hugely important and provides a significant contribution to the Irish Exchequer. In 2024, there were 6.6 million overseas visitors to Ireland, resulting in €6 billion in export revenue, while the total value of domestic overnight trips by residents in Ireland was estimated at €3.6 billion. It is estimated that Ireland’s tourism sector accounts for around 3.7% of all tax revenue.

As well as inbound tourists, those that are outbound also generate significant travel activity, particularly when travelling from large transport hubs like Dublin Airport and Dublin Port. For example, Dublin Airport facilitated more than 36.4 million passengers in 202520, split relatively evenly between departures and arrivals. It is critical that appropriate steps are taken to encourage sustainable travel choices, and discourage private car use, among all the millions of people travelling to and from such hubs. In 2024, almost 41 million passengers were collectively handled by the airports of Dublin, Cork, Shannon, Kerry, and Knock21.

The Tourism Policy Statement contains a section dedicated to reducing emissions in transport, and notes that increasing the use of public and active transport by tourists is critical to achieving decarbonisation in the sector. It advises that larger tourism attractions will examine the feasibility of using sustainable transport modes and that tourism agencies will explore the development of an online tool to enable decarbonised touring. Among the measures cited as helping to reduce transport emissions are orientation plans for tourists, and the integration of transport and access measures into all Destination Experience Development Plans (DEDPs).

The National Tourism Policy Statement, A New Era for Irish Tourism, sets out a clear strategic direction for the sector to 2031. The policy prioritises increasing visitor numbers, boosting revenue, and supporting employment. It reinforces tourism’s role in balanced regional development, focusing on less mature areas and unlocking rural potential through targeted investment in tourism infrastructure. It promotes sustainable transport for visitors, encouraging public transport, active travel, and low-carbon options while ensuring convenient access to key attractions.

Transport is one of the first experiences tourists will have when visiting Dublin, highlighting the importance of direct links to Dublin City Centre. An easy to use, attractive public transport system is important in influencing how visitors opt to travel and see Ireland. While the majority of tourists staying in Dublin use public transport for their stay, 33% of visitors to Ireland use the car option if travelling beyond Dublin. Most rented

Similarly, large sporting events, festivals and conferences are a major generator of tourism activity and revenue but also travel demand, some of which can be managed with carbon and other impacts mitigated with proper planning, tools and guidance.

Sports Sector

Dublin Bus Independent Travel Support

Nearly half of adults in Ireland regularly participate in a sport and it is a goal of the National Sports Policy 2018-2027 to increase participation further. Also, over half of the population are regularly involved socially in sport through volunteering, club membership and/or attendance. Naturally, this level of participation does give rise to significant transport activity. Many national governing bodies, as part of their responsibilities around sustainability, are already taking steps to encourage employees, players, and fans to take more sustainable and active journeys. For example, the GAA’s Green Club Programme, which was established in December 2022, aims to promote sustainability awareness and to support Gaelic Games clubs and communities in taking simple and effective sustainability action in their grounds and activities. The Programme is structured around a range of themes including Travel & Transport. Other initiatives include communications campaigns like the ‘Munster March’. This campaign, which was launched at the end of 2023, encouraged Munster supporters of all ages to walk the one mile from Limerick City Centre to Thomond Park to help raise awareness of the club’s commitment to environmental sustainability and climate action.

can fully participate in public and community life, while inclusion means that everyone has the opportunity to realise that accessibility.

The NPF also acknowledges that ‘liveability’ or quality of life is key to attracting people to live within compact cities, towns and villages.

In this regard ‘place-making’ is central to the implementation of both the TCF policy and the Government’s new housing action plan Delivering Homes, Building Communities, 2025. For places to be successful, they need to be well connected, ensuring all users can move about with ease by incorporating the principles of Universal Design22 and providing attractive spaces in which people can meet, shop, work and exercise.

Universal Design is the design and composition of an environment so that it can be accessed, understood and used to the greatest extent possible by all people, regardless of their age, size or disability. This includes public places in the built environment such as buildings, streets or spaces that the public have access to; products and services provided in those places; and systems that are available including information and communications technology (ICT). The Centre for Excellence in Universal Design is a state funded centre dedicated to promoting Universal Design with a wide range of design guidance information available on their website.

Sports Action Plan 2024 - 2027 acknowledges that the sport sector must progressively move ever closer towards carbon neutrality in the years ahead. As such, it sets out a range of actions that will support and guide the sport sector to achieving these ambitious goals. The aim of this Strategy is to build on best practice across this sector and to replicate learnings to encourage a consistent message from this highly influential sector.

The implementation of these principles is a critical success factor for sustainable mobility, creating ‘people centered’ rather than ‘car-centered’ communities.

Accessibility and Inclusion

It is anticipated that this Government’s new Towns and Cities Fund, which is replacing the Urban Regeneration Development Fund (URDF),
