The quality of the journey undertaken is also important. Street environments that feel safe and comfortable and are free of impediments are critical to promoting increased pedestrian and cyclist activity, as well as increasing accessibility for disabled people, particularly those with visual impairments or sensory issues. DMURS highlights how in recent decades the prioritisation of private vehicle movement in most Irish towns has resulted in a legacy of street environments that are hostile to pedestrians, and make journeys challenging for older people, children, and women in particular.

The National Human Rights Strategy for Disabled People 2025-2030 is the most recent framework for policy and action to address the needs of people with disabilities and the key vehicle for progressing the implementation of the United Nations Convention on The Rights of Persons with Disabilities (UNCRPD). The Programme for Government 2025 placed a strong emphasis on launching this new National Disability Strategy, which outlines a forward-looking vision through to 2030. It embraces a whole-of-Government approach to advancing the implementation of the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities. Responsibility for delivering the Strategy’s commitments is shared across Government Departments and State Agencies, each accountable for actions within their remit. These commitments are structured around five central Pillars: Inclusive Learning and Education, Employment, Independent Living and Active Participation in Society, Wellbeing and Health, and Transport and Mobility.

The Department of Transport has three actions in the Domestic, Sexual and Gender Based Violence (DSGBV) Implementation Plan 2024 reflecting those actions of relevance in the SMP.

This Strategy looks to reinforce accessibility and inclusion principles within the existing transport system and service environment.

Safety is also a key determinant of whether or not people use public transportation. The study ‘Travelling in a Woman’s Shoes’ was designed to investigate the needs and travel behaviours of women. Transport is often seen as gender neutral:, however a growing body of international research is showing that this is often not the case. Women rely heavily on their cars. Women tend to have more caring responsibilities and are more impacted by safety issues. As such, men and women can have different needs, constraints, and expectations for using transport. This Strategy has been considered through a gender lens, ensuring recommendations have taken all genders into account from a social inclusion and accessibility perspective.

Informed by the UNCRPD and national policies on disability, the Design Manual for Urban Roads and Streets (DMURS) requires that new streets networks be designed to be permeable and legible, in particular for sustainable modes. Guidance on improving accessibility, and retrofitting existing environments, is also assisted through the NTA’s Permeability Best Practice Guide and Walkability Toolkit and through the Infrastructure Equality Guidance, which are intended for use on all active travel schemes funded by the NTA. This latter set of guidance, which was published in May 2024, presents a number of common design issues to be aware of when designing or upgrading an active travel scheme and outlines the different guidance

Older persons and particularly older women can face additional barriers in accessing suitable transport, with the ‘Reframing Ageing’ study published by Age Action identifying that nearly 50% of women aged 65+ and 28% of men aged 65+ in rural areas report ‘unmet’ transport needs.

This can have compounding health impacts and lead to social and political exclusion. The inclusivity of our transport system for older persons is considered within the Strategy and its Implementation Plan.

### Health-Related Policies

*Pages 60–61*

Furthermore, ethnicity and religion can play a role in people’s perceived safety on public transport23, particularly those with concerns related to hate crimes. For religious people who have a marked religious identity through clothing there is an increased risk of harassment or discrimination24. Compared to Catholics, members of minority religions in Ireland report somewhat higher discrimination rates in the workplace and in public and private services. Research interviews with Muslim men and women in Dublin outlined the discriminatory practices they felt they experienced from transport staff as a result of religious and racial profiling, which took the form of poor or no service provision25. Equally, members of the travelling community can have greater barriers to accessing sustainable transport alternatives, especially if traveller-specific accommodation is located in peripheral locations with unsafe active travel connectivity and poor public transport services from sites to local towns. Refugee centres and sites are often hard to reach due to more limited transport options to these areas (Dublin-centricity) with asylum seekers needing to travel great distances to central places to reach services and appointments. Ensuring that our public transport system is accessible, inclusive and safe is of vital importance to the success of the Strategy and ensuring the services provided are fully aligned to the core values of the Public Sector Duty.

While pollutants originate from several sources, the transport sector accounts for more than 35% of nitrogen oxides (NOx) and 10% of fine particulate matter (PM2.5). Sources of NOx include the burning of fossil fuel in vehicle engines and by stationary combustion sources, such as industry and home heating. In towns and cities, where people are often close to traffic, vehicles are responsible for a significant portion of localised NOx levels. Vehicle engines also emit PM2.5 directly, and brake and tyre wear also contribute to levels of PM10 in the air. While new vehicles emit significantly less air pollutants than older ones, rising traffic levels also mean that where there are high levels of congestion, or where it is difficult for transport derived air pollutants to disperse (such as in heavily built-up city streets), it is becoming increasingly challenging to maintain high standards of air quality for people nearby.

It was estimated that the external health costs attributable to nitrogen dioxide and particulate matter in Dublin was €80 million in 201526.

In 2023, to address the environmental and public health issues associated with air pollution, a new Clean Air Strategy for Ireland was published by the Department of Climate, Energy and the Environment. The Clean Air Strategy seeks to enhance and protect the quality of air that we breathe through a comprehensive suite of cross Government policies and measures that targets all sources of air pollution, including those from the transport, agriculture and residential sectors. Amongst its key commitments is an ambition to work towards World Health Organisation (WHO) target values by 2040. The WHO guideline values are set for the protection of health and are generally stricter than the comparable EU standards.

Health-Related Policies

An efficient and sustainable transport system can have positive impacts on our health in several different ways.

Air pollution, including pollution caused by vehicles, is known to have adverse impacts on human health. The World Health Organisation (WHO) has called air pollution the greatest environmental risk to human health. In Ireland, it is estimated (in 2025) that 1,700 annual deaths could be attributed to the impacts of air pollution as well as causing, or exacerbating the effects of, illnesses such as stroke, heart disease, lung disease, and lung cancer. The World Health Organization (WHO) also provides evidence of links between air pollution and type 2 diabetes, obesity, systemic inflammation, Alzheimer’s disease and dementia.

While existing policy measures will go a long way towards reducing transport’s contribution to these pollutants, the key role that demand management can play in addressing air pollution issues is recognised in the Clean Air Strategy.

Physical inactivity is also a major public health issue, with estimates of circa €1.5 billion annually27 being attributed to incurred costs for our health system. Ireland’s National Physical

Activity Framework 2024-2040, which sets out a clear ambition to position Ireland as Europe’s most physically active nation by 2040. The Framework concentrates improvement across eight priority areas including Active Travel and Active Urban Design.

In Dublin, the reimagining of Blackrock Village saw a 25% increase in available public space which resulted in a 40% reduction of car trips on Blackrock Main Street, a dramatic increase in bicycle use, increased revenue for local business, and a significant improvement in the ‘health’ of the street with better air quality, safer streets, and a more relaxed overall atmosphere.

A more physically active Irish society is one that is imbued with a physical activity culture where choices to be physically active are facilitated and enabled by design across the range of policy areas which interact with daily life.

As well as the beneficial health impacts of switching to a low carbon transport system, the health sector has a considerable role to play in reducing transport demand through the provision of online and digital services, effective mobility management plans for reducing traffic to existing hospital and health centres and through building programmes that locate new services with the objectives of this Strategy in mind.

While our adult population is one of the more active in Europe28, only 15% of Irish children meet the WHO recommended physical activity guidelines. Furthermore, the number of children actively travelling to school has declined since 2018. Achieving a behavioural shift towards more active forms of travel where possible, particularly for school children, will also have the benefit of improving societal health and easing the burden on our healthcare system over the long-term.

The close alignment of health benefits and demand management/supply side measures were discussed across many of the expert subgroups.

A 2025 position paper by the Climate and Health Alliance entitled ‘Active Travel: The Magic Pill’ explains that ‘active travel gets us moving by embedding physical activity into daily life in a way that recreational or fitness-focused physical activity does not’. To help quantify the scale of benefits that can be attributed to active travel, the report outlined how walking, wheeling and cycling in the Dublin Metropolitan Area saves the HSE €64.5 million, equivalent to the cost of over 1,170,000 GP appointments.

The Generators of Demand (People) sub-group in particular considered the range of potential measures that could impact positively from a demand management perspective. These are set out in their separate report, and synthesised in a number of key recommendations in Chapter 10 (notably Recommendations 13 to 16).

In summary, there are clearly a wide range of national policies and programmes already in place that can and do support the objectives of this Strategy. There is also potential to do more and embed the principles of demand management in the planning and decision making at national and local level. Certain sectors are significant generators of travel demand and there is now an opportunity through this Strategy to assess the scope for change and provide guidance in that regard. There is also significant well-being benefits to be achieved through a closer alignment of polices particularly on health, accessibility and inclusion, which can be captured in the evaluation framework described in Chapter 12.

While the Covid-19 Pandemic was an extremely difficult time for all, some positives did arise from that period. In an effort to allow businesses to reopen and for people to meet in a safe manner, towns and cities all over Europe began giving back public space to pedestrians and cyclists. The net result was making our town and city streets more people friendly places, with increased footfall to our high streets and greater levels of active travel.

Mobility is a key part of healthy placemaking, and towns and cities where walking and cycling is prioritised can bring major health, environmental and economic benefits.

The specific potential to advance the objectives of this Strategy through these policies are explored in more detail under subsequent chapters and in the sub-group reports, with specific recommendations identified in Chapter 10.

The optimisation of road space across users such as pedestrians, cyclists, shared services and public transport (including taxis) can bring economic benefits through increased footfall on our high streets. Research has shown that cycle parking

## 6. Communications and Engagement

Effective consultation, engagement and communication initiatives will be key to increasing public and stakeholder support for the recommendations identified in this Strategy. Public opposition has been highlighted as a challenge across all of the sub- group discussions, and indeed in the context of the wider implementation of the Government’s Climate Action ambitions.

The Public Engagement Strategy also supports the ongoing work of the Department’s Communication Strategy and the flagship Your Journey Counts campaign. The objective of the ‘Your Journey Counts’ campaign, which was launched by the Department in August 2023, is to encourage more members of the public to make the switch to sustainable transport modes.

Your Journey Counts supports this behavioural shift by encouraging the public to take public transport and walk, wheel, or cycle whenever possible. Your Journey Counts is an integrated multi-platform communications campaign. The campaign appears across TV, radio, print (local and national), social media, digital and Out of Home advertising formats. Subsequent phases of the campaign focused on rural communities, employing tailored messaging focused on improvements in regional and local public transport links; socially native, short form content to appeal to younger demographics online; and reward messaging around passenger figures and emissions saved in public transport hubs and invehicle. A dedicated campaign landing page was developed in both English and Irish, to highlight investment in public transport, active travel and supports available to people in helping them make the switch.

Given the significant impact that communications and engagement can have in promoting transformational change, the Department of Transport has developed high-level strategic approaches to communications and engagement in relation to climate action and sustainable mobility, specifically in relation to efforts to reduce transport related carbon emissions.

On engagement specifically, and in line with the OECD’s recommendations in its 2022 Report, it is clear that a robust strategy around public engagement is needed to realise our climate policy objectives, to deliver the infrastructure and services required, and to achieve buy-in from the public around necessary behaviour changes and individual transport choices that will facilitate these aims. This was reflected in specific actions in CAP23 (TR/23/69(TF)) and the Government’s Sustainable Mobility Policy (SMP). In this context, the Public Engagement on Climate Action and Sustainable Mobility: Strategic Approach 2024-25 was published in May 2024. A successor strategy will be prepared in 2026.

The main objective of the Public Engagement Strategy is to build awareness and inspire ownership for action and behavioural change in individuals, private organisations, and public sector bodies, whilst also increasing support and acceptance for the delivery of critical sustainable mobility infrastructure. Supported by robust evidence and market research, the Strategy and its implementation will provide very useful insights in relation to demand management measures and will inform how best to engage communities in the design and implementation of these measures over the coming years. Building awareness around the services and incentives that already exist is also a clear goal, bringing attention to progress that has been - and will be - made in public transport as well as highlighting all the positives to help counteract established negative beliefs.

As part of post-campaign analysis, B&A Research conducted a nationally representative survey demonstrating that 82% of respondents said that the campaign increased their understanding of the need to use more environmentally friendly transport options; 74% stated that the campaign made them consider transport carbon emissions; 2 in 5 report that they walk, cycle, or use public transport more now than before the campaign and 1 in 3 asserts to have replaced private car travel as a result of the Your Journey Counts campaign.

As part of the overall suite of measures to support the objectives of the Strategy, the Department has considered the role of car advertising in inducing a car-centric mindset, as well as the effectiveness of Ireland’s application of car labels at points of sale, in accordance with relevant EU Directives.

The labels provide consumers with information regarding the CO2 emissions and fuel efficiency of new cars to facilitate informed decisions.

Communications and engagement-related actions are also expected to be a key feature of that Plan.

At the 2024 Forum29, a new Sustainable Mobility Academy (SMA) was launched. This platform brings together professionals from national and local governments, academia and the private sector, creating a space to connect, and share insights on sustainable mobility. Key features of the platform include:

In light of this, the Department of Transport is engaging fully and proactively with the EU’s reappraisal of the Car Labelling Directive 1999/94/EC. The review is expected to be complete in 2026, after which a new regulation may be proposed by the European Commission. Required amendments to the implementing regulations at a national level will be followed through as a priority. This work will be a critical element in implementing Recommendation 27 of this Strategy (raising consumer awareness on emissions from car use).

ᴏ A curated library of case studies from Ireland and abroad

ᴏ Insights into emerging best practices in transport and urban planning

ᴏ 90+ public-sector only resources, serving a centralised hub of information and tools to assist users in their mobility needs.

Finally, under the SMP, the Department of Transport hosts an annual National Sustainable Mobility Forum with a view to providing ‘a platform for collaborative engagement with national, regional and local stakeholders. The National Sustainable Mobility Forum has been held on an annual basis since 2023. The 2025 Forum, which took place across four parallel sessions in May 2025, brought together a wide range of stakeholders with a view to informing the development of the next SMP Action Plan, which will cover the period from 2026 to 2030.

In addition to the above, ongoing communication campaigns and engagement will be needed to increase awareness about the need for, and the benefits of, demand management measures at a local level in particular. For this reason, Recommendation 8 of this Strategy aims to provide a communications and engagement support for LAs to build on the work that is already underway at a national level.

## 7. Legislative Powers and Levers

Within the existing legal framework, the legislative powers and levers required to reduce travel demand are spread across multiple government departments and agencies, as well as Local Authorities. This Strategy recommends a review of legislation to ensure local authorities, agencies and relevant Ministers have sufficiently robust legal vires power to bring forward the most appropriate demand management measures.

The Strategy makes recommendations within the existing remit of the NTA, particularly in terms of guidance for other local authorities based on the scheme currently being developed for the GDA.

There are also other pieces of legislation currently being used to contribute to aspects of emissions reduction in the transport domain and, subject to strengthening in some cases, could be used to facilitate new or enhanced measures such as:

The overall power to reduce emissions from the transport sector stems from the Climate Action and Low Carbon Development Act 2021. As well as committing the State to achieving overall GHG emissions reduction of 51% by 2030, compared to 2018 levels, the Act provides further clarity on the role for Local Authorities in climate action including the development of five-year Local Authority Climate Action Plans.

ᴏ Low emissions zones/clean air zones (building on ‘Special Control Areas’ in Air Pollution Act 1987)

ᴏ Road space reallocation, general regulation and control of traffic including the control and regulation of parking vehicles (Road Traffic Act 1994), particularly Guidelines under Section 38(6) of the Road Traffic Act 1994

Section 71 of the Dublin Transport Authority Act 2008 provides the NTA with powers to pursue the implementation of certain demand management measures in the GDA. It defines demand management measures for the purpose of that section as being “measures which promote a reduction in the total amount of travel or reduced growth in the total amount of travel and includes measures to:

ᴏ Workplace parking levy (Finance Act 2008)

ᴏ Integrated land use and planning (Planning and Development Act)

Local authorities, as critical implementors for demand management measures, will also utilise their devolved powers under the Local Government Reform Act, 2014.

Chapter 10 sets out a number of recommendations which relate to reviewing and strengthening existing legislative powers to advance the objectives of this Strategy in line with the overall legal imperative set out under the Climate Action and Low Carbon Development Act 2021. Of particular relevance to the above is the following:

c. increase travel by public transport, bicycle or on foot as an alternative to the private car

other than by means of a congestion charge, and

Recommendation 17: Review relevant legislation to ensure powers are sufficient to bring forward demand management measures.

It also provides for the NTA to give a direction to a public transport authority in relation to the implementation of demand management measures and to issue guidelines to public transport authorities or to other public bodies in relation to the implementation of demand management measures. The NTA may also allocate moneys and make payments to public transport authorities or other persons in relation to demand management measures.

A key output from this recommendation, as detailed in the Implementation Plan (Appendix 3), is to develop a legislative programme that will identify the legal and regulatory steps needed to support a range of demand management measures and to seek approval to progress this work, through the standard legislative process, from Government. This will provide implementing bodies with legal certainty in the delivery of measures.

## 8. Research and Policy Analysis

### The Five Cities Demand Management Study

*Pages 69–69*

The Five Cities Demand Management Study

This Strategy has been informed by a wide range of research, and evidence-based policy analysis. The primary sources are itemised below highlighting relevant findings.

The Five Cities Demand Management Study, which was published in November 2021, arose from a commitment in the Climate Action Plan 2019. The rationale for undertaking the Study was to better understand generators of transport demand in the five cities of Dublin, Cork, Waterford, Limerick and Galway, and to encourage a greater shift to more sustainable and healthier forms of travel. The Study provides a focused and evidence-based approach to addressing the carbon, congestion and air quality challenges facing Irish cities – using insight from an extensive international best practice review, outputs from national and local stakeholder engagement as well as detailed qualitative and quantitative analysis.

International Transport Forum (ITF) Decarbonising Transport Initiative

The work of the sub-groups associated with this Strategy was guided, in some part, by the Decarbonising Transport initiative developed by the International Transport Forum (ITF) Decarbonisation and the Pricing of Road Transport. This initiative was developed with the specific purpose of providing decision makers with tools to select CO2 mitigation measures that deliver on their climate commitment and to help government and industry translate climate ambitions into actions. Specifically, it created a catalogue of effective CO2 mitigation measures known as the Transport Climate Action Directory. This Directory was used as a springboard for discussion at the sub-group meetings.

It is well known in the first instance that the high rate of car usage in cities results in many negative impacts.

Based on the above, the Study explored how best to:

In addition to the above, the ITF Outlook 2023 reinforced the need for a mix of policies to promote sustainable choices in different contexts. It asserted that demand for passenger and freight transport will continue to grow in the coming decades across all world regions, in all scenarios.

ᴏ Manage vehicular traffic congestion.

ᴏ Reduce GHG emissions from road traffic.

ᴏ Address air quality issues due to vehicular traffic emissions.

ᴏ Improve the quality of the urban environment.

Without appropriate policies, unmanaged growth could result in increased urban sprawl, longer average trip distances and a lack of any meaningful emission reductions.

The Study recommended three priority Tier 1 measures. These were:

1. 15 Minute Neighbourhoods.

This Strategy has been informed by the ITF Outlook, which encourages decision makers to:

2. Enhanced Delivery of the National Planning Framework (NPF).

ᴏ Take a long-term view of urban development and adopt integrated approaches to transport and land-use planning to avoid future sprawl in growing cities.

3. Public Parking Controls

ᴏ Adopt holistic sustainable urban transport plans that combine investment, pricing and access or space restrictions to encourage sustainable choices.

ᴏ Support multimodal and sustainable transport networks.

ᴏ Combine pricing measures in a coherent manner and allocate funds to sustainable modes.

### OECD Report

*Pages 70–70*

OECD Report

The Climate Change Advisory Council (CCAC) commissioned the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) to review Ireland’s transport system and to determine its capacity for achieving net zero by 2050. The associated report, ‘Redesigning Ireland’s Transport for Net Zero’, which was published in October 2022, indicated that Ireland was unlikely to achieve its ambitious climate goals without a systemic change in its transport system and substantially different patterns of travel behaviours than those observed today.

Among the key recommendations were:

ᴏ Redefining the goal of the transport system as sustainable accessibility,

ᴏ Prioritising the up-scale of policies with high potential to transform the car-dependent system,

ᴏ Redefining the electrification strategy to support the transition towards a sustainable transport system,

ᴏ Embracing a systemic approach to policy decision-making across government departments,

This Strategy reflects that change of focus and the evidence and findings of the OECD report have informed the deliberations of the expert sub-groups and in turn the recommendations in Chapter 10.

### Modelling for CAP

*Pages 71–72*

Modelling for CAP

In February 2021, the NTA and Department of Transport commissioned a detailed transport modelling and appraisal exercise to support the development of the Climate Action Plan (CAP21) and the emerging Climate Action Plan

tool available to the Department of Transport for estimating overall emissions impacts and the likely behavioural responses to potential interventions and policy measures at a national level.

Measures modelled in the analysis included certain policies aimed at reducing travel demand, a broad range of measures to enhance public transport services, delivery of expanded active travel and public transport infrastructure, as well as measures to increase the share of biofuels and electrification in the transport sector.

2023 (CAP23). The modelling and appraisal were undertaken over three phases and used purposebuilt modelling tools as well as the NTA’s Regional Modelling System (RMS). Underpinned by a National Demand Forecasting Model (NDFM) that accounts for projected demographic growth and spatial planning data, the RMS is the most robust

Measures modelled in the analysis were:

Some of the measures within CAP23 are within the scope of this Strategy but will require further policy design. There are also a range of potential measures that are considered as part of this Strategy which do not feature in the modelling assumptions but have strong potential to impact efforts on reducing vehicle kms. A recommendation arising from this Strategy is to consider how broader socio-economic impacts of various demand management measures can be determined alongside the behaviour impacts which the NTA framework can model, in a way that can better inform a wider cost/benefit analysis of demand management schemes.

ᴏ Increased working from home/public awareness campaigns;

ᴏ Car-free urban centres;

ᴏ Congestion charging;

ᴏ Increased fossil fuel cost;

ᴏ Parking constraints;

ᴏ Improved public transport service interchange through investment in programmes such as Bus Connects; and

ᴏ Reduced public transport fares through initiatives such as the 90-Minute Fare, Young Adult Card and the 20% discount on PSO services.

Noting that three years have elapsed since the last major update of this transport emissions modelling, and given the likely overshoot of the transport sectoral ceiling for Carbon Budget 1, the Department and the NTA are undertaking an updated transport modelling and appraisal exercise (the Corrective Actions Modelling Project) to revise and recalibrate the existing CAP23 modelling, and re-assess and strengthen the decarbonisation policy pathway for transport to 2030. This work will be accompanied by extensive stakeholder engagement and a socio-economic assessment of the proposed policy measures / pathways.

While the modelling assumptions and measures did not represent agreed policy measures, they outlined one representative pathway that could be pursued in order to reach the decarbonisation targets set by Government, which was provided as example measures to better communicate the level of change that is necessary to deliver a 50% reduction in transport emissions by 2030.

### Better Road User Charging Evaluation

*Pages 73–73*

Better Road User Charging Evaluation

The objective of this project is to develop clear policy pathways for future CAPs that would set Ireland on a trajectory to meet the combined emissions ceiling of carbon budget 1 and carbon budget 2 of 91 MtCO2eq.

The Better Road User Charging Evaluation (BRUCE) project was completed by TII. The project explored future options for user charging, and for operation and maintenance funding of the National Road Network in Ireland.

Commission on Taxation and Welfare Report

Currently, thirteen sections of the national motorway network (one third of the motorway network) are operated via public private partnerships (PPPs). Eight of the PPP scheme operate toll plazas and 5 receive availability payments from TII. In addition, TII collect tolls directly on the M50 and Dublin Tunnel Toll Points. Income collected from tolls covers the operational costs on these roads as well as partially offsetting the cost of operations and maintenance funding for the larger publicly operated national road network. The majority of the 8 PPP toll plaza franchises expire between 2033-2037, with the Limerick tunnel expiring in 2041 and the M3 in 2052. The 5 availability payment PPPs expire between 2040 to 2045.

The Commission on Taxation and Welfare (COTW) was established in April 2021 to consider how the taxation and welfare system can support economic activity and income redistribution, whilst promoting increased employment and prosperity in a resilient, inclusive and sustainable way and ensuring that there are sufficient resources available to meet the costs of public services and supports in the medium and longer term.

The Report made four recommendations with potential impacts for transport demand. The Commission recommended:

ᴏ A redesign of Motor Tax and Vehicle Registration Tax on commercial and noncommercial motor vehicles in the medium to long term, in a manner that is consistent with environmental objectives and ensures that tax revenues from motor vehicles are maintained.

As the national car fleet transitions to EVs, with implications for revenue from motor tax, VRT, BIK, fuel excise, carbon tax, among others, and a growing deficit in Exchequer finances TII explored options for next generation road user charging/ tolling to enable Government to deliver on improved climate and economic performance, as the network returns to public ownership.

ᴏ The introduction, in the medium term, of distance, location and time-based road usage charges and that planning for such charges should include early identification of the appropriate technology to be used in calculating and applying them (see BRUCE project below).

It is worth noting that the charging of vehicles for the use of road infrastructure in the European Union is governed by Eurovignette Directive (EU) 2022/362, which has been transposed into Irish law. While the provisions of the Directive do not require Member States to levy road charges, the common rules laid down in the Directive must be followed in instances where charges are introduced.

ᴏ The introduction of congestion charges in key urban areas, based on a number of key metrics linked to environmental and individual impact and that these charges should be reviewed following the introduction of road usage charges.

ᴏ The introduction of an additional duty on non-residential car parking, both public and private, and not limited to employer-provided car parking, in the same key urban areas identified as suitable for congestion charges.

The COTW report and the extensive evidence used in support of its recommendations provide a strong system-wide rationale for many of the recommendations emerging from the Fiscal Measures expert sub-group in particular.

### Tax Strategy Group

*Pages 74–74*

Economic Cost of Congestion in the GDA

was €5.8m in 2022 and €22.4m in 2040. Like the GDA, even with the range of planned public and sustainable transport interventions in these areas, the overall growth in the cost of congestion will not be offset by these alone.

Car dependency has many impacts, but congestion is the costliest. Dublin is considered to have some of the worst congestion in the world, with other Irish cities also ranking highly in this context. In addition to increasing emissions, congestion means lost time, heightened stress, increased fuel costs and drastically worse air quality. Additionally, it brings wider costs to the economy through the increased costs of doing business.

Tax Strategy Group

Tax Strategy Group (TSG) papers, published by the Department of Finance, highlight the potential risks that may come from decarbonisation of the economy over the medium to long term, and the need to prepare for eroding energy and transportation tax revenues from the overall Exchequer tax base. In its Budget 2022 TSG paper, the Department of Finance specifically noted the area of vehicle taxation and that changes will be required over the medium to long term, to meet the challenges being faced by the transport sector in decarbonising the economy and in moving towards net zero by 205032.

In 2017, the Department of Transport conducted analysis to estimate the Greater Dublin Area’s (GDA) cost of aggravated congestion30. In 2023, the Department carried out further analysis, building on the work in 2017. Results of this analysis show that the cost of congestion is estimated to be €336m in 2022 rising to over €1.5bn by 2040 (both in 2016 prices)31. Results show that planned transport interventions in the GDA are projected to reduce the rate of growth in the cost of congestion for bus users, but that they are not sufficient to offset the overall growth in the cost of congestion which is driven by factors such as population, employment, and economic growth.

It noted that while medium term options may encompass newer approaches such as road user charging, in the short-term, policy decisions will have to be made on how the State maintains or adapts revenue streams. In the context of this Strategy, there is now an opportunity to consider the variety of taxation measures, including road user charging, as part of a medium to long term strategy to achieve mutually reinforcing objectives arising from this strategy and wider fiscal sustainability objectives in the COTW report (see Recommendation 2 in Chapter 10).

An Alternative Future Scenario, which examined the impact of changes such as increased working from home as a result of the Covid-19 pandemic, was also modelled as part of the study. This scenario resulted in a reduction in the cost of congestion to €263m in 2022 and €1.4bn in 2040. This demonstrates that behavioural change interventions can have significant impacts in reducing the cost of congestion over the longterm.

In recent TSG papers, including for Budget 2026, the Department of Finance noted that in the medium to longer term, vehicle taxes may have to shift to a weight based vehicle tax or surcharge in order to protect the vehicle tax base33. However, it also notes that ‘in relation to environmental, road safety and congestion outcomes, expectations should remain modest as a weight-based tax is a complementary measure rather than a central pillar for change. More targeted measures, in relation to vehicle dimensions, speed enforcement, driver behaviour and infrastructure capacity are required in order to fully achieve the latter outcomes’.

In respect of the regional cities, a further report was published in May 2025, and featured the three cities of Cork, Galway and Waterford. Results of this analysis indicate that the cost of congestion to the Cork Metropolitan Area (CMA) were €57.5m (in 2022) and is forecast to rise to over €97.2m by 2040. In the Galway Metropolitan Area (GMA), the cost was €35.3m in 2022 and is forecast to hit €106.9m in 2040. In the Waterford Metropolitan Area (WMA), the equivalent figures

Travel Behaviour and Public Insights

Almost half reported living within a 15-minute walk of a doctor’s surgery (47%).

The source of trips was an important input to understanding and designing a Strategy that would support a reduction in demand for unsustainable journeys. For example, the above graphic is illustrative of the breakdown of national trips, showing some differences in patterns between weekdays and weekends as expected.In respect of trip lengths, the NHTS shows that 50% of trips are under 5km and 36% are under 3km. This underlines the clear potential to achieve a significant reduction in car kms for those shorter journeys through a variety of measures such as enhancing shared mobility provision, increased permeability, roadspace reallocation and active travel infrastructure, etc – across all journey types.

National Householder Travel Survey

The National Householder Travel Survey (NHTS) is a nationwide study of Ireland’s travel habits, which will be published annually from this year onwards as per a commitment in the SMP. According to the 2024 NHTS, work/business

is the main reason for travel (21%), followed by education (19%), social reasons (18%) and shopping (15%). The car is the most frequently used mode of transport, accounting for 71% of all trips, followed by walking (18%), bus/coach (4%), truck/van (3%), cycling (2%) and Train/DART/Luas at 1%.

Public Attitudes to Demand Management Measures

Those aged 55-64 are most likely to travel by car at 77%, followed by those aged 45-54 years (76%). Those aged 15-24 are least likely to travel by car at 59% and are more likely than any other age group to travel by bus/coach (10%).

While the NHTS provides a snapshot of actual travel behaviour, we also need to understand the attitudes and motivations that inform these choices. In that regard, the Department of Transport commissioned research in 2023 to test public sentiment and attitudes towards sustainable mobility policies generally, including various demand management measures as part of the development of this Strategy. Key headline findings, arising from a series of focus groups were:

The age cohorts most likely to walk are 15-24s and 25-34s (both 22%). Cycling is highest at 3% among those aged 4-14, 15-24 and 35-44. The majority of those surveyed for the NHTS 2024 live within a 15-minute walk of a shop (71%), pub or restaurant (64%), a bus stop (67%), a chemist/ pharmacy (58%) or a public leisure space (60%).

ᴏ Driving by car is the default option for the vast majority of people in Ireland today. This is seen to offer convenience and comfort and good time management. A key sentiment that emerged from the research was “If you own a car, you might as well use it!”

ᴏ Revisiting initiatives like the Tax Saver Scheme to reflect the hybrid working model and engaging with companies to encourage hybrid working, with leadership coming from the top of organisations and companies for Smarter Travel Schemes.

ᴏ Changing individual behaviour away from cars is a tough ask, especially when individuals feel that the impact of their behaviour is far less significant than the changes that can be achieved by industry and Government.

ᴏ Looking at incentives such as discounted costs for families traveling on public transport and/ or the use of short term ‘novelty’ discounts, which may help to introduce many people to the advantages of public transport.

ᴏ There is a lack of awareness around improvements made, and being made, to public transport services and their relative utility by those who don’t or rarely use it. Improvements that have been made can be overlooked by the public who drive and there is a need to strongly assert the improvements that are being made by Government.

ᴏ Drawing attention to progress that has been and will be made in public transport and highlighting all the positives to counteract established negative beliefs.

In summary, all of the research, policy analyses and focus group work referenced in this Chapter point to the wide range of benefits to be achieved from addressing travel demand and from supporting greater efficiency in our transport system. They also point to the range of potential measures available (see examples in Appendix 1), which informed the discussions of the expert sub-groups and the final set of recommendations in this Strategy.

ᴏ To avoid being seen as ‘anti-car’ and to get drivers on board, it’s vital that a wellconsidered and well-funded plan, showing integration and connection, is presented to people to win their confidence in non-car options.

ᴏ The health impact of car driving is largely overlooked. At a high-level, air pollution associated with emissions is acknowledged, but contributions to this at a personal level as well as the local and personal negative impacts, are poorly understood.

ᴏ Behavioural change will require a clearly thought-out pathway consisting of data, actions and ease of adoption. It will be important to emphasise the benefits, at every level, to encourage people to make changes. Change at a small level needs to be applauded but underpinned by a sense of urgency.

The research also suggested certain longer-term opportunities such as:

ᴏ Highlighting a positive plan to enable the public to lessen their reliance on car usage. People need to know that it can be done.

ᴏ Reiterating existing initiatives that are available to encourage non-car travel: 90-minute journey, rebalanced public space to create safer streets in cities, Bike to Work etc.

## Six Key Areas of Focus

## 9. Six Key Areas of Focus

### Integrated Land-Use and Transport Planning

*Pages 78–83*

As outlined in the Introduction and informed by the foregoing research in Chapter 8, the Steering Group identified a number of key areas that warranted particular focus – areas that were considered to have the most potential for impacting transport demand reduction, given their role in generating journeys (particularly where historic system design has been less than optimal).

In summary, it is clear from the subgroups’ examination of the research and case studies provided that there is already significant scope within existing policies and programmes to strengthen demand management as a focus. It is also clear that solutions here will need to be tailored to specific circumstances particularly at a Local Authority level and that experimentation and innovation will be a feature of demand management schemes in order to test their impact but also to gain support and demonstrate the benefits of related measures. There is also a clear message from the sub-groups deliberations that measures to address travel demand need to work alongside the rollout of supply side measures – and therefore the continued acceleration of the Sustainable Mobility Policy Action Plan is key to the success of this Strategy.

Consequently, and as noted in Chapter 1, the National Steering Group established expert subgroups to focus on the following areas:

1. Integrated Land Use and Transport Planning - to consider how a high-level national policy approach to demand management could support and inform the revision to the NPF and current policy approaches to compact growth of settlements.

2. Optimal Use of Space - to examine a range of measures for maximising the well-being of people and the utility of public space, particularly in urban centres.

A summary of the outputs from each of the subgroups is set out as follows. These reflect ‘point in time’ considerations and can be reviewed in more detail in the associated reports that were prepared by each sub-group:

3. Fiscal Measures – to consider the role of fiscal measures in encouraging a shift towards sustainable mobility journeys – and greater network optimisation - while ensuring the ongoing fiscal sustainability of the sector’s contribution to the Exchequer.

Integrated Land-Use and Transport Planning

4. Generators of Demand (People) - to consider demand generated by escort-to-education trips, including the role of the School Transport Scheme and Safe Routes to School Programme as well as other major generators of transport demand e.g., hospitals/health services, tourism, leisure/sport and large transport hubs like Dublin Airport.

Introduction

Integrating land use and transport planning is key to ensuring that the built environment is connected in a way that reduces travel demand by private car sustainably. Planning is a system that is reliant on policy and investment decisions across Government. For example, the planning of homes, jobs, recreation, education, and other activities and services is complementary to the planning of transport infrastructure and services required to connect these activities. This approach to integrated planning can contribute significantly to reductions in transport-related emissions, as it requires a rethinking and reversal of decades of development patterns driven by the prioritisation of the private car as the primary form of transport. In practice, this means setting policies across Government that promote:

5. Generators of Demand (Freight) – to consider demand generated by industrial zones, ports, retail parks and consignors, freight hubs and gateways as well as the construction industry.

6. Captive Car Usage - to consider the impacts on, and supports for, captive car usage in line with the principle of just transition.

Not surprisingly, there was significant overlap in terms of the findings and recommendations arising from the sub-groups, although each was considered through a different lens. The final suite of recommendations set out in Chapter 10 has been informed by the work and reports of the subgroups along with the research and analysis, case studies and deliberations of the National Steering Group.

ᴏ Compact settlements while discouraging urban sprawl;

ᴏ Land use patterns that facilitate active travel, decrease the reliance on private cars, and increase the efficiency of existing sustainable transport systems; and

Measures focused on intensification and diversification

ᴏ Coordinated land use and transport policy objectives to form complementary intervention measures.

Densification measures focus on the increase of population and activity in an area through the intensification and diversification of underutilised land. Greater diversity of land use can contribute to more walkable neighbourhoods, thereby avoiding trips that otherwise would have been made by motorised transport. Rather than only equating densification with increases in building height, measures can look to intensify different activities in existing spaces by avoiding underutilisation at different times of the day and week to create consistency and diversity of use across time and place.

Ireland’s national, regional, county, and local land use planning system led by the NPF takes into consideration how the development of new and regenerated land might impact transport requirements. This approach aims to ensure that new development projects are accessible and connected by sustainable modes of transport, by tying together the infrastructure needed to facilitate public and active transport journeys with activity centres. The Office of the Planning Regulator (OPR) was established in April 2019 to reinforce this and ensure that Regional Spatial and Economic Strategies and city and county development plans are consistent with the development objectives of the NPF.

Measures incentivising densification through intensification and diversification should take into account how growth might contribute to negative impacts, to mitigate unintended consequences.

Measures Discussed

A comprehensive set of measures for supporting the objectives of this Strategy were gathered and consolidated to address challenges and approaches to integrated land use and transport planning at various scales and locations across Ireland. The high-level measures that emerged address the following areas:

Encouraging the redevelopment of brownfield sites and the reuse of underutilised spaces to cater to different activities across different times of the day and week are more sustainable ways of accommodating densification. Funding mechanisms such as the Urban Regeneration and Development Fund and Government policies such as Town Centre First and Delivering Homes, Building Communities prioritise the refurbishment of vacant buildings in centres and infill and brownfield development opportunities along transport corridors to regenerate centres and drive compact growth targets. A mix of uses and activities should also be carried out in accordance with universal design principles such as the provision of adequate facilities to provide ease of movement for people including those with disabilities, children, families, older persons and people with mobility difficulties. Land use taxation can also play a role in incentivising development in key areas.

ᴏ Intensification and diversification

ᴏ Access to services

ᴏ Permeability

ᴏ Evaluating plan and policy efficacy

ᴏ Behaviour change

While many of the measures that are relevant to integrated land use and transport planning are already written into policy, in some areas they have had limited implementation on the ground. When applied in practice, these measures need to be adapted to respond to the challenges specific to the location and its scale.

National planning guidelines should discourage private car use where it conflicts with sustainable development and land intensification objectives. For example, the Sustainable Residential Development and Compact Settlements Guidelines require graduated parking rates to be applied based on proximity and accessibility to services and public transport. The guidelines require a graduated approach to be taken with regard to densities, with highest densities in

centres and along public transport corridors and more compact and sustainable forms of development overall, noting that densities will be lower in areas where there may be higher levels. The guidelines also encourage the repurposing of car parks in highly accessible urban locations to support densification and make space available for other land uses.

Considerations in respect of developing these studies to date has fed into the revision of the National Planning Framework and broader policy development to support implementation of the NPF. This includes consideration of legislative and policy provisions, institutional arrangements, capacity-building measures and funding and resources required to facilitate the delivery of such plan-led development in a timely manner. To finance TOD, local authorities and other statutory undertakers will need to be supported in generating revenue to finance infrastructure needed to support TOD. DHLGH is advancing proposals for Urban Development Zones (UDZs) which may assist in supporting this approach.

Measures focused on access to services

Linking land development with public transport nodes that connect areas through Transport Oriented Development (TOD) facilitates public and active transport use. This approach can avoid mobility deserts, or places with constrained mobility access. Further co-locating public transport stations with mobility hubs can facilitate sustainable, multimodal mobility and make the full door to door journey more convenient and competitive to the private vehicle alternative. In this context, universal design planning should be considered to assess the effective integration of public transport infrastructure with workplaces, healthcare facilities, education centres, retail facilities and social spaces.

Measures focused on permeability

Permeability in integrated land use and transport planning refers to the ease of movement in and through an area, particularly via active transport. Areas separated by barriers are unattractive to pedestrians, cyclists or others using active modes of travel as they need to take long detours around the impermeable space. Permeable design aims to create liveable and more inclusive spaces by making the city accessible to all people. Liveable settlements are those that have compact, mixed use design with active building frontages to create lively streets and adaptable spaces to accommodate the changing needs of users. Such issues are highlighted and addressed in the DMURS, published in 2013 and updated in 2019.

To discourage reliance on private vehicles, high quality and affordable public transport network development that precedes the development of new housing, services, and employment can encourage sustainable travel patterns before unsustainable habits are formed. From the demand side, to contribute to the development and sustainable use of transport nodes, measures should focus on incentivising companies and services to locate in proximity to the public transport node, supported by fiscal levers where necessary.

Permeability should also not only be thought of in terms of physical connectivity, but also consider psychological effects that can make routes impermeable to users. Particularly along pedestrian-only pathways, poor lighting and blocked sightlines can decrease users’ perceived safety of a route and disincentivise them from using it to avoid a longer detour. Perceived safety of cycling and walking/wheeling can also affect how permeable an environment is to an individual. Long stretches of heavily trafficked roads without safe crossing points for pedestrians or dropped kerbs at reasonable intervals can also act as soft barriers.

From the supply side, developers should be incentivised to build mixed-use developments that make efficient use of space and create more inclusive environments for different user groups.

To deliver transport-oriented development (TOD), multi- department and agency coordination and strategic investment are required. Under Housing for All, a working group was established by DOT and DHLGH to consider opportunities for TOD in major urban centres. The initial focus of the group was on Dublin, with a report published in June 2023. Further reports on the Eastern Region, Cork and Limerick, Galway and Waterford will be published shortly.

Measures focused on evaluating plan and policy efficacy

maximum parking requirements, physically distancing parking space access from urban centres and pedestrianised areas, introducing pricing for parking that varies spatially and temporarily, and incorporating city, town, and village centres with surrounding residential areas into parking demand management strategies.

To ensure that integrated land use and transport policy and plans result in the desired outcomes related to this Strategy’s objectives, measures that support monitoring and evaluation are essential to identifying issues, tracking progress and assessing outcomes. Evaluation of plan and policy efficacy can focus on one or more of the following areas: the alignment between the proposed plan and the policy goals, the extent to which the plan or policy has been implemented, and to what degree the plan or policy has achieved its desired outcomes.

Parking management can also take the form of workplace parking levies which typically involve charging employers a fee based on the number of parking spaces they provide for their employees and visitors.

To further encourage the use of new infrastructure that supports sustainable travel modes, information campaigns targeting attitudes to, and perceptions of, active travel and public transport are key to induce behavioural change. Engagement and outreach initiatives are needed in communities, schools, and at the workplace to expose people to the negative externalities of private car use and the positive outcomes, such as the health and wellbeing benefits of active travel in particular. The national dialogue regarding climate and transport needs to be ever consistent to fully support this messaging.

Co-designing plans with the input of stakeholder groups is a valuable way of designing a plan that meets the target group’s objectives and adequately addresses their challenges. Aligning integrated planning and policy measures with climate action targets helps focus efforts and brings CO2 emissions reduction targets to the forefront of conceptualising how these measures are implemented. Setting performance metrics and measurable targets for determining the effectiveness of integrated land use and transport planning measures is valuable for identifying where and when implementation efforts may be falling short of policy aspirations. Baseline assessments are key to understanding current conditions and provide a basis for determining the outcomes and impact of each measure.

Challenges

Decades of development that prioritised the role of the private car led to policies that prioritise travel time savings over the need for proximity. Congestion was seen as the primary challenge, and road capacity expansion was seen as the solution, thereby compounding the increasing problem of underutilising space for other functions and thinking about ways to avoid the need for the private car through better integrated land use and transport planning. This has in turn segmented the responsibilities of transport and planning, often minimising the role that land-use and housing decisions play in the performance of transport systems, including in terms of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions.

Measures focused on behaviour change

To change attitudes and perceptions in favour of sustainable modes, integrated land use and transport planning should anticipate real-world complexity and account for how travel needs are driven by the types of locations that people may have a reason to access. Providing conveniently located sustainable alternatives, and increasing the availability of services at and between centres, can enable behavioural changes in favour of sustainable transport modes and support the successful outcomes of integrated land use and transport measures.

Implementing many of the measures discussed on the ground can present challenges. Retrofitting existing spaces can face pushback from local residents due to fears around how the design changes may result in negative effects. Particularly, creating new linkages in existing areas or planning new roads to accommodate all transport modes to cut through spaces can negatively affect the attractiveness of the

Leveraging parking management as a means of encouraging sustainable travel and reducing private vehicle usage is a common strategy employed internationally. Different approaches to parking management include constraining

environment to non-car users. Not only does this risk discouraging a shift to sustainable transport modes, but also has little impact on reducing the demand for private car use.

ᴏ the enactment of legislative proposals to support National Policy Objective 69 in the NPF, which relates to the extension of statutory arrangements between spatial and transport planning in the Greater Dublin Area to other cities for Metropolitan Area Transport Strategies;

That said, it is acknowledged that the main impact of improving the integration of land use and transport planning will be around new developments – rather than in the retrofitting of established developments and, as such, may have limited effects in terms of reducing emissions on an absolute basis.

ᴏ the development of policy and guidance in relation to planning for enterprise and employment uses in statutory development plans, noting that these uses continue to occur in peripheral locations which are challenging to serve with effective alternatives to the private car.

Measures aiming to improve the perceived ease and comfort of switching to public or active transport, such as requirements for cycle lanes and bicycle parking in tandem with reductions in car parking access, can disincentivise private vehicle use. However, at the same time, when implementing these measures on the ground, consideration needs to be given to how a measure might unintentionally generate demand. For example, park-and-ride facilities placed at train stations might need to impose a nominal parking fee to avoid displacing trips to the station previously undertaken on foot or by bicycle with private vehicle use.

Accordingly, it is anticipated that the placing of transport plans on a statutory footing can strengthen the capacity of the Office of the Planning Regulator to address transport matters in city and county development plans and local area plans, allowing them to have a greater focus on transport plans and strategies. The implications of this would require further consideration, particularly its impact on working relationships between with NTA and TII and OPR.

Measures aimed at ensuring further integration of land-use and transport planning, taking account of intensification, diversification, access to services and permeability, will be delivered as part of implementation mechanisms under Recommendation 1 of this Strategy as well as through recommendations relating to the development by Local Authorities of Demand Management Strategies and the guidance and supports that would underpin these (Recommendations 3 to 9):

Additionally, communications and engagement will be essential to the delivery of these measures. Among other issues, opposition from local residents has complicated several initiatives in recent years and implementing authorities will need to work with local residents to alleviate any fears that they may have in relation to planned projects.

Conclusion and Recommendations

Recommendation 1: Strengthen policy alignment and implementation mechanisms to deliver NPF National Strategic Outcome 1 ‘Compact Growth’ and NSO 4 ‘Sustainable Mobility’ given the critical role of compact growth in reducing travel demand and enhancing transport system efficiencies.

In light of the above, and in an effort to bring a renewed focus to densification, intensification and diversification of underutilised land, which can support the viability of a sustainable transport system and related mobility alternatives, it is proposed to strengthen policy alignment, governance and funding mechanisms to support the achievement of compact growth priorities detailed in the NPF and subject to regulatory oversight via:

Recommendations aimed at behavioural change, with regard to parking management approaches, are captured across other sub-groups – Optimal Use of Space, Fiscal Measures and Generators of Demand (People). However, any high-level plans in this area would involve the collating and analysing of data on parking at a national level including

ᴏ the functions of the Office of the Planning Regulator;

### Optimal Use of Space
