Generators of Demand (Retail, Industry, and Freight)

As referenced above, the School Transport Scheme 2030 report has now been published. Arising from the recommendations in the review, it is projected that access to the School Transport Scheme will be expanded so that an additional 100,000 pupils (over 2024 levels) can be carried by 2030. It is clear that if achieved, the overall way forward recommended in the review to decrease car journeys to school and get more children and young people to access school via public transport and safe cycling and walking routes, if achieved will bring enormous benefits to local communities.

Introduction

Ireland’s domestic and international freight sector is an integral part of the economy. Most domestic freight movements take place by road whilst international movements (including those between Northern Ireland and Ireland) include more significant multimodal elements covering road, air, sea and rail freight. The freight sector has faced many overlapping challenges in recent years including Brexit, COVID-19 and the war in Ukraine. These challenges have highlighted the importance of the freight sector and have also demonstrated how complex international supply chains have become. This underlines the importance of all stakeholders working together – industry, Government and international partners - to ensure the resilience of Ireland’s supply chains.

The proposed changes to eligibility criteria as set out in the recommendations in the review are expected to expand the numbers of students availing of transport, reducing reliance on individual car journeys to access education.

Overall, the recommended changes to the future operation of the Scheme concern:

ᴏ expansion of the current eligibility criteria, addressing current operational challenges charges and grants

The significance of the need for a sustainable freight system cannot be underestimated, and, as the economy grows, demand for goods and the requirement for bulk movement continues to grow in Ireland. This growth puts strain on the people, infrastructure and businesses operating in

ᴏ over time, moving towards better integration with public transport to ensure optimum value for money to the Exchequer.

It is recognised that resources including contractor supply of vehicles and drivers would

the freight, haulage, logistics and transportation sector. There is a need to assess how this growth and demand can be best managed and supported by Government, industry, and the public.

and infrastructure planning would be beneficial. Further consideration of rail access to ports will be summarised within local implementation considerations.

Modelling future demand for freight to a greater level of specificity is key to understanding future constraints to capacity and investment priorities. Demand modelling is currently undertaken across rail, roads, ports and land use and given the essential nature of freight, should be interfaced to provide a multimodal perspective of future demand.

Additionally, the need for a certain level of infrastructure provision was required to encourage hauliers to move to alternative fuels. In particular, the need for a critical mass of electric charging infrastructure and alternative fuel supply infrastructure was emphasised. Tied to the need for infrastructure provision was the need for subsidies. Feedback from the group on the availability and level of subsidies available for a number of freight-industry related items – particularly drivetrain transition and alternative fuels – was that these are insufficient. Moving forward, more accurate subsidy setting will increase uptake, drive change and deliver the policy outcomes necessary while limiting financial deadweight.

This sub-group considered the impact and management of goods demand on haulage and road freight and associated aspects of the wider transportation system, and how we may best collectively work to support growth while reducing the environmental impact in a sustainable way.

Measures Considered

Discussions related to modal shift focused on two main areas: large scale bulk movements and disaggregated light movements. Bulk movement such as construction materials, quarried aggregates etc. are dominant by tonnes per kilometre (TKM). However, light vehicle fleet numbers and kms travelled have increased significantly over the past decade. Bulk movements internationally arrive or leave the island of Ireland via its ports. Support was given to the development of port facilities, including the additional space allowances and the potential extension of rail links as indicated within the All- Island Strategic Rail Review, and Iarnród Éireann Rail Freight Strategy. While it was anticipated that bulk movements will continue to be via HDVs in the main with a focus on developing these movements on the TEN-T network, positive sentiment was held towards the continued development of rail freight where possible. It was noted also that the benefits of the ongoing National Roads 2040 Strategy will accrue in part to the alleviation of congestion for road freight haulage. Continued investment in infrastructure to support freight haulage is necessary to ensure that the growth demands of road freight do not exceed the constraints of the road network.

When considering measures, the sub-group grouped them into national, regional and local measures. Measures implemented at a national scale include those related to policy, centrally administered subsidies, industry standards and certification, cross-industry engagement, large infrastructure interventions and measures related to the interface between national and international interests such as port administration requirements. Regional measures included regional infrastructure networks and hubs, county level and regional economic planning forums, hinterland access networks, and sub-national consolidation programmes. Local measures discussed related to implementation and included micro consolidation, load sharing, access control and emissions zones, local land use planning and urban multimodality.

National Measures

Planning and the delivery of infrastructure were identified as being essential to the sustainable movement of goods around Ireland. The subgroup felt that the freight sector is an afterthought when it comes to the delivery of new land-use policies and that going forward the needs of the sector must be incorporated more completely into policy planning. Particularly, integrated planning both between ports (regarding forward balance planning), industry forecasting and signalling for hauliers and also in terms of hinterland access

Smaller, disaggregated goods delivered via LDVs and vans in urban areas are considered to be a separate and different challenge. It was noted that, in many cases, breaking bulk loads into smaller vehicles can increase vehicle kilometres travelled

Regional Measures

by up to 16-fold. Additional implementation considerations for consolidation hubs included feedback on the reality of collaboration across commercial delivery entities. In this case, focus was given to creating partnership agreements including government partners. National level measures also included support and pathfinding projects for urban mode shift including loadsharing hubs facilitated via public transport operators and subsidies for active mode systems such as cargo bikes. Specific discussions were had about the role of consignor liability and procurement standards, and what role liability has in procurement relationships between demand generators and hauliers. Sustainable procurement measures should include the consideration by representatives such as the Office of Government Procurement to set a baseline of consignor liability expectations and potentially to legislate minimum safety and sustainability practices associated with consignor liability and related practices.

Measures practically implemented at a regional scale held valid aspects of national considerations also, but received specific feedback relating to the delivery of interventions at sub-national levels.

Such measures included logistical planning for large-scale infrastructure investments, delegated autonomy from national policy for regional policy implementation, and port access and hinterland infrastructure access.

The sub-group noted the potential benefit of utilising city coordination policies for large scale infrastructure programmes, helping to identify, coordinate, and optimise the movement of construction-related movements for urban and large regional projects. Such policies have been employed effectively in the City of Sydney for Sydney Metro and during London 2012’s Olympic development. It was indicated that Dublin and Cork could benefit from such policies during Metrolink and potential Light Rail projects in the future.

The benefit of engagement and collaboration between the Department, TII and NTA was highlighted particularly on the multifaceted agenda of decarbonisation and demand management. Engagement and alignment across multiple programmes - electrification, demand management, alternative fuels infrastructure, rail freight planning and ports capacity - were all noted as areas with considerable collaboration potential. Alignment in messaging is essential across all programmes.

The challenges faced at sub-national level to dictate the implementation of programmes that can have industry-level transformational impact were highlighted, particularly the national level focus on predominantly urban issues. It was noted that regional and rural distribution is still a significant challenge, and it was recommended that actions taken by large food producers around consolidation should be investigated for best-practice and replication. Rural distribution in communities and for rural businesses is still challenging and often not suitable for support by TEN-T Core Network.

TII continue to work on implementation of multi-point tolling initiatives and feedback from the subgroup with regard to such initiatives was focused on ensuring that regional and national movements via the TEN-T network were supported, and that pricing was applied in a manner to deliver on policy aims and on keeping HDVs off local roads. The existing model for access pricing on the Iarnród Éireann network was raised as a current barrier to entry for increasing rail-freight proportions of overall freight tonnage. It was noted that for a sustainable balance of multi-modal freight across road and rail to be established, in line with policy aims, evaluation of a more economic access charging basis would be required.

In addition to the previous noted feedback on port infrastructure planning, regional integration planning particularly at Dublin Port and Port of Cork were considered as being beneficial to improve flow and access with the potential to also support multimodality including hinterland access via rail freight links and dry-dock infrastructure.

Local Measures

The scale and implementation of local measures was given specific consideration, in particular the constraints, challenges and needs of logistics, freight and goods supply in urban areas. A number of key themes were raised with an overall message of ensuring the needs of the essential deliveries to businesses, places of work, education centres

and homes are considered across all subgroup interventions proposed.

minimum number of hours on one site to support such demand management.

Local measures discussed included kerbside management and loading access, delivery access and times, freight management strategies, construction management planning, and urban multimodality.

As noted in previous sections, the potential use of active modes and public transport for deliveries was discussed. Enabling infrastructure and the provision of cycling facilities in urban areas were particularly important, as well as limiting interactions between heavy vehicles and vulnerable road users such as cyclists. Sharing public transport facilities to support micro consolidation hubs was noted for consideration of key public transport providers as well as the potential for multimodality that local hubs could provide.

Kerbside management and loading bay access was discussed at length, particularly with regard to parking, access restrictions and potential pedestrianisation initiatives. Key insights for implementation focused on planning requirements for adequately sized loading bays, smart-loading bay solutions, loading bays that can facilitate just in time delivery, enforcement of parking around loading bays and the encouragement of planning requirements for logistics plans and adequate loading bay infrastructure.

Challenge

The freight sector poses a unique challenge as it is expected to significantly grow in the coming years and the upfront costs for alternative technologies are significantly higher than for diesel HDVs.

Significant weight was also placed on ensuring that the needs of deliveries are adequately considered in pedestrianisation and access-based measures. For example, anecdotal evidence was offered in respect of heavy deliveries having to be undertaken at significant distances due to an inability to gain access close to served businesses. Allowances for out-of-hours access in pedestrianised and shared areas are widespread and consideration should be made for these in all cases. As noted above, logistics plans should be included in all planning considerations.

Additionally, as an island our ability to transport goods long distances by train is limited compared with mainland Europe. At regional and local levels, consideration should be given to ‘last mile’ logistic operations for freight and associated deliveries. How this is implemented will need careful consideration as space for delivery trucks is not always factored into the rollout of demand management measures such as road space reallocation which has had unintended consequences for deliveries in some cases. The management of kerbsides, ensuring deliveries are built into proposed measures, will be essential to maintaining levels of service and preventing disruption. As with all measures, consistent communication will be important.

It was raised that an interfacing suite of city and regional freight management strategies should be developed on a coordinated and collaborative basis with industry, to enable forward planning of freight management in urban, peri-urban and regional-rural areas. Sharing the approach between jurisdictions will create a network of best practice and will allow for transport, ports, energy and other related national agencies to sufficiently plan for the future.

Conclusion and Recommendations

National Measures

Planning processes that require construction management should be coordinated across similarly linked projects. Submissions should be site- specific and not generic, and focus on the breakdown of material types, delivery types, labour types and the mode of transport to be used and its management. Where possible and on larger projects, labour in urban areas should use public transport and provide secure storage for tools and equipment for contractors that have a critical

Given the positive sentiment towards the continued development of rail freight, and working collaboratively across multiple agencies, it is intended to develop a policy for the development of Strategic Rail Freight Interchanges. This will ensure that the transport system is capable of meeting demand from a growing market in intermodal goods and parcel services in a sustainable way. The All-Island Strategic Rail

Review (AISRR) acknowledges the role that heavy rail can play in supporting a sustainable freight, logistics and transport system and its suitability, in particular, to the traditional bulk freight market (which are generally non-time critical flows), as well as the growing market in intermodal goods and parcel services (which are more time critical). Rail freight is generally considered to be most competitive over relatively long distances. In Ireland, this means the potential role of rail freight will be focused on serving inter-regional journeys between the island’s largest cities and busiest ports. The All-Island Strategic Rail Review recommends the following:

Recommendations to develop national level measures for freight are set out in Chapter 10 and summarised here as follows:

Recommendation 10: Continue to enhance rail freight capacity and explore potential for future growth in the sector.

Recommendation 27: Raising consumer awareness on vehicle buying and ownership.

Regional Measures

As outlined in the Road Haulage Strategy, the preparation of strategies for sustainable freight distribution for the Greater Dublin Area and the metropolitan areas of the four regional cities would provide a solid basis to achieve more effective management of goods movement.

ᴏ Develop a sustainable solution for first-mile last mile rail access for Dublin Port;

ᴏ Reduce Track Access Charges for freight services;

The sub-group concurred with this position but reinforced the need for stakeholder involvement in this work. For this reason, it was advised that the NTA, TII, regional assemblies, Local Authorities, Irish Rail, port and airport operators, the haulage sector and key freight-generating sectors of the economy should be represented in any process to develop such strategies.

ᴏ Strengthen rail connectivity to the island’s busiest ports; and

ᴏ Develop a network of inland terminals close to major cities on the rail network;

Aligned to the above is Iarnród Éireann’s Rail Freight 2040 Strategy, which commits to providing rail connectivity to Ireland’s Tier 1 ports, including connections from the ports at Foynes and the Port of Cork to the rail network, direct rail connections to industrial facilities and increasing the market share of rail freight in line with other European countries. Of the AISRR recommendations, the policy could consider the potential for accelerating a reduction in rail access charges.

The Greater Dublin Area Transport Strategy 2022- 2042, includes a Measure ‘FREIGHT 1’ which will support the decarbonisation of the freight sector, seek to further integrate smart technologies in logistics management and reinforce the role that the strategic road and rail network, and the location of ports, have in the efficient movement of freight. Key in this are two elements– the Strategic Traffic Management Plan, as required under Section 64 of the Dublin Transport Authority (DTA) Act 2008 and the Demand Management Scheme as provided for under Section 71 of the DTA Act.

Another national level intervention would involve the ongoing implementation of measures related to energy efficiency, for example widespread eco-driving training and practice for HGVs– in line with the Department of Transport national standard, recently established. Also of importance in this area is the potential role of vehicle fuelefficiency labelling (VFEL). This would help to raise consciousness about the fuel efficiency of the vehicles on sale, based on the assumption that properly informed consumers/ businesses will be more likely to purchase vehicles that are more fuel efficient. Intelligent Transport Systems (ITS) technologies can also help to progress energy efficiency, providing drivers with real-time feedback to directly control vehicle speed to improve traffic flow and reduce the incidence and severity of collisions.

Consistent with GDA Strategy Measure ‘FREIGHT 1’, a consultant team appointed by the NTA has developed a framework for the future development of a Sustainable Freight Distribution Strategy, in consultation with key stakeholders. This work has now been completed and is intended to inform how the GDA Strategy’s freight-related objectives are progressed. The NTA will also consider the applicability of this framework to the regional cities’ metropolitan areas as part of the implementation of their respective transport strategies.

Local Measures

In addition to the five largest urban centres, consideration of goods movement at regional level via Regional Freight Strategies would add value.

Building on outputs from the SENATOR project, it is proposed to review national and international best practice case studies and identify what options are best suited for implementation in Ireland. Additionally, information on activities and projects already underway in Ireland in relation to freight operational efficiencies will be gathered and the capacity for building on, or replicating, those initiatives where relevant and appropriate will be examined.

These should be consistent with the objectives of the RSESs, with a particular focus on intersettlement freight movement and on the preparation of regional freight distribution plans. The RSES Monitoring Plans and Local Transport Plans, currently being prepared in conjunction with statutory Local Area Plans, would be relevant in this context. When evaluating infrastructure investment, the operational impacts on freight should also be considered.

This work could also:

ᴏ assess and identify possible incentives for supporting urban consolidation centres, last-mile green delivery and the promotion of cycling/walking for deliveries;

The NTA commissioned consultants in June 2023 to carry out research on the different types of innovative logistics hubs that would, if implemented at scale, have the potential to reduce carbon emissions from road freight to a significant extent in Ireland. The scope of this research included recommendations for some practical pilot actions that could be taken over the next 2-3 years to demonstrate (on a smaller scale) the inherent potential of such hubs to the market, such as:

ᴏ consider the potential for shifting urban delivery trips to off- peak times to reduce trip times (emissions), leading to more efficient freight operations and reduce congestion;

ᴏ consider the options for the creation of a single destination for delivery vehicles that can be shared by multiple users in the same neighbourhood/ vicinity;

ᴏ Operating a sustainable distribution centre, using zero emission HGVs;

ᴏ consider the use of various interventions to optimise freight transport including (but not limited to): asset sharing and digital platforms (addressing any existing barriers), spatial modelling of logistics demand, dynamic routing, AI and machine learning, autonomous deliveries and ITS;

ᴏ Operating a ‘Green Port’ which uses zero emission equipment and offers ships the opportunity to use shore power;

ᴏ Developing and operating a Construction Consolidation Centre;

ᴏ Developing and operating a micro-hub for ‘last mile’ deliveries to a city centre; and

ᴏ strategies for increasing awareness of the environmental impact of same/next day deliveries and setting out alternatives and their benefits;

ᴏ Developing and operating a decoupling point for ‘last mile’ deliveries.

In summary, there is one key recommendation being made in respect of the above as part of this Strategy:

In summary, the measures recommended would be encompassed in the following.

Recommendation 12: Improve opportunities for freight consolidation, last mile green delivery and use of technology.

Recommendation 11: Develop Sustainable Freight Distribution Strategies for 5 Cities.

This recommendation would also be aligned to the national position on rail freight arising from the AISRR.

### Captive Car Users

*Pages 118–122*

Captive Car Users

Shift car travel to more sustainable modes, ᴏ where possible and practicable. Given that car captivity stems from a lack of transport alternatives, shift measures primarily focus on shortening the car portion of certain journey types by shifting part of a trip to more sustainable modes, recognising that for many captive car trips, a vehicle may still be required for the first or last mile.

Introduction

In the realm of demand management, implementing measures to address the needs of captive car users plays a pivotal role in ensuring a more inclusive and efficient transport system while advancing broader societal and environmental objectives. Captive car users are individuals who rely on their personal vehicles due to limited viable alternatives, often driven by factors such as lack of suitable public transport options, physical disabilities, various journey types, or specific job requirements. As cited in the CSO’s National Travel Survey in 2021, for over three quarters (76.4%) of journeys to work by car as driver, there was no alternative mode of travel to using the car for going to work, suggesting that a significant portion of the Irish population find themselves to be captive car users for some or all trips.

Improve the carbon emission per km for those ᴏ who do not have an option but to travel by car.

This approach acknowledges the necessity of minimising social and economic disparities during the transition towards sustainable mobility.

As noted by the sub-group, it will be crucial that the just transition component is integral when defining the above group of measures.

“Avoid” measures should consider the impact on employment and community well-being. ”Shift” measures, encouraging sustainable alternative transport modes, must prioritise equitable access and affordability. “Improve” measures, such as enhancing public transport and car emissions, should focus on preserving and creating jobs in the new markets, affordability of new technologies and accessibility to charging infrastructure. A just transition ensures that the benefits of reduced car dependence are shared inclusively, addressing potential challenges to employment, accessibility, and social cohesion. This approach not only promotes environmental sustainability but also fosters a fair and balanced evolution of the transportation system for all members of society.

Representative cohorts of captive car users were considered by this sub-group to assess the potential impact of the potential demand management measures under consideration. The cohorts have been informed by sub-group discussions and insights from the NTA National Travel Survey. The NTA National Travel Survey provides insights into the purpose of journeys that are undertaken by car nationally. The survey results identify differences in the purpose of car usage across urban, suburban and rural areas, as well as across a range of demographic and socioeconomic categories.

It is recommended by the sub-group that additional analysis and quantification of captive car users is undertaken to better inform an assessment of all proposed demand management measures from a just transition perspective.

Avoid

The primary objective of “Avoid” measures is to reduce or avoid the overall amount of travel and, therefore, kilometres travelled by car, by supporting shorter distance trips or fewer trips. These measures promote compact, mixed-use, and transport-oriented development, as well as remote working, e-commerce, and other forms of digitalisation that can substitute physical trips. Moreover, measures that discourage the consumption of fossil fuels and carbon-intensive products, such as carbon taxes, also could encourage people to avoid unnecessary trips.

Measures Considered

In collaboration with various stakeholders, a comprehensive set of measures aimed at delivering the objectives of this Strategy was considered through the Avoid-Shift-Improve lens in order to:

Avoid the need for car travel and, therefore, ᴏ the total kilometres travelled.

Shift

Another key action will be to increase the share of low-emission vehicle kilometres for goods and public transport vehicles. The most intensely used (and most fossil fuel-consuming) motor vehicles should be the first to transition to low emission technology, including light and heavy goods vehicles and public transport vehicles.

The primary objective of “Shift” measures is to provide attractive and convenient sustainable travel options to captive-car users. This will, in turn, reduce the total number of kilometres travelled by car and allocate them to other modes. With the ‘just transition’ principle in mind, these measures are aimed at addressing issues such as accessibility, affordability, frequency and coverage of public transport, last-mile connectivity, park and ride facilities, shared mobility alternatives, incentives or education and behavioural change campaigns. The scaling-up of on-demand shared services was also deemed to be an essential measure.

When planning for this transition, it will be key to design the measures to be implemented from a just transition perspective in order to minimise disproportionate impacts on specific car dependent groups.

Challenges

To create a more sustainable and efficient transport system across both urban and rural Ireland, it is essential to develop targeted strategies and policies that not only address the unique challenges faced by captive car users but also encourage and facilitate the choice of more sustainable modes for those who have viable options already in place and lower emissions transport where this is not possible. By considering the diverse needs of the national car-dependent population, the country can promote more equitable and effective demand management solutions that benefit both individual car users and the transport ecosystem. In the context of just transition, it is critical to focus and target supports to the most vulnerable captive car users when considering transport demand management.

Improve

The primary objective of “Improve” measures is to decrease the carbon emissions per kilometre travelled by enhancing the efficiency, sustainability, and overall quality of transport systems. These measures are targeted at improving the GHG efficiency of residual vehiclebased transport or the efficiency of the network itself. While less directly transformative than avoid and shift measures in terms of behaviour, fleet electrification and biofuels will continue to play a pivotal role in decarbonising transport, particularly for populations living in more isolated areas. Measures within this category will focus on preserving and creating new jobs, and the affordability of new technologies.

While “Avoid” transport demand measures, such as promoting remote working, offer environmental benefits, there are potential undesired social consequences, especially in rural areas.

As per the OECD report referenced previously, to increase the level of transformation through the electrification of vehicles, it will be key that Ireland’s electrification policy supports the overall system transformation, particularly the goal of reducing car travel. This requires prioritising the electrification of frequently used cars (such as taxis). Reducing the privileges of private cars compared to frequently used electric cars (e.g. parking privileges) can help to electrify the remaining car-kilometres more quickly. Defining the electrification target as the share of the remaining reduced car-kilometres has several benefits: it avoids rewarding efforts that increase overall car ownership and use, as the current subtarget does. It also focuses electrification efforts and resources on frequently used cars, which helps to favour shared over private car ownership and to speed up electrification.

As highlighted by the sub-group members, while strategies such as incentives for individuals to work remotely may be effective, an increase in the numbers working at home may lead to decreased social interactions, which can impact mental health and contribute to feelings of isolation of marginalised populations. Another implementation consideration is the impact of increasing demand for public transport on riders’ experience, especially for disabled people or people with limited mobility. For younger or newer workers in an organisation, it may also negatively impact their full integration into the company, and it may inhibit their progress within the organisation.

Another potential challenge of remote working policies, as discussed by the subgroup, was the phenomenon of people relocating further away from their workplace. This is particularly relevant in the Irish context, where housing prices in urban employment areas are high and available options are limited. This can lead to an increase in car dependency and the overall distance travelled. To overcome this challenge, measures targeting captive car users need to be coupled with policies that encourage sustainable and compact urban development.

longer time-horizons is required to facilitate better longer-term planning and implementation of new and upgraded PSO services.

While significant effort is being made in Ireland in the area of “Improve” related measures, further investment and policy supports will be required to maintain momentum and achieve the ambitious targets set by the CAP. As discussed before, studies such as the OECD’s 2022 report acknowledge that attempting to decarbonise a car-dependent system, such as the one we have in Ireland, via private vehicle improvements is unlikely to lead to substantially different patterns of behaviour, rapid emissions reductions, or large well-being improvements. Car-dependent systems make rapid electrification slow and difficult, by locking-in large and growing vehicle fleets. Even with improved (and fully electric) vehicles, they also fail to reduce life-cycle emissions, address accessibility gaps and reduce other negative impacts (e.g. road fatalities).

While there is significant investment, planning and delivery already underway under the ”Shift” theme, the sub-group members acknowledged that these efforts need to be ramped up to achieve the level of transformation required. There are several challenges that will need to be addressed when designing demand management measures aimed at achieving a modal shift to active travel among captive car users. One significant obstacle is the deeply ingrained car-centric culture, where there is a perceived convenience and comfort associated with private vehicles over other alternatives. Convincing captive car users to adopt active travel modes requires overcoming what are now well entrenched behavioural and cultural norms, perceptions related to inconvenience, safety concerns, and weather-related challenges. The scaling up of communication efforts has high transformative potential and is needed to build political support for any proposed policy.

Sub-group members also identified other significant barriers such as human capacity, communications/buy-in, and a lack of necessary data to design evaluation into measure design. A focus on piloting and iteration was identified as a response with potential to aid in achieving buy-in from citizens.

In terms of financial impacts, these include the risk of subsidising or providing financial incentives to existing users (with a limited impact on behavioural change), start-up costs (for solutions such as Mobility as a Service (MaaS)) and other derived costs such as marketing, app costs and ongoing engagement for the implementation of other carpooling measures. In the financial area it was also mentioned that it would be important to compare the implementation costs versus the cost of doing nothing, with the possible resultant costs to the state under EU carbon credit and statistical transfer systems.

Inadequate or unsafe infrastructure for pedestrians and cyclists is another critical issue, as the lack of well-designed paths and lanes can discourage individuals from choosing active modes. Additionally, the perceived time constraints associated with active travel, especially in comparison to the speed and efficiency of car travel, may dissuade individuals from making the shift. To provide convenient public transport alternatives to captive car users, CAP23 notes that the level of service enhancements needed to drive the required modal shift is significant (e.g. NTA modelling work applies a 3-fold increase in service frequency to drive modal shift). Not only will costs of existing services need to be met, but delivering the required modal shift to public transport is predicated on significant further expansion in services and frequency. Greater clarity as to the availability of current funding over

Conclusion and Recommendations

In line with CAP23, a targeted communications strategy is being undertaken to promote action being taken by Government, and the citizen’s role, in climate action in the transport sector. In the first instance, this sub-group recognised the need to challenge the deeply ingrained car-

Recommendation 15: Continue to evolve opportunities for flexible and remote working and digitalisation.

centric culture that currently exists in Ireland. For this reason, it is intended that the Strategy will seek to engage and motivate the citizen to make sustainable travel choices, supported by robust research. This will include campaigns aimed at raising consumer awareness around car emissions and the cost of ownership, focusing on financial, environmental and social costs of ownership compared to other modes, including the potential for car advertising regulation and mandatory disclosure of emissions data. In this way, people will have more information to help compare costs and benefits of various travel choices. As well as an increased need for data, to help better target supports for captive car users, the effectiveness of regulatory measures relating to advertising will also be considered. To assist in identifying who exactly is a captive car user, the subgroup recommended that a definition of Just Transition in this instance be developed. This is being progressed by the NTA as part of the GDA Demand Management Strategy and Scheme, and which can be adopted by others. Along with a ramping up of sustainable transport alternatives, as reflected in previous recommendations arising from other sub-groups, the following summarise the next steps that must be taken to develop a deeper awareness and understanding of the challenges and opportunities ahead.

Recommendation 16: Encourage greater utilisation of remote working from hubs.

Recommendation 27: Raise consumer awareness on vehicle buying and ownership.

Recommendation 28: Enhance modelling tools to better understand the impacts of specific demand management measures as part of wider climate action initiatives.

Recommendation 29: Commission research to measure and model the cumulative and relative impacts of the recommendations.

It also recognises the importance of supporting captive car users through the following:

Recommendation 13: Develop further ‘supply side’ measures, particularly shared mobility interventions, to augment sustainable mobility alternatives in next iteration of SMP Action Plan.

Recommendation 14: Review existing incentives for encouraging uptake of EVs, with a focus on targeted supports for Captive Car Usages.

## 10. Recommendations

The Strategy is not a prescriptive set of top-down actions. It is clear from the deliberations of the expert sub-groups that this type of approach will not work. As such, it is a strategic framework intended to provide the policy direction required at a national level to achieve these objectives, identifying the wide range of options that could be deployed at national, regional and local level, and supporting, guiding and enabling the appropriate authorities and entities, both in the public and private domain, to take ownership and shape their own actions in a coherent, evidence-based manner. It is vital to the credibility of the Strategy that its implementation is demonstrably fair and equitable. This has been the approach taken in developing the Strategy and it will continue to be the approach taken to its implementation.

Broadly speaking, the recommendations are grouped to reflect those areas that are deemed to be of critical importance to effective demand management: the national planning system, taxation policy, local government, enabling legislation, and key sectors (freight, public, education, sports, business, tourism). The recommendations also reflect the supports needed such as availability of sustainable mobility or ‘supply side’ alternatives, public engagement, good data and the need for strong implementation and review mechanisms.

Implementation methods and capacity to deliver were a key factor in designing the recommendations from the outset of the Strategy. The recommendations are summarised below and further detailed in an Implementation Plan, which is appended to this Strategy (Appendix 3). The Plan provides additional details on the steps envisaged under each recommendation, the timelines, ownership, and outputs involved.

The 32 recommendations set out are the product of a constructive, collaborative and concerted effort across a broad range of Government departments, members of the National Steering Group, expert sub-groups and feedback from the public consultation and engagement processes, to identify the type of interventions that can cumulatively make a lasting and positive impact.

## Delivery and Implementation

## 11. Delivery and Implementation

### Cost Implications of Delivering this Strategy

*Pages 129–129*

Cost Implications of Delivering this Strategy

A strategy’s value and impact depend on its capacity for implementation. As part of the development of this Strategy, delivery was a key consideration from the start of the process, with an emphasis on understanding the capacity and capability of government, business, industry and the citizen to get behind and implement measures where required. The Strategy points to the need for further analysis in this regard, particularly a need for implementing authorities to assess the feasibility of certain measures for their local areas – especially where they are being introduced for the first time in an Irish context. Guidance on demand management schemes will have a crucial role to play in shaping this analysis.

While the Strategy presents no immediate costs for the Exchequer (beyond the proposed identification of funds envisaged in Recommendation 31 to support demonstration and capacity building), it is anticipated that there will be government and economy-wide costs associated with pivoting towards an environment that supports more sustainable transport options – although these are not quantified within this document. For example, the Strategy encourages effort across the private and public sector to develop Smarter Travel Programmes and to seek recognition for this effort by applying to the Smarter Travel Mark. These initiatives would have cost implications for the organisations involved but these are likely to be met from existing resources.

The Strategy identifies who is responsible for implementing measures and what legislative powers exist or may be needed to ensure the delivery of recommendations, including the timescales involved, as well as the enforceability of the options proposed. It considers the acceptability of the proposals, from a political and public perspective, and suggests supporting measures to improve acceptability where needed such as through communications, public engagement and further supply-side measures and incentives.

There are many interventions that have upfront costs but are cost neutral over a period of time. For example, while new infrastructure and technologies would be needed to implement roaduser charging, it is likely that such costs would be recouped over the long-term. The costs and benefits of such an approach would need to be fully examined and considered by Government. Similarly, there would be administrative costs associated with developing any legislation for new or enhanced demand management measures but the model deployed for operating related systems could be cost neutral or have a negative cost due to the likely co-benefits of increased air quality and improved health along with the potential to invest any revenue that might be raised from interventions into, for example, enhanced public transport services. An analysis of all costs - Exchequer, environmental but also the regulatory burden on citizens and business as appropriate – will need to be undertaken as and when specific measures are brought forward for consideration by local councils or by Government. The cost of doing nothing will also require consideration.

The Strategy considers whether relevant ‘owner’ organisations are sufficiently resourced or competent to carry out functions effectively, particularly in new areas of policy or where more innovative interventions are required. Consideration is also given to financial resourcing and whether current funds, revenue raising powers or incentives are sufficient to support the likely new measures.

Finally, the Strategy has taken account of what is needed to achieve the strategic objectives, the relative impacts and challenges of measures involved, the likely high-level quantum of associated costs, and the timing and sequencing of measures relative to ‘supply-side’ interventions, particularly those being funded under the National Development Plan 2021-2030, which was recently revised as part of a commitment under the Programme for Government 2025.

As mentioned earlier in the Strategy, analysis undertaken by the Department of Transport estimates that the cost of congestion in our cities is expected to rise substantially by 2040. Congestion is a symptom of an inefficient system, one that is at capacity at certain times. These costs do not consider the health costs associated with sedentary travel, air pollution (inside and

### EU Taxonomy

*Pages 130–130*

outside of the vehicle) and stressful travel. Neither do these costs take account of the fines that will be applied to Ireland for not meeting its legally binding climate targets by 2030.

successful delivery of recommendations, ensuring a strong focus on Value for Money.

EU Taxonomy

This aim of this Strategy is to communicate the high-level direction, consisting of a set of guiding principles, that will define the type of the actions that implementing authorities may take out to 2030 and beyond. It aims to identify where potential barriers to implementation exist and sets out a work programme to remove them. As it is not prescriptive on the nature of measures to be taken by implementing bodies, the full cost of measures to be deployed is not yet know.

The EU Taxonomy for sustainable activities, established by EU Regulation 2020/852, is a classification system designed to guide investments towards environmentally sustainable economic activities. It aims to clarify what constitutes a sustainable activity and, in doing so, support the European Green Deal’s goals of carbon neutrality and a circular economy. The regulation, which came into force in July 2020, lays the groundwork for the taxonomy, while the European Commission is elaborating on it through delegated and implementing acts.

Value for Money

Any future funding associated with this Strategy will be contingent on compliance with the appropriate expenditure, legislative and value for money frameworks including the Public Financial Procedures, Regulatory Impact Analysis Requirements, and the Infrastructure Guidelines.

The European Commission’s website provides a useful tool, the Taxonomy Compass, which sets out Taxonomy conditions by sector including for transport36.

To comply with the Taxonomy, an economic activity must:

This Strategy sets out the pathway for future demand management measures. Agencies and LAs will finalise implementation plans for various Demand Management strategies in liaison with the relevant departments. These schemes will encompass the principles of this Strategy and will be developed alongside national guidance as per recommendations 4 and 5. Where programmes emerge, a cost-benefit model can be used to assess the viability of a range of proposals and the relative impact of potential options. Relevant departments will work with the Department of Public Expenditure Infrastructure, Public Sector Reform and Digitalisation in relation to ensuring appropriate project appraisal at all stages of the project lifecycle. It is not envisaged that the Strategy will give rise to any schemes or projects over the €200m threshold requiring Government approval. Nonetheless, all necessary procedures outlined in the project lifecycle contained in the Infrastructure Guidelines will be followed in progressing individual projects.

1. Make a substantial contribution to one or more of six environmental objectives and do no significant harm to all others. The environmental objectives are as follows:

a. Climate change mitigation

b. Climate change adaptation

c. Sustainable use and protection of water and marine resources

d. Transition to a circular economy

e. Pollution prevention and control

f. Protection and restoration of biodiversity and ecosystems37

2. Meet technical screening criteria – contained in the delegated acts and relating to how to perform the above contributions in practice.

3. Meet minimum social safeguards, namely complying with human rights and labour standards.

As part of this Strategy, the Department will work closely with relevant stakeholders to develop an appropriate governance structure to provide the necessary oversight and management required for

### Financing Options

*Pages 131–131*

over the next few years, companies and financial services firms are expected to develop systems to comply with these requirements efficiently. This could propel the Taxonomy into becoming a leading-standard setter in sustainability in the Irish and European economies.

The process to increase the monitoring of sustainability within public expenditure and increase the sustainable use of public funds, in general, is essential regardless of whether the framework underpinning it is a domestic one or the EU Taxonomy. Government should aim to lead by example in applying sustainability best practice and monitoring the sustainability of expenditure throughout its life-cycle.

Though the Taxonomy regulation is developed for use by the private sector, there are applications for it in the public sector in terms of tagging public expenditure in the green budgeting process.

The system for climate-tracking EU budget funding from the Recovery and Resilience Facility provides an example of how determining and monitoring climate and sustainability contributions of government expenditure might look under the Taxonomy.

Financing Options

This Strategy also includes a recommendation aimed at exploring broader financing options for helping transport to transition to a cleaner and greener people-centered system. Among the options that may be explored are development contribution schemes, retail parking levies, European funding schemes, adoption of internal protocols within implementing bodies for harnessing private investment and financing supports from the European Investment Bank.

## 12. Evaluation and Review

The success of this Strategy will be measured against its objectives. Measuring such success is complex because this Strategy does not stand alone. Firstly, it is one of two key policy documents involved in the effort to reduce vehicle kilometres travelled - by reducing transport demand (this Strategy) and by increasing the opportunities for shifting to more sustainable journeys (the Sustainable Mobility Policy). This Strategy relies on the implementation of the SMP and the SMP’s objectives will be heavily assisted by this Strategy.

means to people in Ireland. For example, the Wellbeing Framework 2023 provides policymakers with a more holistic way of thinking about the success of policies and consists of eleven dimensions which cover outcomes associated with sustainable quality of life. The most recent report under the Framework, Understanding Life in Ireland 2025: The Well-Being Framework 2025, states that ‘Ireland’s Well-being initiative seeks to inform Government priorities and policy objectives, and to think about longer-term challenges in a new way, by reviewing economic, social and environmental outcomes together. It places an explicit focus on sustainability and equality.’

Equally, a reduction in kilometres travelled, and associated emissions reduction, will not happen unless other conditions are also favourable e.g., the proliferation of compact settlements, transport oriented planning, people-centred design, public sector leadership, appropriate location of schools, hospitals, health centres, libraries etc.

Having defined well-being in broad terms, the Department of Transport’s Spending Review paper (2023) advises that it is important to consider the transport sector’s contribution to well-being and how transport can enable people to live meaningful lives. While it is difficult to connect the provision of transport infrastructure and services to the dimensions of the Well-being Framework, there are several ways in which the transport sector can both directly and indirectly impact on individual and societal wellbeing.

Given the complexities involved, it is proposed to incorporate a wellbeing approach to the monitoring and evaluation of this Strategy.

Assessing the well-being impact of public policy interventions has begun to gain prominence both internationally and in Ireland in recent years. The focus on well-being has emerged in response to a recognition that tracking indicators such as economic growth, while useful, does not provide a holistic view of what is intrinsically valued by a society. As set out in the report ‘Beyond GDP – Quality of Life Assessment’, Government has come a long way in understanding what quality-of-life

For example, transport directly affects the Built Environment in many ways, particularly its role in shaping how land is used and how public spaces and neighbourhoods are designed. Within this context, developments need to take account of the implications for nature restoration and climate adaptation.

![Figure 17: Well-being framework](assets/p133-f01.webp)

*Figure 17: Well-being framework*

### Air Quality

*Pages 134–134*

Transport also impacts the Built Environment by influencing the proximity of users to transport services and the accessibility and choice of those services.

used to assess transport policies and projects from such a perspective – utilising the associated policy analysis tool developed by the Department of Public Expenditure Infrastructure, Public Sector Reform and Digitalisation. This tool provides a step-by-step guide to analysing the well-being impact within a specific policy area.

The transport sector also indirectly impacts several other dimensions of well-being. At a societal level, the provision of transport is responsible for negative externalities including road deaths and accidents and the production of greenhouse gases and local air pollution.

The OECD notes that improving the monitoring of metrics related to overall Transport GHG emissions, sustainable accessibility, transport affordability, safety and security and local air pollution will help assess the well-being impacts of climate policy interventions across a range of dimensions of well-being.

In addition to these societal level impacts, the transport sector is also responsible for a number of impacts on quality of life and well-being at the individual level, including time-use, and the degree to which people are enabled to use their time effectively by the transport sector.

The evaluation of the Strategy will also have regard to the Just Transition Framework. This Framework was set out in the Climate Action Plan 2021 to guide approaches to implementing climate action policies, through the following key principles:

For example, long commutes are linked to lower levels of physical activity, lower job satisfaction, and a reduction in subjective quality of life.

The transport sector can also have an impact on the physical and mental health dimension of well-being. By enabling the use of active modes such as walking and cycling, the transport sector can influence the levels of physical activity and support positive health outcomes. In addition to time use, another transport factor which can influence an individual’s subjective well-being is their satisfaction with their mode of transport - this depends on a variety of factors including the safety, comfort, and cleanliness of the mode of transport.

ᴏ An integrated, structured, and evidence-based approach to identify and plan our response to just transition requirements.

ᴏ People are equipped with the right skills to be able to participate in and benefit from the future net zero economy.

ᴏ The costs are shared so that the impact is equitable and existing inequalities are not exacerbated.

ᴏ Social dialogue to ensure impacted citizens and communities are empowered and are core to the transition process.

The transport sector can also contribute indirectly to other dimensions. By enabling access to key services and amenities such as healthcare, educational facilities and employment opportunities, transport can impact on dimensions such as Connection to Community and Participation, Work and Job Quality and Income and Wealth. These impacts are evident when considering the transport sector through an accessibility rather than a mobility lens.

Taking all of the above into account, the following metrics will be used in assessing this Strategy:

Air Quality

Air Quality is one of the greatest environmental health risks facing Ireland today. In recent years, the EPA has significantly upgraded its national monitoring network. This monitoring network allows the EPA to access localised, up-to-date air quality information. There are two key drivers of localised air pollution in Ireland - solid fuel and traffic. Solid fuel is the main source of particulate matter (PM10 and PM2.5) which are the air pollutants that are most detrimental to human health.

Building on the OECD’s Redesigning Ireland’s Transport for Net Zero Report, which recommended that Ireland move away from targets based on enhanced mobility and instead target sustainable accessibility in order to enhance well-being, this Strategy aims to identify appropriate metrics for the transport sector which, in addition to more traditional metrics, could be

### Percentage of Urban Road Space Designated to Vehicles

*Pages 135–135*

Transport, particularly from congested traffic, is a significant contributor to nitrogen dioxide (NOx) which is more of an issue in towns and cities. Given the localised impact that many demand management measures could have, the EPA’s monitoring and reporting of air quality to ensure compliance with limits under the EU’s Ambient Air Quality Directive provides the basis for assessing the potential impacts of any interventions, particularly in areas at risk of exceedances. As such, the annual Air Quality in Ireland Report will be used to track progress in improving transportrelated air quality.

undertaken nationally have a duration of less than 30 minutes (74%). Tracking the number of journeys over a specific distance would be useful too e.g. journeys of less than 2km.

The NHTS also includes respondents’ selfreported 15-minute walk time to a variety of amenities such as a health centre, post-office or public transport stop. This could also be used to track the relationship with transport and landuse planning including concepts such as the 15-minute city.

Both surveys have merits, and the latest figures for 2019 - 2024 will serve as the baseline for measuring future performance. In December 2023, the CSO launched their new Transport Hub which will replace the Transport Omnibus and will house all their transport-related statistics in one area for ease of access and will be a useful resource for tracking performance in meeting the objectives of this Strategy. For example, TII traffic counter data is now available, and odometer readings from the NCT will be available in the Hub. Statistics on a broad range of topics, including journey times, are also integrated into the Regional Development Monitor Hub maintained by the Regional Assemblies in collaboration with the All Island Research Observatory and Ordnance Survey Ireland (via GeoHive).

From an acute health perspective, changes in acute hospital admissions for respiratory (asthma & COAD) and cardiac conditions may also be evaluated over time.

NTA National Household Travel Survey

The NTA’s National Household Travel Survey (NHTS) is a nationwide study of Ireland’s travel habits. From 2023 onwards, this is an annual survey and as such will give the most up-to-date information. The CSO’s National Travel Survey (NTS) is also worth utilising although there are a number of similarities between both.

The CSO’s NTS was last carried out in 2019, and the next iteration is expected shortly. The sample for this survey is stratified using administrative county and population density. The sample used in the NTA’s NHTS is weighted using population estimates from the CSO Labour Force Survey (LFS).

Economic Cost of Congestion

As outlined earlier in this Strategy, the department prepares regular reports that detail the likely economic cost of congestion to Irish cities, most recently the Economic Cost of Congestion in the Regional Cities 2022 – 2040.

The NHTS collects information on distance, duration and mode of transport used, making it a useful tool for measuring success. For in-depth analysis, it looks at travel in six regions Dublin City and suburbs, the GDA, the regional cities, urban towns, rural areas and other urban districts. The most recent NHTS highlights the dominance of car travel, with seven in 10 (71%) trips being made by car across Ireland. This is in line with the 2022 Census results.

Future studies by the Department will capture any changes in trends related to congestion – positive or negative - following the implementation of demand management measures.

Percentage of Urban Road Space Designated to Vehicles

As road space reallocation is expected to play a significant role in transport demand reduction, it is proposed to explore a mechanism whereby LAs would catalogue their public space on an annual basis to better understand how such space is used and to track reductions in the percentage of public space dedicated to private and commercial

Tracking the profile of private car journeys is also important. As well as tracking the distance and duration of journeys as part of the National Travel Survey (NTS), the NHTS collects information on the distance, duration and mode used. For example, it notes that the majority of car journeys

### Distance from Public Transport Services

*Pages 136–136*

Distance from Public Transport Services

vehicles compared to that for pedestrians, in particular, and also for cycling and shared mobility services.

In order to assess well-being under the Housing and Built Environment dimension of Ireland’s Well-Being Framework, the CSO tracks the Average Distance to Everyday Services. This indicator refers to the average distance of residential dwellings to everyday services in Ireland such as schools and hospitals and provides insights on the differences in service accessibility within urban and rural areas. This indicator also provides information on the average distance of households to their nearest bus stop. The results of this indicator shows that the average distance for a house to the nearest public transport stop was 2.7km. However, as expected, this distance was significantly lower for urban areas and higher for rural areas. This reflects the greater provision of public transport services in more densely populated urban centres.

Noise Harmful Effects Assessments

Noise caused by transport is considered the second most significant environmental cause of ill health in Western Europe and as such, the reduction of noise pollution will be a benefit of this Strategy. To assess the impacts of the transport sector on noise pollution, the department will review the work of LAs and the other Noise Mapping Bodies (NMBs) (e.g. TII) in undertaking harmful effects assessments as required under S.I. No. 663/2021, European Communities (European Noise) (Amendment) Regulations 2021.

Road Traffic Volumes

The Central Statistics Office (CSO) periodically produces Road Traffic Volumes data38. This Road Traffic Volumes data comes from two important datasets:

Separately, the CSO, in conjunction with Transport for Ireland (TFI) conducted an examination of the share of the population located within a variety of distance-based catchments from public transport stops, (Luas, Train and Bus stops), and also assessed the frequency of services provided at those stops. Results are based on the 2016 Census and the Transport for Ireland Network survey in 2019. In addition to providing information on the national average distance to access public transport services, this metric also provides insights for specific cohorts of the population, such as the distances households who do not own a car or van, or older people aged 66 and over, have to travel to access public transport services. These indicators show that on average older people tend to be located further away from public transport services compared to the population as a whole. Conversely, households who do not own a private car or van tend to be located nearer to public transport services. This has important implications for monitoring wellbeing as ensuring that transport is accessible to older population cohorts and those who may not have access to alternative private transport modes.

1. The number of vehicles in the fleet

2. Regular odometer readings from roadworthiness tests

The number of vehicles in the fleet are obtained from the National Vehicle and Driver File (NVDF), administered by the Department of Transport.

Odometer readings are available from two separate administrative sources; the National Car Testing (NCT) Service and the Commercial Vehicle Roadworthiness Testing Service. These sources are linked to the NVDF by vehicle registration number. Odometer readings are typically captured for private cars (four years old or more) at least every second year. Odometer readings are typically captured for commercial goods vehicles, public service vehicles and other selected categories of vehicles on an annual basis.

Given that the principal unit of measurement used to assess Road Traffic Volumes is vehicle kilometres travelled (VKT), and one of the main metrics in CAP is a reduction in 2030 VKT, Road Traffic Volumes data will be used to assess the effectiveness of this Strategy and various measures rolled out in the coming years. Information on average Vehicle Kilometres Travelled is available on the CSO’s Transport Hub here.

### Safety Indicators

*Pages 137–138*

Public Transport Accessibility Levels

This data shows that a greater proportion of Irish transport expenditure was spent on transport services compared to other peer countries. Only the United Kingdom is recorded as spending a greater proportion of transport expenditure on transport services over the period 2011-2021.

While the indicators discussed above provide an indicative sense of the level of sustainable accessibility provided by investments in public transport and active travel infrastructure and services, they have issues such as the use of selfreported data, or infrequent reporting periods.

This indicator can be used to provide useful insights on the accessibility of the transport system.

There is therefore a need for an indicator based on observed data, which is updated on a regular basis to provide insights into the level of sustainable accessibility provided by the transport network.

Safety Indicators

Road safety is another important aspect of wellbeing within the transport sector. Currently, data on the number of road fatalities per year is tracked by the Road Safety Authority (RSA) and reported as an indicator under the Safety and Security dimension of the CSO’s well-being dashboard. This indicator is also used to assess the performance of investment in the road network. However, as more road-space is reallocated to active travel, in order to achieve the walking and cycling modal shift targets set out in CAP23, and in order to support the monitoring of wellbeing when assessing the performance of Programme A, which relates to the provision of cycling and walking infrastructure, it may be useful to highlight the number of walking and cycling deaths or serious injuries that occur on Irish roads. These deaths could be reported as a proportion of the total number of fatalities.

A potential source of this indicator is the Public Transport Accessibility Levels (PTAL) tool. This tool was initially developed by Transport for London (TfL) to assess the level of accessibility provided by the transport network and is currently being adopted for an Irish context by the NTA39. The PTAL tool measures the connectedness of an area combining the walking/cycling journey time to a public transport stop and the level of service at that stop. In general, an area will have a higher PTAL score if:

ᴏ It is a short walk to the nearest station or stop

ᴏ There are short waiting times

ᴏ Multiple services pass by the stop

In the five years between 2021 and 2025, Active Travel Road User Fatalities (cyclists and pedestrians) accounted for roughly 1 in four (27.7%) of all road user deaths. During this fiveyear period, the proportion of Active Travel Road User fatalities ranged from 19.6% in 2021 to 32.2% in 2022.

ᴏ A major rail station is located in the area

Affordability Indicators

Another important element of the concept of well-being related to climate policy is ensuring that sustainable modes are affordable to transport users. This can be assessed through examining the proportion of total household expenditure spent on transport over time. As mentioned in the Department of Transport’s Spending Review 2023, the OECD reports on the percentage of a household budget spent on transport, which includes expenditure on private vehicles and operational expenditure on personal transport equipment.

Every Citizen is entitled to use our roads without concern for their safety. Tragically, road fatalities have significantly increased in the last number of years. As per the RSA’s 2025 provisional review of fatalities, there were 185 road fatalities in 2025, the highest since 2014 when the figure was 192.

Analysis of the provisional data for 2025 show that there were 174 fatal collisions resulting in 185 fatalities, representing an increase of 8% in deaths compared to the previous year.

The OECD also provides data on the proportion of household expenditure which is spent specifically on transport services such as the purchase of bus and rail tickets.

39 ABTA, How To Guide, Guidance Document - Pilot Methodology

By reporting on the proportion of active travel fatalities that occur, the Department of Transport can acknowledge trends in fatalities in a subgroup of road users which is important not only due to the tragic loss of life of the individuals involved but also because of the future needs and ambitions of the Department of Transport to decarbonise the transport sector.

The Transport Infrastructure Ireland, “Travelling in a Woman’s Shoes” Report found that creating a safer travelling environment and improving the perception of safety was particularly important for encouraging more women to engage in sustainable mobility. As above, the National Household Travel Survey (NHTS) captures data on the travel behaviour of the Irish public across different demographics including gender, and can be used to track any emerging trends particularly in travel by women.

While the tracking of adverse safety outcomes, such as the number of annual road deaths, provides useful information to support the monitoring of well-being, it is also important to assess factors which contribute to subjective well-being when travelling. One of these factors is perception of safety when using a transport service.

More broadly, it should be noted that CAP23 also introduces more detailed modal split targets setting out specific growth rates for the number of journeys undertaken by sustainable mode. This is aligned with adopting a sustainable accessibility approach to monitoring the implementation of the Climate Action Plans. The evaluation of the Climate Action Plans’ performance will also be reflected in the evaluation of the Strategy. The following represents the KPIs in CAP23 relevant to this Strategy.

## Case Studies

### 1 Sligo County Council:

*Pages 140–145*

An essential element of the Strategy is recognising existing and ongoing success, which can be harnessed to deliver impact. While the concept of demand management may be in its infancy in Ireland, there are many national and international examples of successful implementation in this area – examples that could be leveraged by other local authorities and agencies to achieve the objectives set out in this Strategy. The following represents a sample summary of case studies that were gathered to inform this Strategy and published as part of the public consultation process in 2024. As well as a case studies compendium, the subgroup reports featured many relevant examples of learnings from practice40.

Background

In 2023, as part of the Pathfinder Programme, Sligo partnered with Bolt to extend their shared e-bike scheme. Part of the motivation for this project was to make Sligo a “3km Cycle Friendly Town”. The Bolt e-Bike pilot had initially begun in July 2022. Initially intended as a 6-month pilot, it was extended for 12 months due to its popularity and success. The trial scheme included 40 designated bike parking areas around Sligo Town, with 80 bikes. The trial was successful, and has now been further extended, with additional charging stations, and more than 10,216 unique users recorded as of May 2025.

National Case Studies

Measure Impact

The pilot sought to understand if a shared e-bike scheme could encourage the desired modal shift from private car usage towards sustainable transport within Sligo. Since its introduction, over 194,844 trips have been taken on the e-bikes, travelling over 371,534 kms (as of May 2025). The average journey is 9 minutes and covers a journey of 2km. The high uptake and usage following the pilot demonstrated the success of the scheme and helped to justify the case for an active travel corridor connecting Carraroe, Sligo Town and Atlantic Technological University which is currently being developed.

1. Sligo County Council: Active Travel

Sligo County Council established a partnership with mobility companies, to provide a new active travel offering to its citizens and tourists with minimal cost to the local authority. Through this partnership, Sligo gained access to data to support future decision making on further provision of active travel infrastructure.

Lessons Learned

Background

Utilising a pilot approach is an effective tool for delivering impact in a short timeframe and can be easily replicated through the continuation and broadening of current schemes. Additionally, shared mobility including e-bikes, scooters or on-demand shared transport offer the benefits of geocoding and GPS tracking to collect detailed trip data. This is helpful to understand where travel demand exists and can be used to support the justification of more sustainable infrastructure, as seen with the planned active travel corridor connection between Sligo Town and ATU.

Cork City Council pedestrianised the streets of Cork City centre temporarily in the summer of 2020 as part of ‘Reimagining Cork City’ programme. In January 2021, the seventeen streets in Cork City centre were permanently pedestrianised.

Initially, the scheme was established in response to the Covid-19 pandemic and the need for accessible public space – reallocating road space so that pedestrians could move about at a safe distance from each other. Good air quality is an important long-term objective for Cork City Council. The pedestrianisation initiative not only supports public health, but also creates a liveable and people-friendly city. Cork also hosted its first car-free day on September 23rd, 2023, in the city centre as part of a pilot initiative. The car-free area stretched from Grattan Street in the west of the city centre as far as the Custom House building in the east of the centre, a total of 1.7km by car.

2. Cork City Council: Pedestrianisation and Carfree Initiatives

Cork City Council reallocated road space to active travel and pedestrians across the city centre, utilising a variety of funding streams. These pilots allowed Cork City to establish an evidence base (e.g. measured improvements in air quality) and to take on board learnings in order to better refine future iterations of these initiatives.

Measure Impact

Both initiatives provided the public with the opportunity to reimagine a different reality for the city centre and to understand how the absence of cars on the pedestrianised streets could improve the day-to-day experience of residents and

![Figure from page 141](assets/p141-f01.webp)
