Figure 19: Map of cork city pedestrianised streets

visitors alike. As part of the car free day pilot, air quality impacts were analysed via air-node sensors installed at appropriate locations. The sensors measure nitrogen oxide (NO2), which is strongly associated with traffic-related emissions. At midday on 23 September 2023, the car-free day pilot commenced, and within 10 minutes, there was a recorded reduction in NO2 of 50%. Over the next 50 minutes, the sensors recorded further reductions in NO2 until 3pm. From 3pm, the levels of NO2 steadily rose, before returning to typical pre-car free day levels by 5pm.

Blackrock. The combination of these projects entitled Living Streets is in essence a 10-minute town model. A key part of the Living Streets case study is the 4.5km coastal mobility route (CMR), which links five villages along the South Dublin coast between Blackrock to Sandycove.

Measure Impact

DLRCC worked with Technological University Dublin (TUD) to develop an evaluation framework to define what data needs to be collected for monitoring and analysis. The independent evaluation of the CMR by TUD found that the route demonstrated positive impacts both in terms of mobility and from a socio-economic perspective. It is the highest performing cycle route in the DLR area, with bike trips increasing through the provision of quality cycle infrastructure. In terms of business and economic impact, businesses interviewed along the CMR were largely positive regarding its economic impact and accepted or supported its ongoing presence. Several businesses noted an increase in footfall.

Recommendations and Lessons Learned

These initiatives are replicable across all scales and settlement types but there is a need for monitoring expertise. By collaborating with institutions such as universities, there is the possibility to access equipment, skills and resources which may not otherwise be available in the local authority. Vehicle restrictions on some streets can lead to more air pollution on other streets, where the air flow is less. Using low-cost sensors for street scale monitoring can provide enhanced data.

The Blackrock main street redesign also had significant stakeholder support with 84% and 72% of surveyed residents and businesses, respectively, confirming the new layout is a good addition.

Lessons Learned

3. Dun Laoghaire-Rathdown County Council:

A lesson learnt from implementing the schemes in Blackrock and Dun Laoghaire is the need to use tactical urbanism or pilots to illustrate the benefits of a scheme. It is important to use language that highlights the benefits to the community – focus on improving the streets and green spaces. Regular engagement helps to bring people along on the journey, furthering support. Having strong input and buy-in from resident or business community is important and valuable to success.

Public Realm and Coastal Mobility Route

Background

DLRCC introduced initiatives including segregated cycleways, public realm design, and reconfigured vehicle circulation to support pedestrians and cyclists in the area Dun Laoghaire as well as

Engaging with a single representative body (e.g. a chamber of commerce) can be useful, while seeking broader inputs. One recommendation is to manage the narrative of the scheme, in terms of highlighting the long-term success and positive qualities that the scheme brings. Telling the story well creates buy-in.

Dun-Laoghaire Rathdown County Council (DLRCC) has taken a multi-disciplinary approach to public realm improvements and the enhancement of active travel infrastructure, drawing on expertise across communications and accessibility to complement technical excellence. By prioritising engagement and leveraging realised success in other areas, they were able to secure buy-in and build public support across the Blackrock and Dun Laoghaire areas.

A multi-disciplinary team that can work without silos and understands the project is efficient in implementing the scheme. Ensuring universal design and engaging with disability or community groups on co-design of the scheme is crucial to ensure accessibility and inclusion.

4. Dublin City Council I: Parking Regulations

When existing schools apply for alterations and extensions, DCC seeks removal or reduction in car parking on site, the provision of cycle and scooter parking and the preparation of a School Travel Plan.

Dublin City Council utilises carparking policy as a tool to deliver modal shift and behavioural change in urban areas. In addition, Dublin City is actively innovating to support the optimisation of freight, introducing freight consolidation, last-mile green delivery and innovative technologies in the movement of goods in the city.

In recognition of hospitals as large employment centres with some in Dublin having thousands of staff, DCC has been working proactively to make hospital campuses more sustainable. Since the mid-2000s DCC has required hospitals to undertake car parking and staff travel surveys. DCC has worked with hospitals to develop sustainable mobility strategies for the hospital campuses with restriction of car parking and proactive car parking management central to the approach.

Background

Over the last two decades, Dublin City Council has embedded sustainable car parking policies in the city development plans with a particular emphasis on car parking as a mechanism to reduce congestion and encourage modal shifts. Limiting car parking at destination has been policy for Dublin City Council since the early 2000s, and this was strengthened by the 2005-2011 Dublin City Development Plan, which brought in maximum, accessibility-based car parking standards based on three zones across the city. DCC has been working to remove free on-street carparking over the past two decades and on-street Pay and Display schemes have been implemented in efforts to reduce long term on-street commuter parking in favour of short-term parking. Car clubs have also been a new feature of shared mobility over the last decade to address changes to car parking at destination. Regarding residential car parking, maximum accessibility-based standards are also applicable to residential developments. DCC has developed an integrated approach to mobility planning for new residential developments in collaboration with the developers of the sites41. For each site, an integrated mobility strategy is developed.

Measure Impact

The implementation of all these policies results in minimal car parking at destination, maximum accessibility-based standards at origin and the introduction of sophisticated travel and mobility planning in schools, businesses and hospitals. In the case of business use, the policies have been effective mechanisms for behavioural change.

Dublin City Council II: Kerbside Management Pilot

Background

Kerbside management refers to the planning, organisation and regulation of activities that take place at the edge of the roadways, specifically the areas adjacent to the kerb. The kerbside plays a crucial role in urban transportation as it provides space for various activities, including parking, loading and unloading, public transit stops, pedestrian facilities and cycling infrastructure.

With the growth in technology and new modes of transport, it has become increasingly important to manage competing demands on the kerbside in order to create an urban space where communities can thrive.

DCC works proactively with the Department of Education and Youth to develop sustainable mobility strategies for the new schools. New schools have been developed as car-free with only limited accessible car parking spaces. This approach is supported by the provision of highquality cycle and scooter parking provision, maximisation of permeability to the schools, improvements to the surrounding environs and the preparation of School Travel Plans.

Measure Impact

Led by Smart Dublin, two kerbside management platforms were trial and tested with different teams in Dublin City Council. Different data

41 Dublin City Development Plan, 2022 - 2028 - Volume 2:

Dublin City Council III: Micro Consolidation Centres (Eco Hubs)

sets that were either surveyed or pulled from open data sets and made available on the digital platform. The pilot captured more than 15 unique kerb regulations with 4000+ data points for 60+ kerb kilometres.

Background

Recommendations and Lessons Learned

A Micro Consolidation Centre refers to a strategically positioned logistics facility situated within the boundaries of the urban area, specifically focusing on Dublin City Centre. This hub serves a defined geographical scope and facilitates a transition to low-emission vehicles or alternative modes of transportation, such as walking and cargo bikes, for the final phase of product deliveries.

Keeping the kerbside maps up-to date posed as a real challenge for the pilot. Smart Dublin realised that the data could be kept updated in two ways (1) the City Council teams update the maps whenever any changes were made on the streets. This would require major process changes and would not be viable for a small pilot project; (2) A third party would be required to resurvey the area, under contract, on a quarterly basis using either LiDAR or mobile mapping.

The Dublin City Development Plan sets out policies and objectives to guide how and where development will take place in the city over the lifetime of the Plan. Under the plan for 2022- 2028, Dublin City Council commit to achieving a significant reduction in the number of motorised delivery vehicles in the city through supporting and promoting the use of the ‘last-mile’ delivery and the development of sustainable deliveries eco hubs and distribution centres.

It is also important for the local authorities to use common data standards to advance their kerb management journey. Smart Dublin, which is part of the Open Mobility Foundation (OMF), is also exploring Curb Data Specifications (CDS). CDS is developed as an open standard by a public Curb Management Working Group that began work in 2020 and includes 160+ leaders of major US cities, transit systems, global automotive and delivery enterprises, and technology startups.

![Figure 20: Kerbside management tool](assets/p144-f01.webp)

*Figure 20: Kerbside management tool*

Measure Impact

Dublin City Council published an Eco Hub Bye Law in 2018 under which a Sustainable Deliveries Eco Hub Operator (Delivery and/or Logistics Companies) can apply to the Council for a Licence to operate an on-street Sustainable Deliveries Eco Hub prior to commencing operation.

UPS (United Postal Services) is operating eco hubs successfully at two locations and through these hubs UPS has eliminated the use of five diesel delivery vehicles in the city centre, easing congestion and reducing CO2 emissions by up to 45% in 2020 .

Recommendations and Lessons Learned

Public space in Dublin is ever-changing and this can make it difficult for local authorities to accommodate the demand of public space to host the eco hub. Hence, Dublin City Council is now seeking solutions to facilitate the sustainable deliveries of eco hubs and distribution centres in off-street locations in Dublin City by working closely with the delivery companies and infrastructure providers.

### International Case Studies

*Pages 146–152*

International Case Studies

The WPL charge started at £288 per annum per liable car parking space and was increased each year by inflation along with a three-year accelerator. The cost per space for the 2023/24 period is £522. Nottingham City Council included certain exemptions and discounts in the Scheme.

Nottingham, UK: Workplace Parking Levy

Measure Impact

Nottingham, UK, addressed carparking through the introduction of a workplace parking levy in 2011 which currently operates at 100% compliance. A lengthy process for the city, it took four years to get from business case to implementation. Nottingham prioritised public and industry engagement throughout the planning and development process and continued this through to implementation, providing access to supports for businesses in travel and transport planning.

As the Nottingham WPL was introduced as part of a wider strategy to reduce car use, the impact of the WPL in isolation cannot be identified. However, the impacts of the wider strategy have been monitored and reported, from which the likely impacts of the WPL can be discerned, for example:

ᴏ Liable parking space numbers reduced by 25% initially and then levelled out.

ᴏ 80% of employers now pass the levy charge onto employees – covering just over half of the liable spaces.

ᴏ The public transport improvements paid for by the levy create better alternatives to the car. Around 40% of workplaces are served by the tram and 55,000 employees use it to commute to work.

Background

ᴏ Traffic has declined by 9% since 2004.

Nottingham introduced a Workplace Parking Levy (WPL) in the city in 2011. WPL is a charge on the occupier of premises (typically the employer) for the number of parking spaces they provide that are used by employees, business customers, business visitors, and anyone parking in the course of their business or education (including students). A WPL is a pricing mechanism intended to help directly manage car demand and generate revenue streams that are often ringfenced to be invested in local sustainable transport. There is an incentive for employers who are subject to the levy to reduce the number of parking spaces they provide.

ᴏ Reducing car parking demand leads to redevelopment potential on brownfield sites, which reduces demand on greenfield sites which are normally more difficult to serve by sustainable modes. This has been very important for some large employers such as the two universities.

ᴏ The number of employers with travel plans has doubled since 2012 – one third of larger employers now have travel plans.

There is no evidence of any significant negative economic impact in Nottingham. WPL has raised almost £90 million in 10 years.

Multiple benefits are associated with WPL, including reduction in car trips and the associated air pollution, carbon emissions and traffic. These charges applied can generate funds that can be invested in sustainable transport and lower demand for car spaces providing opportunities for alternative land uses. Indirectly, a WPL can help manage the wider impacts of traffic on communities and the environment, such as air pollution and poor land utilisation.

Recommendations and Lessons Learned

There are several takeaways regarding the Nottingham Workplace Parking Levy, including:

ᴏ Parking levies are best applied in areas that are well suited to public transport investment or are already served by public transport, thereby offering an alternative.

ᴏ Timeline for introduction can be lengthy with a need to allow for constructive consultation. It is important to clearly identify the allocation of funds raised by the WPL, and ideally use the monies raised to invest in sustainable transport as an alternative to the car.

design and the ability to grow active travel mode share while supporting economic development. Amsterdam also runs two on-demand transport services in AMLflex and Mokumflex and takes an economic approach to the allocation of urban space. Public space is described as holding more economic value than ever before. Streets, squares and parks are not only a location requirement for residents and companies, but also a production environment.

Amsterdam

Amsterdam’s car and bicycle parking development standards encourage residents to own more bikes than cars, while their approach to the design of public spaces makes it simple and affordable to create high quality public spaces.

Amsterdam, the Netherlands has standardised and simplified the planning processes in urban areas to provide a transport system that prioritises sustainable modes. Sustainable mobility (via active travel and on-demand public transport) is seen as the preferred option to users due to the transparency of planning processes and the prioritisation of craftmanship, innovation and user experience across design and implementation.

Parking standards in Amsterdam are divided into three zones:

ᴏ “A localities” (purple), have very good public transport accessibility and easy access to intercity train stations, so the municipality only prescribes a maximum parking standard.

ᴏ “B localities” (grey), have good public transport, but are further away from intercity stations. In these locations, the municipality applies both minimum and maximum parking standards.

Background

The Amsterdam metropolitan area offers a lot of lessons learned to Ireland with regards to urban

![Figure 23: Zonal mapping of Amsterdam car parking standards](assets/p147-f01.webp)

*Figure 23: Zonal mapping of Amsterdam car parking standards*

![Figure 24: Zonal mapping of Amsterdam bicycle parking standards](assets/p148-f01.webp)

*Figure 24: Zonal mapping of Amsterdam bicycle parking standards*

ᴏ “C localities” (orange), are more rural areas that don’t have good public transport options. Parking numbers in these areas are more customised – the municipality may require that parking is provided on private property, but parking on the street may also be allowed.

Lessons Learned

The standardisation and simplification of urban design processes can improve the speed and quality of completed street redesigns. Framing sustainable transport and urban space developments from an economic perspective, attracting investment and young knowledge workers and encouraging retail and hospitality spending makes it easy to get stakeholders on board with changes to the status quo.

Bicycle parking standards similarly vary by area of the city, with Zones 1, 2, and 3. Bicycle parking is always provided as a minimum standard. For residential developments, individual bike storage facilities are the preferred parking method, but communal storage facilities are permissible. In practice, this typically means that individual storage is most logical for low-rise housing, while collective storage is most logical for multi-storey housing. All of these standards are higher than car parking standards, encouraging households to own multiple bikes, but not multiple cars.

Amsterdam’s parking standards that encourage residents to own more bikes than cars and require developers to support the municipality’s reduction of on-street car parking is a cost-effective way to reduce car usage and improve the quality of the public realm. These standards cost very little for the municipality to enforce, while allowing them to completely transform streetscapes without removing the net amount of parking in the city.

Measure Impact

On-demand transport may have its place in the more rural and peri-urban areas of Ireland, and the possibility of partnering with private operators may make this form of transport a cost-effective way of bringing public transport to areas where traditional public transport is infeasible.

Car vehicle-kilometres travelled has largely stayed the same since 1990, despite its population growing approximately 25%. This would indicate that growth is being facilitated by public transport and active travel, rather than by private car.

Copenhagen Metro and Cycle Superhighway

The Copenhagen Metro is a light rapid transit system throughout the three municipalities of Copenhagen, Frederiksberg and Tarnby. The system operates 24/7 and is a world leading public transportation system. The metro system is served by a network of driverless trains and does not operate on a timetable but rather has fixed intervals (between 2-4 minutes) depending on the time of the day.

Copenhagen has rolled out ambitious and extensive public transport and active travel projects in an effort to promote modal shift and reduce car usage in the city. The delivery, and extension of, the Copenhagen Metro and the development of cycle superhighways can act as two examples of how large projects can influence travel patterns.

The cycle superhighways in Copenhagen are a coherent network of cycle highways across the capital region, where the needs of cyclists are given the highest priority in the road hierarchy. A cycle superhighway is defined by its location and physical qualities. The highways connect work, study, and residential areas as well as public transportation hubs making it easy to combine a commute with public transport. The superhighway network ensures a safe, smooth ride with fewer stops and increased safety with the aim to increase the number of commuters who bike 5-30km daily.

Background

The expansion of the metro network and the development of cycle superhighways in Copenhagen have resulted in a significant shift from car use in the city, while encouraging a transport network which is sustainable, fast and safe for all ages, persons and abilities.

![Figure 25: Copenhagen metro line network map](assets/p149-f01.webp)

*Figure 25: Copenhagen metro line network map*

Measure Impact

Lessons Learned

Overall annual ridership of the metro system grew significantly between 2010 to 2019, from 52.5 million to 78.9 million. The expansion of the network, along with the existing network lines, has resulted in approximately 85% of citizens in Copenhagen having access to a metro or train station within 600 meters from their home, as well as being a part of the 75% of total resident journeys covered by foot, bicycle or public transport modes.

The use of public/private financing structures to develop a highly efficient and highly used metro network in the capital, which is possible through the re-development and renewal of urban areas and land, could be an avenue to support the heavy investment and financing required for transportation developments.

The development of cycle superhighways, combining new and existing cycle pathway infrastructure, could also work in the context of Ireland’s cities and their surrounding commuter belts. Through the improvement to existing cycling networks, there will be a reduction in travel disruptions caused by the provision of new routes.

After the development of the first 8 cycle superhighways in 2019, the following outcomes were found:

ᴏ 23% increase in number of cyclists when route is upgraded to a cycle superhighway.

Overall, across both projects, establishing clear frameworks, collaborations and partnerships were crucial for the successful implementation. User buy-in is equally important. Finally, the various forms of public and active travel modes in the city are well integrated to each other. Across the metro line, there are many stations which are shared by other s-train services in the city and are all well connected to bus routes and stops. Most stations on the network are fitted with bike racks, and bikes can be brought onto the Metro. The cross integration across the different transport offerings is something Ireland could seek to incorporate in their own travel network, to make the utilisation of multiple forms of public and active travel modes for users easier, and more accessible.

ᴏ 14% of the increase in cyclists are from new cyclists that previously travelled by car.

In terms of environmental benefits, the development of the 45 superhighways are projected to result in annual reductions of 1,500 tonnes of CO2 emissions per year, as well as leading to an avoidance of 2,500 kg of NOx emissions per year.

Outside of emissions reduction, wider benefits include reductions in hours spent in traffic, reductions in commute times, reductions in car journeys, reductions in public cost spend due to a reduction in sick days taken due to the wider health benefits of cycling and economic benefits resulting from reduced congestion.

New Zealand Road User Charging

Road user charging apples to both heavy duty vehicles (HDVs) and light duty vehicles (LDVs) in NZ. Under the current system, operators of all vehicles that do not use a fuel that is charged Fuel Excise Duty (FED) are subject to paying road user charges (note Petrol vehicles pay FED). Therefore, diesel vehicles and most recently LDV Electric Vehicles (EVs) pay road user charges. Currently, road user charges for LDVs are NZ $76 per 1000 Km prepaid licence for a vehicle <3500 kg (€0.043 cents/km). NZ petrol excise duty is currently charged at $0.70 per litre43 (approx. €0.043). The cost of road user charging for HDVs is greater (from €82 - €1,087 per 1,000 km) and varies depending on vehicle type and weight)44.

New Zealand has long been a pioneer of road user charging (RUC). Introduced in 1978, RUC in New Zealand is mature and the ringfenced revenue is used to maintain the country’s extensive road network and to fund the New Zealand Transport Program (NZTP).

Background

New Zealand (NZ) is one of the pioneers in road user charging having first introduced it in 197842. From its introduction which was initially driven by a need to generate revenue to fund road maintenance, the NZ road user charging system is now also a funding source for the land transport system. Its core concept is a road user charging approach based on vehicle’s distance travelled (i.e. it does not vary by time and location) and contribution of these funds to transport investment, operations and maintenance.

NZ provides a number of key considerations and potential lessons learnt for other jurisdictions, such as Ireland, considering the implementation of a road user charging system.

Measure Impact

The NZ road user charging system generates a significant revenue stream ($NZD 2 billion, equivalent to €1.1 billion, in the 2020/21 financial year). This revenue supports (and is ringfenced for) investment in road maintenance,

subsidies in public transport, enforcement, and road safety. The NZ Road user charging system materially contributes to the funding for broader National Land Transport Program (NLTP) objectives including transport infrastructure investment, maintenance and road safety.

alternative road user charging approaches such as tolling which generally costs considerably more.

Enforcement: The ‘honour system’ supported ᴏ by odometer readings and ad hoc Police checks is a simple and low-cost form of enforcement.

Furthermore, under the current system vehicles are charged according to their overall use of the road network and the associated costs they impose, this system has gained general public acceptance in terms of equity, and has now been in operation for almost 50 years.

Lessons Learned

Case studies such as this one are important for Ireland to consider in the context of electrification and the implementation of other transport policies. Key lessons learned from the NZ case study include:

Equity: The ability to accurately allocate ᴏ charges based on road usage and to reduce road user charging for exempt vehicles/users which are subject to FED promotes equity and fairness, ensuring that those who use the roads more contribute proportionally

EV Transition: NZ provides an example of a ᴏ climate action focussed jurisdiction which is implementing road user charging on alternative fuelled vehicles including LDV EVs (introduced 1st April 2024/brought forward from initial date of 2030) and HDV EVs (31 December 2025) whilst continuing to endeavour to encourage the EV transition.

Public Awareness: The importance of ᴏ effective communication, engagement and consultation with key stakeholders can help in fostering public acceptance, enhancing its effectiveness and legitimacy.

Sustainable Funding: Transparent mechanisms ᴏ for reporting on and allocating revenues associated with road user charging can enable the public to appreciate how transport infrastructure/ services are funded, benefiting all road users.

Economically efficient collection system: ᴏ The operational cost of road user charging in NZ represents c. 1% of the total road user charging revenue which compares very favourably to the operational cost of

## Ensuring a Just Transition through Investment

### Ensuring a Just Transition through Investment

*Pages 153–159*

![Figure from page 153](assets/p153-f01.webp)

*Figure from page 153*

Appendix 2 Ensuring a Just Transition through Investment

Keeping public transport affordable

Just transition is a core principle of this Strategy and will guide the implementation of the recommendations outlined in Appendix 3. More broadly, the Just Transition Framework is guiding the Department of Transport’s approach to implementing climate action policies, ensuring that investment is targeted to support people as the economy decarbonises.

ᴏ Approximately 1.6m (or 30% of the population) adults and children in Ireland can travel for free on PSO services, as well as on certain commercial bus services under the Free Travel Scheme

While Just Transition primarily refers to an equitable transition for those people whose jobs/ employment are directly impacted by the green transition, it also encompasses wider equity, justice and wellbeing considerations as well as the ‘leave no-one behind’ principle, which is enshrined under the United Nation’s Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). By making sustainable forms of transport more affordable and accessible, workers can be shielded from the impacts of decarbonisation in terms of cost and provided with greater opportunities for engaging in and taking up roles/jobs in the low-carbon economy of the future.

ᴏ An additional 1m people between the age of 9 and 25 benefit from significantly reduced fares, with those between 19 and 25 receiving a 50% discount with the Young Adult Card

ᴏ There is also no charge for people aged 16+ who travel as companions with persons over 70 years of age

ᴏ Therefore, circa 50% of the population have access to free travel or significantly reduced fares, while fares for the remainder of the population are also heavily subsidised

Key initiatives to reduce or eliminate fares have been put in place in recent years, including the introduction of the 90-minute fare, and the launch of the Young Adult Card. In 2025, free public transport was extended for children to include those aged 5 to 8 years. Free travel for all children under-five on subsidised public transport services was introduced in 2017.

Just Transition principles are also aligned to efforts to protect those who are transport poor, or those vulnerable groups or micro-enterprises who are most at risk of transport poverty and/ or the impact of rising fossil fuel prices as Ireland and Europe decarbonises. For this reason, it is recognised that Just Transition in transport can be best achieved by (1) keeping public transport affordable and (2) providing better infrastructure and services to more people and enterprises.

Taken alongside more longstanding initiatives such as the Department of Social Protection’s Free Travel Scheme for older and disabled people, these measures ensure that approximately 1.6 million people in Ireland can travel for free on PSO services and some commercial bus services. In September 2025, a new Free Travel Companion scheme was launched, meaning anyone over 16 years of age can travel for free when acting as a companion for persons aged 70 or over.

Following a revision of Ireland’s National Development Plan, as part of a commitment under the Programme for Government 2025, over €24 billion has been pledged for transport infrastructure over the period 2026-2030. In 2026, and to support the above objectives, the Department of Transport has been allocated more than €4.7 billion to progress the continued roll-out of active travel and public transport infrastructure as well as the continuation of enhanced Public Service Obligation (PSO) and Local Link services. The implementation of recommendations under the Moving Together strategy is heavily reliant on the concurrent delivery of feasible and affordable alternatives to the private car – and an environment that makes it easier for people to choose healthier, more efficient and less polluting journeys.

In addition to the foregoing, more than one million young people benefit from significantly reduced fares (those aged 9 to 25) while PSO fares for the remainder of the population are also heavily subsidised, by up to 64% on the cost per passenger (in 2023), through the Exchequer. The cost of using public transport can also be substantively reduced for employees through the Taxsaver Commuter Ticket Scheme.

Providing better infrastructure and services to more people

Collectively, these initiatives serve to make sustainable transport a more affordable option for the whole population, but particularly families, young people and those most vulnerable to fossil fuel increases. Average fares on all PSO transport services per passenger in Ireland remain low in comparison to other countries and are in line with the commitment in the Programme for Government to keeping fares low and affordable, while acknowledging the need to develop a sustainable funding model for PSO services into the future.

The 2025 Programme for Government commits to streamlining the delivery of major transport projects, from planning to execution and to providing extra funding for the improvement of transport networks countrywide to promote increased business investment in the economy and the supply of new housing. It also places a focus on linking new housing developments to public transport and schools through Transport Oriented Development, ensuring easy access for residents and visitors. Moreover, it commits to developing a new Sustainable Mobility Action Plan which promotes sustainable modes and transportoriented development to address congestion, improve air quality and reduce noise, and enhance attractiveness and accessibility of cities, neighbourhoods and town centres. The work to develop this Plan is underway and will underpin the following:

As well as keeping public transport more affordable, schemes like the Cycle to Work Scheme make it more affordable for employees to purchase bikes, e-bikes and cargo bikes, thus providing another avenue for offsetting the impacts of rising fossil fuel prices. Other, more targeted measures are also being trialled. For example, the Department of Transport, in conjunction with the Department of Rural and Community Development and the Gaeltacht, have introduced a pilot initiative (2022-2025) via Pobal’s Community Services Programme to provide high quality up-cycled bicycles and e-bikes to those on low incomes and/or are marginalised/ disadvantaged. Just Transition is at the heart of these schemes.

Addressing Public Transport Gaps in Rural Ireland

A key aspect of Just Transition is addressing challenges specific to rural areas. Rural Recognition, along with Just Transition, are among the key principles shaping this Strategy. It is well recognised that non-urban areas in Ireland experience hotspots of forced car ownership and transport disadvantage, including high levels of transport poverty. Under Ireland’s National Sustainable Mobility Policy, an enhanced rural transport network is being implemented through the 5-year Connecting Ireland Rural Mobility Plan. To date, 185 new and enhanced services have been introduced since the Plan was launched in 2022. New connections have been made to 240 towns and villages, 41 colleges, 61 hospitals and 71 new connections to onward rail services.

Ensuring equity of access to Primary and Post-Primary Education

As outlined in Chapter 5 of this Strategy, the School Transport Scheme is transporting over 181,000 children in 2026. This Scheme supports those households most at risk of transport poverty and ensures equity of access to education through the provision of low-cost school transport services i.e. annual fees for the service are currently €50 for primary students, €75 for post-primary students and €125 for a family ticket (for the 2025-2026 school year). There are no fees for children with special needs who avail of services under the Scheme. It is anticipated that access to the Scheme will be expanded in the coming years so that an additional 90,000 pupils can be carried by 2030 when compared with 2024. This will be facilitated through pilots that integrate school transport services with general public transport services as well as expanded eligibility criterion under the Scheme.

Funding for Rural Transport has seen a five-fold increase over the last decade, with commensurate increases in passenger numbers, rising from 1.76m in 2015 to 6.97m in 2025. The Programme for Government 2025 commits to increasing Local Link services in rural areas to better connect villages, towns, and cities, and continuing the rollout of Connecting Ireland Rural Mobility Plan and investment in new town bus services.

Continuous Investment in Public Transport Infrastructure

platforms to boost the rail network’s resilience and capacity. Funding is provided to support the full delivery of these projects by 2030.

IIreland is committed to the delivery of major public transport projects. That commitment is evidenced by last year’s review of the National Development Plan, which allocates approximately €10.1 billion for public transport infrastructure investment from 2026 to 2030, with a further €2 billion from the Infrastructure, Climate and Nature Fund specifically to start construction on MetroLink. This €12.1 billion investment marks a major increase in public transport capital funding, more than double the level seen in the previous five years.

The Strategy also identifies Major Projects to be progressed for the longer-run, involving intercity electrification and significant capacity upgrades on the rail network. Included among these Major Projects is the reinstatement of the Western Rail Corridor between Claremorris and Athenry, and funding is provided in the Sectoral Plan to advance this project into construction.

With their flexibility and adaptability, buses are the foundation of our public transport systems, and BusConnects is the programme to improve bus services in our cities. To date, BusConnects Dublin has made the most progress, with all 12 Core Bus Corridor schemes approved by An Coimisiún Pleanála. The Liffey Valley to the City Centre scheme is the first to enter construction, to be shortly followed by Ballymun / Finglas to the City Centre in the second half of 2026. BusConnects in the other cities is at various stages of advancement, and the National Development Plan is to support construction on new Sustainable Transport Corridors in Cork (subject to planning) and Galway. This will significantly improve the infrastructure available to support the continued investment in bus services, supported through the PSO programme.

In addition to the Government’s support for MetroLink through the provision of funding, the project has also seen other positive developments, such as the granting of the Railway Order. With planning permission and funding secured, significant momentum has been gained, with procurement commencing in 2026 and construction earmarked to start by the end of 2027.

Investment in the Sectoral Plan provides funding to advance the DART+ Programme into construction, starting with DART+ West to enter construction from 2027, which will act as a key enabler for the rest of the Programme.

Improving Accessibility of Services and Sustainable Options

In Cork, Phase 1 the Cork Area Commuter Rail Programme, which consists of a signalling upgrade, construction of a through-platform at Kent Station, which opened in April 2025, and double-tracking from Glounthaune to Midleton, will be completed in 2026. The Sectoral Plan provides funding to commence Phase 2 starting with new train stations at Blackpool and Dunkettle and initial electrification works and depot construction.

Accessibility and inclusion are central to the principle of Just Transition. In addition to making new transport infrastructure accessible from the design stage, Ireland’s Public Transport Accessibility Retrofit Programme is making legacy infrastructure and facilities accessible for everyone, including disabled people and older people, in line with SDG Target 11.2 and the UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities. The Programme consists of subprogrammes, including in rural and regional areas, to upgrade accessibility at bus and train stations, increase the number of wheelchair accessible taxis and provide supports to enable disabled people

The Rail Project Prioritisation Strategy, which was prepared with the assistance of the European Investment Bank, the Department of Infrastructure in Northern Ireland, the NTA and rail agencies across the island, was published in December 2025. This outlines how to best sequence and optimise the recommendations of the All-Island Strategic Rail Review which was launched in July 2024.

Accessible Local Link Services

The Strategy specifies short-term rail infrastructure investments, referred to as Early Interventions, to be delivered in the coming years, including a series of new track passing loops and

Expanding the Provision of Shared Mobility Options

to travel independently on public transport. The Programme was allocated €25m in 2025 which was a 67% increase on the 2024 allocation. This increased allocation will be maintained under the National Development Plan 2026-2030. Ireland’s National Human Rights Strategy for Disabled People 2025-2030 also contains a number of priority accessible transport actions, some in the area of the associated Built Environment and Active Travel. The Strategy emphasises the importance of using Universal Design and Whole of Journey principles, and engaging with disabled people to ensure accessible transport for everyone and especially for disabled people.

Shared mobility is recognised for its high transformative potential in transport decarbonisation, and in the sustainable and compact growth of our urban centres. Equally, shared mobility hubs can expand journeymaking choices, by providing easy access to a range of transport modes, such as bikes, e-bikes, e-scooters, e-cargo bikes and electric vehicles. The provision of shared mobility services and hubs can also help to reduce car ownership by offering a cost-effective alternative to owning a personal vehicle, alleviating the financial burden of car maintenance, insurance, and fuel.

Ongoing Investment in Active Travel Infrastructure

On-demand shared services have been operating in Ireland for some time. Over the last decade, publicly funded bike-sharing schemes have been rolled out across Ireland’s five cities. In addition to these publicly funded initiatives, a growing number of services are now being operated on a commercial basis under licence or contract with local authorities. For example, car-sharing services have been available in Ireland for over a decade and an increasing number of car-sharing companies are now in operation. Other companies are providing access to shared pedal bikes and e-bikes in towns across the country, with Ireland’s first shared e-scooter scheme launched in Wexford in August 2024.

Significant investment in walking and cycling infrastructure is set to continue into 2026 for walking and cycling projects (circa €1 million every day). Most of this funding is allocated to the National Transport Authority (NTA) for its Active Travel Infrastructure Programme the remainder is allocated to Transport Infrastructure Ireland (TII) for Greenways. As well as being a tourism proposition, Greenways also provide alternative and safe infrastructure in areas of the country that are transport poor and heavily reliant on cars. Many of the existing and planned Greenways routes pass though electoral divisions which have been scored as disadvantaged per the Pobal Deprivation Index.

Acknowledging the potential of shared mobility across multiple policy objectives, Ireland is working hard to expand the availability of related services. For example, the bike sharing schemes in the cities of Cork, Limerick, Galway and Waterford are being expanded, to encompass wider areas of operation and more modes (e.g. e-scooters, e-bikes, e-cargo bikes etc), with enhanced services coming on stream from late 2026. The 2025 Programme for government also commits to the more specific goal of coordinating bike and e-bike rental programmes across further and higher education campuses.

By investing in extensive active travel infrastructure and networks, those most impacted or vulnerable to fossil fuel increases can be provided with a safe and affordable alternative to private car use for short journeys. The 2025 Programme for Government reasserts the need for investment in dedicated infrastructure for walking and cycling, in consultation with communities and people of all abilities including older people and people with disabilities, to promote active travel across Ireland for those commuting or enjoying the countryside. It also commits to:

ᴏ increasing investment in greenways, blueways and pedestrian-friendly infrastructure,

ᴏ expanding the Safe Routes to School initiative, and

ᴏ providing a dedicated maintenance budget to each local authority to maintain active travel infrastructure, including footpaths.

Also, in keeping with a Programme for Government pledge to establish mobility hubs in every local authority area, a new Mobility Hub Pilot Programme is also being developed to test how mobility hub networks might be designed and operated on a long-term basis and to inform a model for more widespread delivery of hub networks nationwide. While the business case for the Programme is still in development, it is intended that approximately 300 hubs will be rolled out across five urban locations from 2027.

In respect of the latter i.e. the zero-emission heavy duty vehicles (ZEHDV) scheme, grants are made available to companies and enterprises who wish to buy zero emission heavy duty vehicles (instead of buying diesel equivalent). The scheme, which has a budget of €4m in 2026, provides greater grant aid to smaller companies verses larger companies (60% of the cost differential for small companies, 50% for medium companies and 30% for larger companies). Included in this budget is the Zero Emission Heavy Duty Vehicle Infrastructure (ZEHDVI) scheme, which supports companies and enterprises with the costs of charging infrastructure and related technical equipment. This ZEHDVI scheme also gives greater support to smaller companies - 50% of the cost for small companies, 40% for medium companies, 20% for larger sized companies. Similarly, the permissible funding levels for ZEHDVI allows for a 10%-15% uplift of funding for companies in disadvantaged areas (defined though Section 6 of Article 36 of GBER) - based on the company size and location of the investment relative to the regional aid map.45

These initiatives reflect the growing ambition for shared mobility to be a key player in Ireland’s transport landscape, given its potential to offer an affordable alternative to private car use.

Supporting captive car users and the transition to zero emissions vehicles

The Moving Together strategy acknowledges that many people are locked into car dependency due to various personal circumstances. Continuous investment in our road networks is vital to ensuring connectivity and safety for car drivers, as well as for bus travel, cycling and walking. The 2025 Programme for Government commits to increasing funding for new roads as part of the NDP review. It also commits to an increase in funds for the protection and renewal of the road network to address maintenance backlogs.

These supports underpin Ireland’s Regional and Local EV Charging Network Plan, which provides a pathway for the accelerated delivery of regional and local networks of public EV charging infrastructure in cities, towns and villages across Ireland, in line with both national and European ambitions for cleaner transportation. This Plan, which was published in 2025, ensures a cohesive and standardised approach and will be led by local authorities, working together to develop regional and local strategies to promote a unified and efficient rollout of charging infrastructure and to facilitate the equitable transition to EVs.

To ensure drivers and businesses also have the opportunity to access lower running costs through zero-emissions private or commercial transport, where alternative options are not available or feasible, a generous range of supports is currently in place to support a transition to electric. These include a purchase grant for battery electric vehicles (BEVs); a home charging scheme; an apartment charging scheme; a benefit-in-kind tax relief for BEVs and home chargers; a VRT relief of up to €5,000 for BEVs; accelerated capital allowances for BEVs and a low rate of annual motor tax. From a business perspective, among some of the supports available is a grant for taxi drivers to make the switch to an electric vehicle (EV); and a grant for HDVs to bridge the gap between a zero-emission vehicle and a fossil fuel vehicle.

The new Programme for Government 2025 commits to developing a new National EV Infrastructure Strategy which ensures EV charging is ahead of demand and in line with EU mandated obligations, including neighbourhood charging for those who do not have access to home charging and charging across the main transport arteries. It commits to substantially increasing the number of publicly available EV charging points, ensuring better coverage nationwide, and reducing the average distance between EV recharging points. It also commits to making EVs more affordable by examining the current EV grant system and the introduction of additional incentives and reviewing customs duties on second-hand EV imports.

45 https://www.gov.ie/en/department-of-enterprise-tourism-and-employment/publications/regional-aid-map-for-ireland/

In summary

As acknowledged in the NDP, there is a need for record levels of capital investment in public transport infrastructure to be complemented by increased and expanded public transport services as supported under the Public Service Obligation (PSO) subsidy. Continued funding will need to be secured yearly from the budget process to meet existing levels of PSO services between 2026 to 2030. To maximise the benefit of capital investment, and to ensure Just Transition principles are upheld, the Department of Transport will continue its close engagement with the Department of Public Expenditure Infrastructure, Public Service Reform and Digitalisation to align current requirements for services as well as rollout of demand management measures with the delivery of major infrastructure projects.

Without the large-scale investment envisaged in the NDP, the transport sector would be at critical risk of significantly underachieving in its effort to cut transport emissions in half by 2030. Demand management measures can help to minimise that risk, by complementing the investment envisaged in the coming years - through greater use of the infrastructure and services provided and greater efficiency through the consequential reduction in overall car journeys. For this reason, it is anticipated that the deployment of many demand management measures will be rolled out in tandem with, or subsequent to, the planned investment to 2030 and beyond, ensuring that ‘no one is left behind’ in our transition to a better and more efficient transport system.

Increasing the supply of purpose-built student accommodation and digs accommodation in the vicinity of HEIs will reduce travel demand.
